Asker Healthcare · ASKER-SE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 22 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote in the layers behind the Asker Healthcare memo

These are the Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions it worked through before the Stock-specific Investment Case A&J Memo on Asker Healthcare was written: 53,486 words, against 13,823 words in the memo Analyst's draft.

Word for word. Every answer below is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written. Two things are changed, both layout only: each group's judgement sits above the questions it sums up, because it was written last; and each score is shown as a pill. A script checked that every word of every answer is on this page, in order.

The Investment Analyst's terms: it calls Layer 1 "the first set", Layer 2 "the second set", and Layer 3 and Layer 4 "the third and fourth sets". "The Consumer" is the Investment Analyst who works the next layer. "Our" means our own forecasts, as opposed to the sell-side's. Acronyms are explained under "Terms used" at the end, and on a computer by hovering over a dotted term.

On request: a clarity-edited version, with a summary of the most important messages and a comparison with the memo, as made for Xvivo Perfusion on 30 October 2026.

How to read the scores

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning below its title.

The letter beside each score is the system's own grade of that score (A from 90, B from 75, C from 50, D from 25, F below 25). ❌ marks a D, ❌❌ an F.

Layer 1

Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

82 questions in 10 groups · 27,690 words · written 23 October 2026, 00:18 to 00:41 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Asker Healthcare (ASKER-SE): First set of questions, the foundational components of an investment case

Written 2026-10-22 for the Investment Analyst answering the second set. Money is in SEK unless stated. "Adjusted EBITA" is the company's own measure; "brokers' basis" and "FactSet" figures are from the financials block of 2026-10-22. Last reported period: Q2 2026 (H1 report of 21 July 2026); Q3 2026 results are due 4 November 2026.

A note on how to read this set

Three facts shape every answer below, so I state them once. First, Asker is a decentralised European distributor of medical supplies, devices and equipment (not pharmaceuticals) with roughly 1% of a SEK 1,355bn European medtech market and about 3% of the value-added distributor segment; FY2025 sales were SEK 16,787m, adjusted EBITA SEK 1,594m (9.5%), and H1 2026 sales grew 15% (5% organic, 12% acquired, -2% currency) with adjusted EBITA up 23% to a 9.9% margin. Second, the equity has been through a sharp de-rating: the shares (SEK 70.8 on 21 October 2026) sit at the March 2025 IPO price of SEK 70, the 24-month forward P/E of 19.5x is at the 9th percentile of its own five-year range (median 24.0x), and the forward EV/EBITA has fallen from about 21x to about 16x. Third, the sole broker research visible in the files (DNB Carnegie) moved from Buy with a SEK 105 target (5 August 2026) to Sell at SEK 60 (10 September 2026) on an earnings-quality review, then to Hold at SEK 66.5 (1 October 2026) after the shares underperformed AddLife by about 20%. The dispute that triggered this, whether reported adjusted EBITA overstates cash earnings by roughly 20 to 25%, is the single most important modelling question the Consumer faces, and I give my own position on it in C-54 and the Economic strength group judgement: the truth sits between the company's figure and the broker's, with a cash earnings base roughly 10 to 15% below adjusted EBITA while the acquisition programme runs at its present pace.

External change forces

C5010 questions
C-0162C-0242C-0348C-0454C-0552C-0658C-0738C-0860C-0952C-1050

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The external forces are, on balance, neutral for the investment case with a defensive tilt: demand is structural, visible and growing at a low single-digit rate with a one to two point premium available to a scaled value-added distributor, and none of the forces is turning for the worse at the demand level. The forces that are turning for the worse are financial and competitive: a rates regime that punishes levered small caps, and a target market that other consolidators have re-entered. The Consumer should therefore build the revenue line with confidence (4 to 5% organic, no price above inflation, currency a mild headwind from 2027) and build everything below EBITA with caution. The most useful external judgement is that there is no external excuse for a demand shortfall over the next 18 months; if Asker's numbers disappoint, the cause will be internal (cash conversion, leverage, accounting) rather than the market.

Horizon: the last six months and the next six months, compared with the last 12 to 18 months.

C-01

End-market demand: structural or cyclical change, and visibility

C62

Question asked To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

Judgement: end-market demand is structurally stable and growing at a low rate, is not changing materially in either direction, and carries high visibility; the only cyclical element in Asker's own demand was the 2024 defence and civil-preparedness orders in Business Area North (about SEK 350m), which have now fully washed out of the comparison base. Nothing in the last six months changes the structural picture relative to the last 18 months.

Reasoning. The underlying European medtech market was about SEK 1,355bn in 2024 and is forecast to grow 3 to 4% a year to 2028 (company and Carnegie; MedTech Europe recorded only 1.8% in 2025, which I treat as a one-year trough rather than a new trend). Demand is driven by ageing (the European over-80 population grows about 50% by 2030), chronic disease, the shift from hospital to homecare and direct-to-patient channels, and provider cost pressure that favours bundled "system sales" and fourth-party logistics. Two shifts matter for the model: (a) the channel shift to homecare and direct-to-patient is structural and Asker's Dutch direct-to-patient business has scaled from 80,000 patients in 2015 to over 570,000, so the mix is moving toward recurring consumables with predictable volume; (b) within hospitals, equipment and capital projects can be delayed when budgets tighten, which is a timing risk, not a demand loss. The 2025 organic slowdown to 3% (from an average near 6% in 2022 to 2025) was almost entirely the defence-order comparison (a 10 to 11 percentage point headwind in North in Q4 2025), and organic growth recovered to 5% in both Q1 and Q2 2026 once those comparisons lapsed; North itself grew 10% organically in Q2 2026. That tells the Consumer the 2025 print understated the run-rate and the H1 2026 print is the cleaner base.

Visibility: contracts are typically 2+2 years in Nordic public tenders, 3 years in direct business-to-business, and 1 year in German and Dutch direct-to-patient with automatic renewal; the 88% re-win rate in Sweden, Norway and Finland over 2019 to 2024 means the base is largely known 12 to 24 months out. Two-year-plus visibility exists for the structural trend; it does not exist for lumpy public preparedness orders, which could return (Nordic defence budgets are rising) but should be modelled as upside, not base case.

Skew over the next 12 months: narrow cone, slightly positive. Organic sales growth of 4 to 5% is the central path; the downside tail is a return to the 3 to 4% market rate if public budgets squeeze tenders; the upside tail is a renewed preparedness cycle. Peers confirm the picture: AddLife adjusted organic growth 4% in Q3 2026 and Uniphar Medtech 9% organic gross profit growth in H1 2026.

C-02

Competitive intensity: extent and direction of change

❌ D42

Question asked To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

Judgement: competitive intensity is rising, modestly, in two places that matter to the model and is unchanged in the third. It is rising in the market for acquisition targets (AddLife has restarted its programme with six deals in twelve months after a two-year pause, Uniphar is explicitly chasing European medtech deals, and private-equity platforms are present); it is rising in customer procurement through consolidation of buyers (cross-border Nordic procurement pacts, Sykehusinnkjøp in Norway, German hospital concentration); and it is unchanged in day-to-day distribution, where the 37,000-company fragmented landscape and local tender mechanics leave Asker's win rate intact.

Reasoning. On targets: AddLife's Unicam and Lehnen deals were done at under 7x EV/EBITDA and AddLife's CEO said recent deals were "not affected by significant competition", so headline multiples have not yet moved; but AddLife needs six to eight deals a year, Uniphar targets EUR 200m EBITDA by 2028 partly through acquisitions, and an expert formerly at Asker warns the pool of attractive, professionalised targets above about EUR 50m revenue thins out within two years. The more likely form of rising intensity is therefore not higher multiples but a drift toward smaller, less professional targets that cost more to integrate per krona of EBITA. On customers: 70% of European medical-product purchasing is public procurement and 55% of tender processes focus mainly on price; a Henry Schein expert describes post-pandemic networks "actively pitching distributors against each other". That is a persistent margin ceiling, not a new deterioration. On distribution rivalry: AddLife prices above competitors on service in several tenders, distributors absorb typical annual price declines of 2 to 3% offset by mix (AddLife's own disclosure), and there is no evidence of a volume-first player in the Nordics or Benelux.

What this means for the forecasts: acquired growth is the line most exposed, and I would hold it below the company's 10 to 12% EBITA contribution target (see C-18); gross margin should not be assumed to expand from price. Competitive intensity is therefore a slow headwind to the mid-term M&A engine rather than a near-term earnings threat.

C-03

Disruption forces: strengthening or disproved, and their direction

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

Judgement: the one disruption force with a live precedent, original equipment manufacturers (OEMs) selling direct and bypassing distributors, is slowly strengthening in knowledge-intensive device categories and has been demonstrated at a peer (AddLife lost its UK endoscopy business to its OEM in 2025 and sold it for SEK 158m). It acts against Asker in the roughly 30% of medtech activity that is devices and equipment and barely at all in recurring consumables and homecare. Digital procurement and artificial intelligence are gradual, not abrupt, and the net effect of regulation-driven complexity (MDR, IVDR) is to push OEMs toward outsourcing to scaled distributors, which partly offsets the bypass threat.

Reasoning. The expert evidence is specific: OEMs tend to pull a distributor contract once the local subsidiary's sales in that line exceed SEK 20 to 30m, so the bypass risk scales with Asker's own success in a category. Against this: Asker's CEO reports OEMs initiating partnerships to use Asker's regional infrastructure under MDR; global OEMs lack the local warehouses for frequent small-quantity outpatient and homecare deliveries; and the Dutch direct-to-patient model is one most OEMs cannot replicate. Downstream, buyer insourcing (Region Västmanland, Serviso) is real but marginal. Digital marketplaces make price comparison easier in commoditised supplies, which is a gross-margin headwind over years, not quarters. Asker's own digital assets (OneWound, Health Net Connections, Ambercell's supply-chain software) are option value with no disclosed revenue; I would not put them in the model.

Direction and magnitude for the forecasts: a slow negative on device-category organic growth and mix, roughly 0.5 to 1 percentage point of organic growth at risk over the mid term if several OEM contracts were lost together; the recommended monitoring point is any local subsidiary whose single-OEM line passes the SEK 20 to 30m threshold.

C-04

Political or regulatory change and its bearing on the company

C54

Question asked To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

Judgement: regulatory change is under way at a moderate pace and is net positive for Asker's competitive position and net negative for its cost base and deal execution. The Medical Device Regulation and In Vitro Diagnostic Regulation raise compliance costs that small distributors and some small manufacturers cannot carry (a Henry Schein expert reports small manufacturers exiting Europe), which feeds Asker's acquisition pipeline and tender position; against this, the Danish Competition Authority's year-long review forced Asker to abandon the Kirstine Hardam acquisition (about SEK 200m of sales, roughly 1% of 2027 forecast sales), German reimbursement reform caps hospital spend, and NIS2 and GDPR add cybersecurity and data costs as Asker expands digital and direct-to-patient services.

Reasoning. The MDR effect is the structural one and it works for Asker in two ways: it favours scale in tenders (compliance teams, documentation, traceability) and it drives entrepreneurs to sell. Transition deadlines run to 2027 to 2029 and the European Commission has proposed simplification, so the barrier may soften at the margin, which would be a small negative. The competition-authority precedent is more important for the model than it looks: Asker's acquisitions are local-market bolt-ons, which is exactly where national competition reviews bite once a buyer already holds a strong local share, and Denmark is a market where Asker is already present. I would assume a modest, recurring deal-attrition rate rather than a one-off. ESG criteria are increasingly written into tenders; Asker's MSCI AAA rating and supplier code programme are a mild advantage here.

Over the next six months nothing regulatory is dated; the two watch-points are any further competition-authority intervention in the Nordics and the European Commission's MDR simplification package.

C-05

Input costs, availability and supplier concentration

C52

Question asked How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

Judgement: the net effect is a mild headwind to working capital and a neutral effect on margin. Supply is diversified (more than 2,000 suppliers, none above 7% of cost of goods sold, the top 25 about 48%; 80% sourced in Europe and 20% mainly in Asia), cost inflation has been passed through ("we are floating as a cork", the CEO; index-linked public contracts; the CFO ruled out a supply-chain margin squeeze in Q1 2026), and the company's gross margin rose about 110 basis points year on year to about 42% in Q2 2026. The headwind is that supply-security buffering, acquisitions and the Gothenburg move have taken inventory days from 59 to 65 in 2022 to 75 in 2025 and 82 in Q2 2026, and freight and geopolitical risk (Red Sea, tariffs) sit on the 20% Asian share and on components inside European products.

Reasoning. Deutsche Bank's analysis of healthcare distribution cost bases finds very low direct exposure to petrochemical inflation, with cost pressure flowing through labour and facilities instead, and that matches Asker's experience: the margin risk is wages and warehouses, not products. Private label (10 to 15% of sales; about 14% in 2023) gives purchasing leverage and gross-margin uplift, and gloves, the largest own-brand category, carry the main sourcing risk (Asian factories, forced-labour audit risk, nearly 50% of Scope 3 emissions). Supplier concentration is falling as acquisitions add suppliers; that is good for resilience and slightly bad for procurement scale, and an expert reports that some acquired German businesses already bought cheaper than the group, so purchasing synergies are smaller than a roll-up narrative implies.

For the model: hold gross margin flat to slightly up on mix, not on price; model inventory days falling back toward 75 only after the Gothenburg transition completes in 2027, which makes working capital a 2026 cash drag and a 2027 to 2028 release.

C-06

Where the company's own revenue and demand cycle sits, and whether it is turning

C58

Question asked Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

Judgement: Asker's own cycle troughed in Q4 2025 (organic sales -1%) and turned up in H1 2026 (organic sales +5% in both quarters, organic adjusted EBITA +7 to 8%), which places the company in the early-to-mid phase of a recovery; the next six months will show whether the 5% rate holds against progressively tougher comparisons (H2 2025 organic was about 5% in Q3 and -1% in Q4, so Q3 2026 faces a normal base and Q4 2026 an easy one) while reported EBITA absorbs Gothenburg start-up costs.

Reasoning. The shape of the last 18 months is a comparison-driven dip rather than a demand cycle: 6% organic in Q1 2025, 3% in Q2, 5% in Q3, -1% in Q4, 5% in Q1 2026, 5% in Q2 2026. The regional split confirms the turn: North normalised to 10% organic sales growth in Q2 2026 after the defence comparison lapsed, West delivered 15% organic growth in Q1 2026 on homecare scale, and Central 13% organic in Q1 2026 while deliberately shedding low-margin products. Acquired growth of 12 to 13% adds to this, and the acquisition pace in 2026 (Van Heek, GHC/MPF, RMS, Murray Surgical, Ambercell, Labo Clinic, Med Kjemi; more than half the annual target met by July) means reported sales growth stays mid-teens into early 2027 before the annualisation effect fades.

Where it is turning next: upward on organic sales into Q4 2026 on the easy comparison; sideways on North's margin in Q3 and Q4 2026 because of double-manning and double inventory at Gothenburg (low tens of millions of SEK); upward on group margin in 2027 as the warehouse delivers SEK 50 to 60m of annual savings from H2 2027. The thing the Consumer should not do is extrapolate Q1 2026's 13% and 15% regional organic rates; those were comparison-flattered and management itself frames organic EBITA growth at 5 to 8%.

C-07

Macro force: at which level does it bite hardest

❌ D38

Question asked To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

Judgement: the macro force now acting (a rates-led late cycle with the US 10-year near 5.1%, the Riksbank likely to hike in November 2026, European consumer weakening and the market "refusing re-ratings") bites hardest at the company level, through the cost and availability of debt for a serial acquirer running at 2.37x reported leverage (2.56x on FactSet's lease-inclusive measure, 3.1 to 3.4x on the broker's all-in estimate), and through the discount rate applied to a small-cap compounder; it bites only lightly at the sector level (public healthcare demand is defensive) and moderately at the industry level (healthcare is being bought as a destination for money leaving technology, which is a flow, not a fundamental).

Reasoning. The company's disclosed sensitivities make the point: one percentage point on interest rates moves net financial items by about SEK 35m (about SEK 0.07 of EPS, or about 2.5% of 2026 consensus EPS), and a 1% move in the krona moves earnings by about SEK 3.7m. Rates matter more than currency. Sweden is the most rate-sensitive developed economy because mortgages reset quickly, but Asker's revenue is 78% outside Sweden and 69% in euros, so the Swedish macro reading applies to the listing (and to the krona-denominated share of debt) rather than to the business. The weekly Fitness for Fighting digest rates Fragility at Double Underweight and says 28 of 30 recent memos failed on size, leverage or liquidity rather than on demand: Asker, a SEK 27bn market cap with USD 6 to 8m of daily traded value and leverage at its own ceiling, is exactly the profile the regime punishes.

Transmission to the model: (a) assume no help from lower rates in the next 12 months; model net financial items flat to slightly up; (b) a weaker krona in H2 2026 is a small reported tailwind (DNB Carnegie's October upgrade of 2027 to 2028 EPS by 1% was entirely currency); (c) the equity's fair multiple is held down by the regime regardless of delivery, which matters for the fourth set more than the second.

C-08

A key industry or sector driver accelerating the company's demand

C60

Question asked To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

Judgement: there is a genuine but slow-acting driver: the outsourcing of hospital logistics and procurement to value-added distributors, whose share of the European market rose from 35% in 2019 to 40% in 2023 (versus about 60% in the United States), combined with the homecare and direct-to-patient channel shift and MDR-driven supplier outsourcing. It accelerates Asker's demand by roughly 1 to 2 percentage points above the 3 to 4% market rate, which is what the 5% organic rate in H1 2026 and the 6% average of 2022 to 2025 show. It is not accelerating further in the last six months; it is steady.

Reasoning. The mechanism is visible in the Nordics: system sales (tens of thousands of products bundled with logistics and training) rose from 65% of Swedish and Norwegian sales in 2019 to 79% in 2024, which both raises share of wallet and locks customers in. Mid-sized manufacturers are withdrawing unprofitable local sales forces in parts of Europe and handing the function to distributors (AddLife's disclosure). Provider staff shortages make bundled supply and automated monitoring more valuable. The counterweight is that the same budget pressure that drives outsourcing drives price-led tenders. Peer data corroborate the driver's size: Uniphar Medtech grew gross profit 9% organically in H1 2026 and AddLife's Medtech 5% in Q3 2026, bracketing Asker's 5% sales growth.

For the model: a durable 1 to 2 point premium to market growth is defensible; a 3 point premium is not without evidence of share gains by country, which Asker does not disclose.

C-09

Other material external dynamics

C52

Question asked What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

Judgement: three other dynamics are material. (1) Currency translation: the euro is about 69% of revenue, translation cut reported sales by 3% in 2024, 3% in 2025, 4% in Q1 2026 and 2% in H1 2026, and the krona weakened again from September 2026; translation is unhedged, transaction exposure is hedged 50 to 70% in year one without hedge accounting, so reported growth and net financial items both swing with EUR/SEK; the consensus house view (Nordea, Morgan Stanley) is for the krona to strengthen toward EUR/SEK 10.40 to 10.60 by 2027, which would reinstate a 1 to 3% reported headwind. (2) Nordic defence and preparedness spending: the SEK 350m of 2024 orders show the scale of what a public preparedness cycle can add; with Nordic defence budgets rising, a renewed order cycle is a plausible 2027 upside that nobody models. (3) The Dutch insurer channel: five insurers (Zilveren Kruis, CZ, VGZ, Menzis, DSW) control contracting for Asker's largest West market, where sales are relationship-based rather than tender-based; a change in insurer preference is a concentrated revenue risk that the group's low customer concentration (largest contract 3% of sales, top ten 17%) understates.

Reasoning on weight: (1) is the most certain and smallest per unit; (2) is uncertain and lumpy; (3) is low probability and high impact. I would carry (1) in the base case as a 1 to 2% reported headwind from 2027, (2) as unmodelled upside, and (3) as a named downside scenario.

C-10

Thematic drivers in turn, then the overall external picture

C50

Question asked Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

Judgement, driver by driver. Value chain: tailwind from OEM outsourcing and distributor share gains (moderate, structural); headwind from OEM bypass in devices and from buyer consolidation (moderate, slow); net mildly positive. Industry (healthcare distribution and retail): tailwind from ageing and homecare (moderate, structural, high visibility); headwind from price-led public procurement and reimbursement caps outside the Nordics (moderate, persistent); net neutral to mildly positive. Sector-level finance: headwind from the rates regime and the market's refusal to pay for leverage (strong for the next six to twelve months); competition for targets (mild, rising). Regulation: MDR a tailwind to position, a headwind to cost; competition authorities a new deal-execution friction. Currency: a headwind that has just paused.

Overall external change picture: the operating environment is steady and defensive and has not changed materially in the last six months relative to the last 18, while the financial environment has turned against the company's model. The external tailwinds (3 to 4% market growth plus 1 to 2 points of channel shift and consolidation) are durable but small; the external headwinds concentrate on the balance sheet and the multiple rather than on demand. For the Consumer this means the external world justifies organic sales growth of 4 to 5% and an unchanged gross margin, and does not justify assuming cheaper debt or a higher multiple. The cone of outcomes for revenue is narrow; the cone for EPS is wide because of what sits below EBITA.

Asker Healthcare (ASKER-SE): First set of questions (continued)

Internal change forces

❌ D4713 questions
C-1155C-1252C-1345C-1445C-1542C-1655C-1748C-1860C-1940C-2055C-8632C-8750C-2148

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The internal change forces are slightly negative for the investment case over the next six months and neutral over 18 months. The positives (acquisition execution, logistics consolidation, mix improvement, strengthened board) are real and dated; the negatives (earnings quality, working capital, leverage, productivity) are present now and are the reason the equity trades at the IPO price. The decisive internal variable is cash conversion, which determines whether the second engine (acquisitions, more than half of EBITA growth) can keep running at 10 to 12% or must slow to 7%. The Consumer's model should treat statutory free cash flow after earn-outs, not adjusted EBITA, as the governing line, and should assume the company spends 2026 to 2027 proving it can deleverage while still acquiring, with adjusted EBITA growth in the 12 to 17% range rather than the 21% consensus has for 2026 extending into 2027.

Horizon: the last six months and the next six months, compared with the last 12 to 18 months.

C-11

Changes in chief executive, key leaders, board and key actors, and what they signal

C55

Question asked To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

Judgement: the executive core is unchanged (CEO Johan Falk since 2012, CFO Thomas Moss since 2019, COO Peter Gustafsson since 2013), the board has been refreshed at the top (Mikael Stöhr, former CEO of Coor and Consolis, elected Chair in May 2026 replacing Håkan Björklund; Joachim Zetterlund replacing Martin Lagerblad; Karl Petersson of Interogo replacing Anders Nyman in December 2025), and two second-tier functions turned over (Lovisa Hedin promoted to Head of M&A in April 2026 as Ola Nordh left; Kerstin Mjömark took HR and ESG as Sanna Norman left). The principal owner, Interogo, moved its 40.2% stake into its long-term equity strategy and issued 291,970 call options to the new Chair at SEK 94.90 (a 20% premium at the time). The signal is continuity of strategy with stronger, more owner-aligned oversight; it is not a signal of a reset.

Reasoning. Stöhr is an experienced listed-company operator and serial-acquirer chairman, and the call-option grant ties him to a share price about 34% above today's; that is a positive governance change at the moment the company most needs an independent challenge on accounting and leverage. The M&A succession is internal (Hedin has been at Asker since 2022 and participated in over 40 deals), which preserves the pipeline but does not add new capability. The departures of Nordh and Norman are unexplained, and I treat them as watchful rather than negative. One actual negative signal: the CFO sold 225,000 shares at SEK 78 on 29 May 2026, outside a trading venue, three months before the forensic downgrade; he still holds about 989,000 shares, but the sale sits badly against his role as the architect of the leverage definition under dispute. No activist is present and Interogo's 40.2% blocks one.

Implication for the next six months: no leadership-driven change in guidance or strategy should be expected; the new Chair's first visible test is how the company responds to the earnings-quality critique in the FY2026 annual report.

C-12

Changes in strategy or strategic priorities, and how well articulated and resourced

C52

Question asked To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

Judgement: the strategy is unchanged (the "twin engine" of above-market organic growth and 10 to 20 small acquisitions a year at 6 to 8x EBITA, run through decentralised companies with central Centres of Excellence), but its emphasis has shifted over the last 12 months from expansion toward "gradual realisation of benefits from our enhanced capacity and market presence, and maintaining a focus on cash flow and capital efficiency" (CEO, Q2 2026 report). The articulation is clear; the resourcing is constrained, because the acquisition engine is funded from operating cash flow and debt and both are now tight.

Reasoning. The shift in emphasis is the right one and is the main internal change force: it means logistics consolidation (Gothenburg; the Dutch warehouse completed in late 2025), pruning of low-margin product lines in Central, a push toward higher-value devices (about 70% of medtech activity by September 2026) and private label, and a stated willingness to walk away from deals (Kirstine Hardam). None of these is new in kind; the sum is a company moving from land-grab to digestion. What is not articulated is any quantified bridge from the Centres of Excellence (purchasing, logistics, IT, own brands) to earnings, and an expert reports purchasing synergies proved elusive in Germany. Resourcing: the company has SEK 569m of undrawn facilities, reported leverage 0.13 turns below its own ceiling, and roughly SEK 150m of 2027 earn-outs ahead; the strategy can be executed at the current pace only if cash conversion improves. That is the dependency the Consumer should carry through the model.

C-13

Financial guidance raised, or mid-term targets set or raised

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

Judgement: no. The targets (adjusted EBITA growth above 15% a year, adjusted EBITA margin above 10% over the medium term, net debt to adjusted EBITDA below 2.5x, return on net working capital above 50%, dividend above 30% of profit) are unchanged since the IPO prospectus, were not raised when rolling 12-month adjusted EBITA growth reached 18% (Q1 2026) and 21% (Q2 2026), and there has been no capital markets day or new ambition. The leverage target was tightened from a 2.0 to 3.0x range in 2023 to a 2.5x ceiling in 2024, which is the only direction of travel in the framework, and it is restrictive rather than ambitious.

Reasoning. For a compounder that has grown adjusted EBITA at a 38% compound rate from 2019 to 2024 and 17% in 2025, a 15% floor is deliberately undemanding; management treats it as a floor and the market treats it as a forecast. The absence of a raise is partly prudence (the margin target is not yet met on a rolling basis: 9.8%) and partly that the company does not guide at all below EBITA. The Consumer should note that the LTIP 2026 vesting hurdle is a 12.5% minimum EBITA compound rate over three years, which is below the public target; that is a quiet signal that management's internal floor is lower than the stated one.

C-14

Standards improving in execution, operations, governance or cost discipline

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Judgement: mixed, and the mix is the problem. Operational standards are improving on the measures management chooses (return on net working capital 69% against a 50% target; margin pruning in Central lifting its margin from 7.8% to 9.2% in H1 2026; Gothenburg on schedule; the Asker Management Standard applied to acquisitions within 12 months; a third-party review of financial-reporting controls in 2025). Governance and accounting standards are not improving and on the evidence have loosened: the obsolescence reserve fell from 14.9% of gross inventory in 2021 to 6.5% in 2025, credit-loss provisions fell while overdue receivables rose 170% in 2025, software useful lives were extended from 7.5 to 10 years in 2025, the board again declined to establish an internal audit function, and reported leverage excludes SEK 645m to 1,050m of contingent consideration that the closest peer (AddLife) includes. Productivity is falling: revenue excluding third-party logistics per full-time employee dropped 20% from SEK 5.45m in 2023 to SEK 4.52m in 2025.

Reasoning. The operational improvements are real and show in the margin; the accounting choices are each individually defensible and collectively flatter the margin by perhaps 100 to 200 basis points (the broker's full reconstruction says 220 basis points, from 9.5% to 7.3%, which I think overstates it because contingent-consideration revaluations are genuinely non-cash and IPO costs genuinely non-recurring). The direction of the actions over the next six months is what matters: the company has been publicly challenged and the new Chair sits on top of an Audit Committee that now includes the principal owner's representative. If the FY2026 report rebuilds provisions and discloses intangible capitalisation clearly, standards will have improved at a visible one-off cost to EBITA; if it does not, the discount persists.

C-15

Reducing the perimeter: divestments, simplification, exits

❌ D42

Question asked To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

Judgement: minimal. Asker has made no divestments; the only perimeter actions are the systematic phasing out of low-margin product categories in Central (which cut Central's sales growth below its acquired growth while lifting its adjusted EBITA 47% in Q1 2026), the consolidation of warehouses (Netherlands completed, Gothenburg under way), and the termination of the Kirstine Hardam acquisition in June 2026. The group is still adding companies (over 50, in 19 countries) faster than it integrates them.

Reasoning. For a serial acquirer this is normal, and the product-pruning in Central is a genuinely positive margin action that the model should carry (Central's margin rose from 8.2% to 9.6% in Q2 2026). But the absence of any portfolio pruning after more than 60 acquisitions since 2019 is itself a signal: AddLife's recovery from 2023 was built on closing Camanio, restructuring AddVision and divesting UK endoscopy, and Asker has not yet had to show it can cut. The Consumer should not model perimeter reduction; the one lever that could appear is a sale of a sub-scale country position if leverage needs relief, which would be a negative signal if it came.

C-16

Entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly

C55

Question asked To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

Judgement: Asker is entering new markets steadily and sensibly, by bolt-on rather than greenfield: Poland deepened (Labo Clinic, about SEK 100m of sales, specialised neurosurgical and operating-room products), the Baltics (Ambercell Solutions, about SEK 250m, a procedure-pack supply-chain platform), Ireland (Murray Surgical, SEK 73m, folded into HSL), Belgium (RMS Medical Devices, SEK 170m, endoscopy and digital health), Germany homecare (GHC/MPF, SEK 300m, private-label incontinence and urology), Norway laboratory distribution (Med Kjemi, SEK 50m), and the Netherlands (Van Heek Medical, SEK 350m, diabetes and wound care). The pattern is sound: each deal attaches to an existing platform, adds a product category that can be cross-sold, and is small enough to be digested; management has named France, Spain and Italy as white spots.

Reasoning on sensibility. The risks are margin dilution and integration load rather than strategic error. Germany is structurally lower margin (purchasing organisations dominate; an expert says German targets "do not have very high margins"), the Baltics are lower-price markets, and these moves lower the group's weight in high-margin North (31% of sales now, 39% in 2023; North's margin is 13.5% against 9.6% in West and 9.2% in Central). The arithmetic the Consumer needs: every point of mix shift from North to West or Central costs about 4 basis points of group margin, so the geographic expansion is a persistent small drag that acquired-company margins above 10% and the Central pruning must offset. So far they have: group margin rose 60 basis points in H1 2026 despite the shift.

C-17

Raising prices in real terms: real, nominal, or latent

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

Judgement: Asker's price rises are nominal and defensive, not real. The evidence of application is strong for pass-through: management passed cost inflation through in 2022 to 2024 ("super on top of inflation", an expert; "floating as a cork", the CEO), many contracts are index-linked, and the CFO ruled out a margin squeeze in Q1 2026. The evidence for pricing above inflation is absent: the company gives no price-volume bridge, management attributed the Q2 2026 gross-margin gain mainly to acquired-portfolio mix and cost-of-goods work, 55% of public tenders are price-led, distributors in this sector absorb annual price declines of 2 to 3% on like-for-like items (AddLife), and a B. Braun expert expects European consumable prices to stay flat or under pressure for five years. Management has not begun to talk about real pricing; it is not even latent in the commentary.

What this means: the gross margin should be modelled as mix-driven (private label at 10 to 15% of sales rising, devices rising, pruning), with price holding real margin flat. The question for the second set is whether a scaled distributor with 79% system-sales penetration in the Nordics can, over three years, extract 50 to 100 basis points of price on renewal; I have requested research on it (T-P2) because the files cannot answer it.

C-18

Stepping up mergers and acquisitions: strategic sense, discipline, integration

C60

Question asked To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

Judgement: the pace has stepped up (14 signed and 12 completed in 2025; seven to eight more completed or announced by September 2026, with more than half of the annual 10 to 12% EBITA-contribution target met by July), the strategy is coherent (bolt-ons onto platforms, product-category and geographic adjacency), the price discipline is demonstrated (6 to 8x EBITA for standard deals, 1 to 2 turns more for platforms; Carnegie's three-year return on incremental capital of about 23%; H1 2026 acquisitions contributed SEK 273m of sales and SEK 47m of EBITA from their dates, a 17% margin that the CFO himself calls "moderately rather than strongly" accretive once full-period costs land), and integration is deliberately light (85% freedom, 15% control; financial reporting and the code of conduct immediately, the full management standard within 12 months). The weakness is in the funding and the accounting rather than the deals: acquisitions are consuming cash faster than operations generate it, items affecting comparability for acquisition and integration ran at SEK 90m in 2025 and SEK 141m over 2024 to Q2 2026 (about SEK 15m a quarter the broker thinks is recurring), and contingent consideration of SEK 645m to 1,050m sits outside reported net debt.

Reasoning on what to model. Management's 10 to 12% EBITA contribution requires buying roughly SEK 180 to 220m of EBITA a year at 7x, about SEK 1.3 to 1.5bn of consideration, against operating cash flow after capex, interest, tax, earn-outs and dividend of perhaps SEK 600 to 800m. The gap is debt, and reported leverage is at 2.37x against a 2.5x ceiling. The pace is therefore not sustainable at the current cash conversion without either leverage rising, equity issuance (management says acquisitions are funded by cash and debt) or a slower pace. DNB Carnegie models 7% acquired EBITA growth from 2027; I would model 7 to 9% for 2027 to 2028, below the target, rising only if cash conversion recovers toward 80% of EBITA. On discipline, one terminated deal is evidence, not proof; the test is whether multiples stay at 6 to 8x as the target pool shifts to smaller companies.

C-19

Using the balance sheet more, or stepping up shareholder returns

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

Judgement: the balance sheet is already fully used and the direction over the next six to twelve months is toward less use, not more. Net debt rose from SEK 2,886m (June 2025) to SEK 4,511m (June 2026), reported leverage from 1.84x to 2.37x, and Q2 2026 absorbed SEK 463m of earn-outs, a SEK 149m first dividend (SEK 0.39 a share, a 30% payout), and SEK 354m of acquisition spending. There is no buyback and none is mooted; the dividend policy (above 30%) is in place and consensus has the dividend rising to SEK 0.73 in 2026 and 0.86 in 2027, which at a 1% yield is immaterial to the case. Management's commentary has shifted toward cash flow and capital efficiency, and DNB Carnegie's base case needs reported leverage to fall to about 1.9x by 2027 and 1.5x by 2028.

Reasoning. The relevant internal change is the opposite of the question's premise: Asker will have to demonstrate deleveraging while acquiring, which the AddLife precedent shows is rewarded (AddLife went from 3.5x to 2.2x on internally generated cash and the multiple followed) and the Storskogen and Cary Group precedents show is punished when it fails. The year-end 2026 cash collection (Q4 is seasonally the strongest cash quarter for Nordic distributors; AddLife collects about 70% of its annual cash in Q4) is the first test. The Consumer should model leverage falling to about 2.1 to 2.2x by end-2026 and 1.8 to 2.0x by end-2027 only if free cash flow conversion reaches about 65 to 70% of adjusted EBITA; at the broker's 55% it stays above 2.3x and the acquisition pace must slow.

C-20

Other material positive corporate change forces present or plausible

C55

Question asked What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

Judgement: four, in order of weight. (1) The Gothenburg distribution centre (total about SEK 300m of capital, SEK 150m in 2026): fully operational in H1 2027 with SEK 50 to 60m of annual savings from H2 2027, about 3% of 2026 adjusted EBITA and about 30 basis points of group margin; dated, quantified, and not yet in the numbers. (2) The rolling 12-month adjusted EBITA margin crossing 10% (9.8% at Q2 2026), plausibly at Q4 2026 or Q1 2027, which would be the first stated target "met" and a narrative event. (3) Private-label expansion (about 2,000 own-brand articles; Evercare, Selefa, Embra; GHC/MPF's incontinence and urology brands distributable through other Asker companies): higher gross margin and better OEM bargaining; plausible, unquantified. (4) The Dutch direct-to-patient platform's continued compounding (570,000 patients) and its extension to Germany through GHC/MPF: structural, already in organic growth.

Reasoning on what is not on the list: digital and artificial-intelligence initiatives (OneWound, Ambercell's software, Health Net Connections) have no disclosed revenue and should be left out; a capital markets day is not announced; a return of Nordic preparedness orders is external (C-09). The positive internal forces are therefore modest, mostly dated to 2027, and worth perhaps 50 to 80 basis points of group margin by 2028 in combination.

C-86

Negative corporate change developments present or plausible

❌ D32

Question asked What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

Judgement: the negative developments are more numerous than the positive ones and three of them are already present. (1) The earnings-quality dispute: capitalised intangible investment of SEK 160 to 243m a year excluded from adjusted EBITA amortisation, provision releases, extended useful lives, and SEK 141m of acquisition and integration cost classified as non-recurring over ten quarters; this is a present development that has already cost the company its premium multiple. (2) Working-capital drift: inventory days at 82, overdue receivables up 170% in 2025, net working capital up to SEK 2,767m, and Q2 2026 statutory operating cash flow of only SEK 92m before earn-out add-backs. (3) Leverage at the ceiling with contingent consideration outside it. (4) Plausible: Gothenburg start-up costs running beyond H1 2027 (double manning, SEK 50 to 100m of extra inventory); a second terminated or repriced deal; founder departures after three-year earn-outs expire (several large 2023 deals reach that point in 2026 to 2027); rising staff turnover (total 17.3% in 2025, sick leave above the 4% target, four whistleblowing cases and one fraud incident in 2025); and a covenant disclosure gap (the company does not disclose covenant levels).

Reasoning on the one that matters most: the single mechanism that converts these into a lower share price is cash conversion, because it governs leverage, leverage governs the deal pace, and the deal pace governs more than half of EBITA growth. The Consumer should build the model so that cash conversion is an explicit input, not an output.

C-87

Controlling actors indicating scope for change in strategy or approach

C50

Question asked To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

Judgement: no indication of change. Interogo Long-Term Equity (40.2% of capital and votes, the Kamprad family's long-term vehicle) moved the stake from Nalka Invest to its long-term strategy in November 2025, placed its investment director on the board and Audit Committee, and issued call options to the new Chair; SEB Funds (6.0%) and Capital Group (5.1%) are passive institutions; no activist exists and the 40.2% block makes one unlikely. The nomination committee is dominated by the three largest owners. The signal is patient capital that supports the acquisition strategy and will not force deleveraging, a dividend step-up or a sale.

Reasoning on the two-sided implication: the absence of an activist removes a plausible positive catalyst (a demand for cleaner accounting and a cash-first capital allocation, which the AddLife precedent shows the market pays for) and removes a negative one (a forced slowdown). It also means that if the accounting dispute escalates, the pressure will come from the sell-side and from short interest, not from inside the register. Interogo's presence does lower the probability of a dilutive equity raise being imposed on minorities at a bad price, because the family has a long record of funding through the balance sheet, which is one reason the IPO set-off issue was at the SEK 70 IPO price rather than a discount.

C-21

Taken together: strength, breadth and resourcing of the positive corporate changes

❌ D48

Question asked Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

Judgement: the positive corporate changes are moderate in strength, narrow in breadth and adequately but not generously resourced. The strong ones (the acquisition engine at pace, the Gothenburg consolidation, product-mix pruning, the new Chair) are each genuine; the breadth is narrow because they all depend on the same scarce resource, cash, and the resourcing is constrained because leverage is at its ceiling and the accounting dispute has removed the option of cheap equity. Set against the last 12 to 18 months, the company has moved from a period of maximum expansion (IPO, 14 deals, 19 countries) to a period of digestion, and the internal change forces now read as "prove the model" rather than "accelerate the model".

The reconciling insight for the Consumer: Asker's internal change story is the AddLife story of 2023 to 2025 (deleverage, prove cash, then re-rate) starting from a better organic growth rate and a worse accounting reputation. If management chooses to rebuild provisions and expense more, 2026 to 2027 adjusted EBITA growth will undershoot the 15% framework while the quality of earnings improves; if it does not, the framework will be met and the discount will persist. I would model the former as the base case, because the new Chair and the public challenge make it the more likely path, and because it is the path that leads to a higher multiple.

Asker Healthcare (ASKER-SE): First set of questions (continued)
