Industry and Value Chain

C5416 questions
C-2455C-2545C-2658C-2760C-2855C-2958C-3155C-8445C-3262C-3352C-3452C-3555C-3648C-3754C-3850C-3960

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The industry structure is moderately supportive of a distribution compounder and does not support a product-owner's economics. Fragmentation, non-cyclical demand, regulation that favours scale, rational competitors and OEM outsourcing together justify 4 to 5% organic growth, a decade-long acquisition runway and a gross margin that holds or rises slowly on mix. Price-led public procurement, buyer consolidation, OEM bypass in devices and a structural margin ceiling around 10 to 12% for brand-neutral distributors set the limits. The precedents in the cohort are unusually clear about what the market pays for: cash conversion that funds acquisitions without leverage rising, organic growth above 3 to 4%, and clean accounting; Asker currently has the second, lacks the first, and is contesting the third. For the Consumer, the industry answers the top line and gross margin with confidence and leaves the operating margin, cash conversion and leverage to the company's own execution.

C-24

Rank by market share or size, and distance from the number one or two position

C55

Question asked Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

Judgement: at the European level Asker is a small player in a huge, fragmented market (about 1% of the SEK 1,355bn medtech market; about 2.7% of distributor sales on 2024 figures; about 3% of the value-added distributor segment) but is one of the two or three largest brand-neutral medical-supplies distributors on the continent by revenue (SEK 16.8bn against AddLife's SEK 10.4bn, Uniphar's EUR 3.1bn group revenue of which Medtech is a minority, and Mediq, private, in 14 countries). At the national level, where distribution competition actually happens, Asker holds the number one or two position in Sweden, Norway and Finland (OneMed, system-sales penetration 79%, 88% re-win rate) and in the Dutch direct-to-patient channel, and holds positions below 10% share in every other country.

Reasoning. The question's framing (distance from number one or two) resolves differently by geography: in the Nordics the distance is zero or negative, and the risk is defending rather than gaining; in West and Central Europe Asker is a consolidator building toward number one or two platform positions country by country (Benelux via Van Heek and RMS; Germany homecare via GHC/MPF; Poland via Labo Clinic). The structural ceiling is that 37,000 companies (33,000 small and medium-sized) populate the market and the top ten solution providers held only 21% in 2023 (up from 15% in 2019), so "number one" in European medical distribution is a position no one holds and the economics of being number one are local (warehouse density, tender relationships), not continental. For the model this means share gains are won country by country and are not visible in group disclosure; the Consumer should not expect a group-level share metric and should treat the Nordic re-win rate as the only hard evidence of position.

C-25

Market concentration and the steepness of the share distribution

❌ D45

Question asked How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

Judgement: the market is unconcentrated at the European level: the top ten value-added distributors held about 21% in 2023 (Carnegie elsewhere puts the top 11 at 15% of the total market), so the top five hold well under 30%, and the distribution is flat rather than steep because the largest players are national champions (Asker in the Nordics, Mediq in the Netherlands, Uniphar in Ireland, AddLife in Nordic diagnostics) rather than pan-European leaders. The numbers six to ten are not dwarfed by the top five; the long tail of family-owned distributors is where the volume sits.

Reasoning on what this means for returns. Low concentration is good for the acquisition runway and bad for pricing: there is no price leader, public buyers can run multi-supplier tenders, and the only route to pricing power is local density and service bundling. The evidence that scale is beginning to tell is the six-point rise in the top-ten share over four years and the rise in the distributor share of the market from 35% to 40%. The Consumer should assume consolidation continues at that pace (roughly 1.5 points of top-ten share a year), which is enough to sustain a 10 to 20 deal annual programme for Asker for a decade without the structure becoming concentrated enough to change pricing behaviour.

C-26

Stability of market shares, and the share taken by new entrants

C58

Question asked How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

Judgement: shares are stable at the national level over three to five years, and the share taken by genuinely new entrants over that period is small; the movement that has occurred is the share transfer from fragmented small distributors to scaled value-added platforms (the 35% to 40% shift), and from the pure-wholesale tier (Oriola, Tamro) to the value-added tier. Private-equity-backed platforms are the "new entrant" type, and they compete for targets rather than for customers.

Reasoning. The Nordic evidence is the best: an 88% average re-win rate and 35% of new bids won in 2024 means the installed share is sticky and the contestable share is being won at a rate above the installed share, which is mild share gain. Finland's pharmaceutical wholesale duopoly (Oriola 46%, Tamro the balance) shows how stable Nordic distribution shares become once scale is reached. The counter-evidence is the OEM-direct trend in devices (Tandem Diabetes, DexCom going direct outside the United States; AddLife's UK endoscopy loss), which is an entrant from upstream rather than a new distributor, and which can move share in specific product lines by double digits overnight. For the model: no share loss in the base case; a named scenario for a device-line OEM withdrawal.

C-27

Consolidation toward the largest, and toward the most advantaged; is Asker among them

C60

Question asked To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

Judgement: yes on both counts, slowly. Share consolidates toward scaled distributors because the structural drivers (MDR compliance cost, tender complexity, system-sales bundling, logistics automation, ESG criteria in tenders) are all fixed-cost-intensive and all favour the companies that can spread them; and it consolidates toward the most advantaged rather than merely the largest, where "advantaged" means local density plus a service and compliance layer, which is why Asker (in the Nordics) and AddLife (in diagnostics) gain while pure wholesalers (Oriola) stagnate. Asker is among the advantaged in its home markets and is buying its way toward advantage elsewhere.

Reasoning on the pace. The consolidation is measured in points per year, not in step changes, so it supports a 1 to 2 point organic premium to market growth (C-08) rather than a transformation. The force that could accelerate it is MDR enforcement (small manufacturers and distributors exiting), and the force that could reverse it is regulatory simplification or OEM disintermediation. The weakness in Asker's claim to be "most advantaged" is the falling revenue per employee (down 20% from 2023 to 2025), which says scale has not yet produced operating leverage at group level; the advantage is commercial and regulatory, not yet a cost advantage.

C-28

Competitors' attitude to competition: margin or volume first; benign or willing to suffer losses; do prices fall with volume

C55

Question asked What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement: the scaled competitors put margin and return on capital first and are benign toward each other; the fragmented tail competes on price in tenders but cannot sustain losses; and industry prices do not fall when volume falls, because volume is non-discretionary and prices are set by tender cycle rather than by capacity. The pricing pressure that exists is structural (price-led public procurement, 2 to 3% annual like-for-like declines) rather than behavioural.

Reasoning. AddLife targets 45% return on working capital and 15% EBITA growth, prices above competitors on service, and froze acquisitions for two years to repair its balance sheet; Lagercrantz, Addtech, Indutrade and Lifco target niche leadership and refuse auctions; Uniphar enforces a 12 to 15% return on capital employed hurdle within three years; the four Nordic compounders paid a median 6.8x EV/EBITA in 2023 to 2025. None of these is a volume-first actor. Mediq (private) and private-equity platforms are the possible exceptions, but they bid for companies, not for tender volume at a loss. On the customer side, the behaviour that most resembles a price war is the consolidation of public buyers into price-led tenders, which is a buyer-power phenomenon rather than a competitor-attitude one.

For the model: no price-war scenario is needed; the margin risk is buyer-side (tender price caps) and supplier-side (OEM bypass), and both are gradual.

C-29

Stability of that attitude over 18 months to three years; "O'Leary" instability; a "shark attack" on pricing

C58

Question asked How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement: stable, and becoming more disciplined rather than less. The relevant change over three years is AddLife's forced retreat from acquisitions in 2023 to 2024 and its return at sub-7x multiples with a stated preference to reject deals rather than lower its criteria; Vimian's crisis-driven leadership change and pivot to selective bilateral deals; and Uniphar's hurdle-rate discipline. No scaled competitor shows situation-dependent, reactive behaviour, and none has the balance sheet or the incentive to attack industry pricing: a shark attack in medical distribution would mean buying tender volume at a loss to starve a rival of density, and no listed peer's incentive structure (return on working capital, EBITA growth) would reward it.

The one actor with the capacity to destabilise is not a competitor but an OEM: a large device manufacturer deciding to go direct in a category across several countries would behave like a shark attack on the device-distribution margin pool. The precedents (Tandem, DexCom, the AddLife endoscopy loss) show this happens line by line, not industry-wide. For the model, competitor behaviour is a source of predictability, not risk, over the 18-month to three-year horizon.

C-31

How supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support

C55

Question asked Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement: moderately supportive and moderately fragile. Supportive because demand is non-cyclical and growing, the market is fragmented enough to supply targets for a decade, the regulatory trend favours scale, competitor behaviour is rational, and distributors are gaining share from both small rivals and OEM direct sales forces. Fragile because the profit pool for a brand-neutral distributor is structurally thin (adjusted EBITA margins of 9 to 12% against 20%+ for product owners; Lifco 22.6%, Lagercrantz 18.1%), because 70% of purchasing is public and price-led, because the largest customers are consolidating their buying, and because the OEM holds the option to reclaim distribution in any device line that becomes large enough.

Reasoning on the balance. The structure supports a 4 to 5% organic growth rate, a flat-to-slightly-rising gross margin from mix, and a long acquisition runway; it does not support real pricing power or a step change in margin. The fragility is concentrated in two places that the Consumer can monitor: the Nordic tender renewal cycle (2+2 years; the 88% re-win rate is the number to watch) and the Dutch insurer channel (five insurers, relationship-based). Against peers, Asker's structure is more supportive than Uniphar's (Irish wholesale duopoly but 4% EBITDA margins) and less than Vimian's (cash-pay companion-animal niches with 25% margins), and about equal to AddLife's.

C-84

Historical precedents, rules of thumb and empirical evidence on how companies in this sector outperform and underperform, and the extent to which Asker fits

❌ D45

Question asked What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement. The evidence in the files, across the Nordic serial-acquirer cohort and healthcare distribution, supports five rules of thumb. (1) Share-price outperformance follows the direction of estimate revisions and the "beat-and-raise" pattern, and ends when it ends: Lagercrantz's 15 consecutive years of EPS growth earned 23x EV/EBITA until a 3 to 5% EBITA miss in Q1 2026/27 ended the streak; AddLife's Q3 2026 beat earned a target raise and a downgrade to Hold in the same note because the run was priced. (2) Cash conversion is the variable that carries the multiple in serial acquirers: AddLife de-rated to a 26% discount to proven compounders when leverage hit 3.5x in 2023 and re-rated as leverage fell to 2.2x on internally generated cash and acquisitions resumed; Vimian's Q2 2026 operating cash flow of EUR 3.8m (13% conversion) tempered a 4% beat; Storskogen is the sector's example of a debt-fuelled roll-up breaking. (3) Earnings-quality attacks hit Nordic compounders hard and quickly: a short report on Lifco in January 2026 took it down 16% while Lagercrantz rose 22%; the Montier C-score screen that flagged Asker on all six criteria is the kind of evidence a short-seller uses. (4) Proprietary product share is the structural margin determinant: Lagercrantz's move from under 40% to 80% proprietary took gross margin from 21% to 39% and EBITA margin from 3% to 18%; AddLife at about 10% proprietary earns 12%; Asker at 10 to 15% own brand earns 9.5 to 10%; the gap to product owners is structural, "not fixable by execution" (the sector digest on AddLife). (5) Organic growth below 3% breaks the compounder narrative regardless of acquired growth: DNB Carnegie's own line on Asker is that if organic stays below 3%, the premium cannot be supported because acquisitions supply over half of earnings.

Extent of fit. Asker fits rule (1) partially: a record of adjusted EBITA beats (four of six quarters) but a revision cycle that turned negative at the 12-month window; it fails rule (2) at present (cash conversion falling, leverage at ceiling); it has already been hit by rule (3) and the attack came from a broker rather than a short-seller, which leaves the short-seller risk still open; it sits at the low end of rule (4) with no plan to change the proprietary mix materially; and it currently passes rule (5) at 5% organic. The honest summary is that Asker fits the profile of the cohort's underperformers in 2023 (AddLife) more than its outperformers in 2024 to 2025 (Lagercrantz, Lifco before January 2026), with the important difference that it enters the repair phase with better organic growth (5% versus AddLife's 2 to 3%) and a worse accounting reputation. The empirical path to outperformance from here is the AddLife path: four to six quarters of cash conversion near 90%, leverage falling below 2.0x while deals continue, and no further accounting surprises; the path to underperformance is any one of those failing.

C-32

Customer demand: support for the thesis, and fragility

C62

Question asked How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement: customer demand supports the thesis strongly at the level of volume (non-discretionary, ageing-driven, 5% organic in H1 2026, homecare patient growth, broad-based across regions per the CFO) and weakly at the level of price (price-led tenders, reimbursement caps in Germany and Belgium, Dutch insurer concentration). The fragility is in timing and mix rather than volume: equipment and capital projects can be deferred, preparedness orders are lumpy, and a budget squeeze would shift tenders further toward price.

Reasoning on the evidence and its limits. The company does not disclose contract wins, share by country, price versus volume, or customer retention outside the Nordic re-win statistic, so the Consumer is working from aggregate organic growth and the Nordic re-win rate. Both are supportive. The peer corroboration is mixed: AddLife's Medtech grew 5% organic in Q3 2026 but its Labtech only 2% on weak academic research budgets; Neuca (Polish pharmacy wholesale) reported a 4% volume decline as prices rose faster than inflation, which is a reminder that even defensive healthcare volumes have some price elasticity. The demand support is therefore robust to a recession and fragile to a public-finance squeeze, and the two are not the same thing: a European sovereign-stress episode (France at 152 basis points over Germany in October 2026) would reach Asker through tender pricing rather than through volume.

C-33

Route to market and intermediaries: helping or hindering, and fragility

C52

Question asked To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

Judgement: the route to market is mostly direct and is, on balance, a help: 70% of European purchasing is public procurement in which Asker is itself the intermediary and in which scale, compliance and bundled logistics win tenders (88% re-win, 35% of new bids), and the Dutch direct-to-patient channel bypasses hospitals entirely and has compounded for a decade. It hinders in that public tenders cap price, give no volume guarantee ("a hunting license, not a guaranteed volume contract", an expert on AddLife), and are increasingly centralised across regions and borders (Sykehusinnkjøp buying about EUR 5bn a year; Nordic cross-border procurement pacts; German purchasing organisations). The fragility is concentrated: the Dutch insurer channel (five insurers controlling diabetes and care-product contracting for Asker's largest West market) and the Nordic tender cycle.

Reasoning on the two-sided Dutch exposure. The direct-to-patient model is Asker's most differentiated channel (570,000 patients from 80,000 in 2015), it is where the OEM bypass risk is lowest (OEMs cannot run frequent small deliveries to patients), and it is where a single contracting decision by an insurer can move several hundred million kronor of sales. The group's 3% largest-contract figure understates this because the Dutch exposure is a relationship with five payers rather than one contract. For the model: carry the Dutch channel as a concentrated risk with a low annual probability and a high impact; do not assume route-to-market changes in the base case.

C-34

Existing competitors' behaviour: help or hurt, and fragility of Asker's position

C52

Question asked How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

Judgement: existing competitors' behaviour helps Asker's operating position (rational, margin-first, service-led, no price wars) and hurts its growth position (AddLife's restart adds a disciplined bidder for Nordic and European targets; Uniphar is expanding into continental medtech; Mediq and private-equity platforms contest the same deals). Asker's position against them is robust on the customer side and fragile on the deal side, where its leverage (2.37x reported against AddLife's 2.5x, Uniphar's 2.4x and Vimian's 2.1x, all with stated ceilings) leaves it least able to pay up if competition for targets intensifies.

Reasoning. The one peer whose behaviour changed materially in the last six months is AddLife: six acquisitions in twelve months, decentralised deal sourcing to business-unit leaders, refinanced facilities, and leverage at 2.5x; it has become the peer most likely to compete for the same small Nordic and Benelux targets Asker buys. AddLife's purchase prices (under 7x EV/EBITDA for Unicam and Lehnen) show multiples are not yet rising, which matters: the risk is deal flow being shared, not deal prices being bid up. DNB Carnegie after adjustments considers AddLife and Asker "broadly similar" financially with AddLife deserving a small premium on accounting quality and lower leverage; the market has gone further (AddLife on 29x trailing P/E at the 91st percentile of its history; Asker on 19.7x at the 12th). The competitive implication is that the market now funds AddLife's expansion more cheaply than Asker's.

C-35

New entrants: adjacent, standard, orthogonal; the threat of each

C55

Question asked To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

Judgement. Adjacent entrants (OEMs moving downstream to direct sales; large health systems insourcing supply management; pharmaceutical wholesalers such as Oriola moving into medtech) are the most material threat and it is moderate, slow and line-specific. Standard entrants (new distributors) are a negligible threat because MDR compliance, tender history and warehouse density are barriers that take years to build. Orthogonal entrants (digital procurement marketplaces, Amazon-style business platforms, AI-enabled purchasing tools) are a long-dated threat to the commoditised 30% of the range and a low threat to system sales, devices and direct-to-patient, where installation, training, reimbursement handling and clinical support are part of the product.

Reasoning. The OEM-direct trend is the one with precedents in the files (DexCom to 15% direct sales; Tandem's 30% average selling price uplift from going direct in Europe in early 2026; AddLife's endoscopy loss), and the counter-trend is equally documented (mid-sized manufacturers abandoning unprofitable local sales forces; OEMs partnering with Asker under MDR). Net, the device-distribution margin pool is contested at both ends and the consumables and homecare pool is not. For the model this means the "advanced products" mix shift that management is pursuing (about 70% of medtech activity) raises gross margin and simultaneously raises the share of revenue exposed to OEM bypass; the Consumer should not assume device-mix gains are permanent.

C-36

Substitutes and alternatives, and how that is changing

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

Judgement: the substitutes for Asker's offering are (a) the customer doing it themselves (hospital self-warehousing, centralised regional procurement with in-house logistics), (b) the manufacturer doing it (direct sales and e-ordering), and (c) a different channel (online retail pharmacy for patient-paid supplies; Apotea at 10% of Swedish pharmacy share). Each is a real and slowly rising threat, and the direction over the last 18 months is a modest increase in (a) through Nordic and Norwegian procurement centralisation and in (b) through OEM direct models, with (c) still marginal in medical supplies.

Reasoning on magnitude and offset. The offsetting force is that the substitutes solve the customer's problem less well than a fourth-party logistics provider does: Cardinal Health's own risk disclosure lists exactly these substitutes and Cardinal still grows, because the fixed cost of running medical logistics in-house is high and healthcare staff shortages push providers toward outsourcing (the 35% to 40% distributor share gain is the aggregate evidence). The threat is therefore a ceiling on share and price rather than a displacement. For the model: no substitute-driven volume loss in the base case; a slow drift of 10 to 20 basis points a year off gross margin in commoditised lines, offset by mix.

C-37

Regulators and political actors: help or hurt, and fragility

C54

Question asked What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

Judgement: regulators help Asker's competitive position (MDR and IVDR raise fixed compliance costs and documentation burdens that scale carries better; ESG criteria in tenders favour a company with an MSCI AAA rating and a supplier code programme) and hurt its growth and cost (the Danish Competition Authority's year-long review killed a deal; German reimbursement reform caps hospital spend and tightens wound-care evidence requirements; Belgian generic price cuts of up to 70%; NIS2 and GDPR compliance costs on digital and direct-to-patient activity). Political actors matter chiefly through healthcare budgets, which are under pressure everywhere and under most pressure outside the Nordics. The position is moderately fragile at the margin level (reimbursement) and robust at the volume level.

Reasoning on asymmetry. The regulatory help is diffuse and slow (it shows up as acquisition pipeline and tender wins over years); the regulatory hurt is specific and occasionally sudden (a blocked deal; a reimbursement exclusion list). The company's own filing says distributors cannot pass regulatory compliance costs to public buyers because reimbursement contracts are fixed, which is the clearest statement in the files of where margin fragility sits. The sector digest's rule for the Consumer is apt: prefer Nordic exposure where cost inflation is the risk, and rely on volume and procurement scale rather than price in Germany and France. Asker's mix is shifting toward the latter.

C-38

On whom in the value chain disruption acts, how, and how fragile that leaves Asker

C50

Question asked On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Judgement, actor by actor. Small distributors and small manufacturers: MDR, IVDR and tender complexity are acting on them hardest, forcing exits and sales; this is the force that feeds Asker's pipeline and its share gains, and it is the strongest disruption in the chain. OEMs: regulation and cost pressure act on them in two directions, toward outsourcing commercial functions (helps Asker) and toward direct sales in high-value lines (hurts Asker); the net is line-specific. Customers (hospitals, regions, insurers): budget pressure and staff shortages act on them, pushing them toward bundled outsourcing (helps) and toward centralised, price-led procurement (hurts). Regulators: the European Commission's MDR simplification could soften the barrier that helps Asker most. Suppliers of logistics: automation and artificial intelligence in warehousing act on all distributors; Morgan Stanley estimates AI could cut third-party-logistics staff costs 10% (about 180 basis points of margin), which favours those who invest (Gothenburg) and is competed away if customers demand it back.

Fragility assessment. Asker's position is least fragile where disruption acts on the small tail (it is the consolidator) and most fragile where disruption acts on OEMs' channel choice (it is the intermediary that can be bypassed). The company's countermeasures, private label (10 to 15%), the service and compliance layer, and system sales, are the right ones and are partial. For the model, disruption is a mix question (device share of revenue) rather than a volume question.

C-39

Suppliers: a support or a source of risk

C60

Question asked Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

Judgement: suppliers are a support. Asker buys from more than 2,000 OEMs, none above 7% of cost of goods sold, 85 to 90% of revenue is third-party OEM product and the rest own brand; OEMs are increasingly seeking scaled regional distributors to carry MDR compliance and local logistics (CEO, Q1 2025 call); Asker uses its scale to extract longer payment terms (Carnegie) and to lower cost of goods through own brands. The risks are specific and manageable: OEM quotas and single-channel policies to prevent parallel trade; retrospective discount agreements that limit pricing flexibility; OEM bypass in lines above SEK 20 to 30m of local sales; Asian sourcing (20%) with forced-labour and quality audit exposure; the top 25 suppliers at about 48% of cost of goods sold, which is more concentrated than the headline 7% suggests.

The single supplier-side number the Consumer should carry is that 48% top-25 share: it means the supplier relationships that matter are few and that the terms of those relationships (exclusivity, rebate structure, direct-sales rights) set the gross margin more than the long tail does. None of this is changing quickly; supplier power is stable and mildly favourable.

Asker Healthcare (ASKER-SE): First set of questions (continued)

Company - Competitive advantage

C567 questions
C-4155C-4258C-4370C-4458C-4545C-4658C-4752

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Asker's competitive advantage is real, distribution-grade and bounded. The moats (system-sales lock-in in the Nordics, regulatory and compliance scale, logistics density, a modest private-label layer, the Dutch direct-to-patient franchise) protect share and relationships, produce a demonstrably not-inferior offering, and are slowly widening as regulation and consolidation favour scale. They do not confer pricing power above inflation, they do not lift the operating margin above the 10 to 12% ceiling that brand-neutral distributors face, and they are exposed in the device mix to OEMs reclaiming distribution. The addressable market is enormous relative to the company and is not the constraint; capital is. For the Consumer this group justifies a stable-to-rising gross margin from mix, retention-driven organic growth of 4 to 5%, and an adjusted EBITA margin that can reach 10 to 10.5% on reported definitions by 2028; it does not justify a margin trajectory toward AddLife's 12% or beyond without a change in the proprietary product share, which is not planned.

C-41

Sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats: type, strength, direction

C55

Question asked Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

Judgement. Asker holds four moats of moderate strength and one of weak strength; two are widening and two are narrowing. (1) Customer-related, system-sales lock-in: bundling tens of thousands of products with logistics, training and integration into customer ordering systems, at 79% of Swedish and Norwegian sales (65% in 2019), producing an 88% re-win rate; strength moderate-to-strong in the Nordics, weak elsewhere; widening as penetration rises. (2) Regulatory and compliance scale: MDR, IVDR, ESG and documentation requirements that small rivals cannot carry; strength moderate; widening until 2027 to 2029 transition deadlines, then possibly narrowing if simplification passes. (3) Scale-based: 50,000 products, more than 2,000 suppliers, central purchasing, logistics density in the Nordics and Benelux, the Dutch direct-to-patient platform; strength moderate; narrowing in one respect (revenue per employee down 20%) and widening in another (Gothenburg, warehouse automation). (4) Production-related, private label: about 2,000 own articles at 10 to 15% of sales with materially higher gross margin and OEM bargaining power; strength weak-to-moderate; flat. (5) Culture and knowledge: decentralised local management with tender and regulatory know-how, the Asker Business School, a 14-year CEO; strength moderate; at risk of narrowing with turnover at 17.3% and the founder-retention question after earn-outs.

Reasoning on the overall level. These are distribution-grade moats: they protect share and relationships and they do not confer pricing power above inflation or a margin above the sector's 10 to 12% ceiling. The evidence that they are real is the re-win rate, the system-sales penetration and the growth of direct-to-patient; the evidence that they are bounded is the margin gap to product owners (Lagercrantz 18%, Lifco 23%) and the AddLife endoscopy precedent showing an OEM can reclaim a line at will. Net direction: slightly widening over the next three years because MDR and scale are working in Asker's favour and the warehouse adds density; the counterweight is OEM bypass in the growing device mix.

C-42

Scale relative to competitors and other actors, and what it confers

C58

Question asked How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

Judgement: Asker is the largest brand-neutral medical-supplies distributor in Europe by revenue (SEK 16.8bn, over 5,000 employees, 19 countries, over 50 companies) against AddLife (SEK 10.4bn, about 2,400 employees, 30 countries), Uniphar Medtech (a division of a EUR 3.1bn group) and Mediq (private, 14 countries), and is dwarfed by its OEM suppliers and by its largest customers (Norwegian regional health authorities buying EUR 5bn a year). Scale confers four things: purchasing terms and longer supplier payment periods; the ability to carry MDR, IVDR, ESG and IT compliance centrally; tender credibility for large system-sales contracts; and warehouse density that lowers cost to serve. It does not yet confer operating leverage at group level: revenue excluding third-party logistics per employee fell 20% from 2023 to 2025, and the decentralised model deliberately limits central synergy extraction (an expert reports German targets already bought cheaper than the group).

Reasoning on the gap between scale and its benefit. The 2026 evidence is that scale benefits are arriving through mix (acquired companies with higher gross margins) rather than through cost; the Gothenburg centre (SEK 50 to 60m of savings, about 30 basis points) is the first quantified scale-to-cost transmission. For the model, assume scale protects gross margin and tender share and delivers 30 to 50 basis points of operating-cost benefit by 2028, not more.

C-43

Realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and the mid-term growth it allows

C70

Question asked How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

Judgement: the addressable market is very large relative to the company and allows mid-term growth well above the market rate for a decade. The European medtech market is about SEK 1,355bn and the third-party distributor segment about SEK 556bn; Asker's SEK 16.8bn is about 3% of the distributor segment and about 1% of the total; the company's own count is about 800 priority acquisition candidates and Carnegie's about 11,000 potential targets among 33,000 small and medium-sized companies. Market growth of 3 to 4% plus share gain plus consolidation allows 4 to 5% organic sales growth and 7 to 12% acquired growth without approaching any share ceiling.

Reasoning on the realistic constraint. The limit on growth is not the market, it is capital and integration capacity: at the company's 10 to 12% acquired EBITA contribution the consideration need is roughly SEK 1.3 to 1.5bn a year, above internally generated cash after earn-outs, which is why the realistic mid-term growth rate is 12 to 16% in adjusted EBITA rather than the 38% compound rate of 2019 to 2024. An expert's caution that the pool of professionalised targets above EUR 50m revenue thins within two years means that the composition of growth shifts toward more, smaller deals, which raises integration load per krona. The addressable-market score is high; the deployable-capital score is lower (C-50).

C-44

Surplus customer utility against competing offerings: leading, or demonstrably not inferior

C58

Question asked How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

Judgement: demonstrably not inferior, and plausibly leading in the Nordics and the Dutch direct-to-patient channel; not proven elsewhere. The disproof of inferiority rests on three facts: an 88% re-win rate in Sweden, Norway and Finland over 2019 to 2024 (a customer who had the alternative chose to stay); 35% of new Nordic bids won in 2024 (above installed share); and the Dutch patient base growing from 80,000 to 570,000 with "a large portion" organic and management attributing it to service quality. The system-sales model (one supplier, tens of thousands of items, logistics, training, integration into customer IT) reduces the customer's administrative cost in a way a single-line OEM cannot match, and the CEO's claim that ESG and quality standards are winning tenders is consistent with tender criteria moving beyond price.

Reasoning on what is not proven. Outside the Nordics and the Netherlands the company is a collection of recently acquired local distributors, each with its own customer franchise; the utility there is the acquired company's, not Asker's, and the evidence of it is the acquired company's growth, which is not separately disclosed. In Germany and Central Europe the purchasing-organisation structure limits the scope for service differentiation. For the model: assume retention holds in the Nordics and the Netherlands and treat the rest as market-rate.

C-45

Demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of relative utility, shown by application

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

Judgement: demonstrated for cost pass-through, not demonstrated for pricing above inflation. The application evidence is the defence of a 41.1% gross margin through 2022 to 2025 inflation, index-linked public contracts, the CFO's statement in Q1 2026 that there was no supply-chain margin squeeze, and the 110 basis point gross-margin rise in Q2 2026 (which management attributed mainly to acquired mix, cost-of-goods work and "pricing", in that order). Against this: no price-volume disclosure; 55% of tenders price-led; typical 2 to 3% like-for-like annual price declines for distributors; the group's own filing that regulatory costs cannot be passed to public buyers under fixed reimbursement contracts.

Reasoning. The honest classification is that Asker has defensive pricing power (it does not lose margin to inflation) and no offensive pricing power (it does not gain margin from price), which is exactly what a brand-neutral intermediary between powerful OEMs and public buyers should be expected to have. The 2026 gross-margin gain is a mix event, and the Consumer should model gross margin as flat on price and rising only with private label and device share. The research request T-P2 asks for the evidence on renewal pricing in system-sales contracts, which is the one place a real price lever could exist.

C-46

Sharing scale economies with customers: stated or de facto

C58

Question asked To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

Judgement: de facto yes, and stated in the company's language. The business is positioned as "an independent, essential link" that lowers the customer's total cost of care through bundled supply, fourth-party logistics and direct-to-patient fulfilment; the Nordic OneMed companies run warehousing, picking and delivery for public caregivers; system sales exist because they cut the customer's procurement and handling cost; and the Dutch patient model works because it lowers the insurer's cost of chronic-care supply. The scale economy that Asker shares is logistical and administrative (fewer suppliers, fewer deliveries, less inventory on the ward), not price per item, and it is the sharing that creates the lock-in.

Reasoning on the implication. A business that shares scale economies with customers tends to earn its return on working capital rather than on margin, which describes Asker (69% return on net working capital, 9.5 to 10% margin) and distinguishes it from product-owning peers. It also means that customers, not Asker, capture most of the benefit of further scale, so the Consumer should expect operating-cost savings (Gothenburg) to be partly competed back into tender prices over time. In the Wisdom Library's terms this is "scale economies shared": durable, low-margin, and valuable for retention.

C-47

Gravitational or mechanical forces improving advantage and financial outputs each period, rather than heroic dynamism

C52

Question asked To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

Judgement: partly. Three forces act mechanically in Asker's favour each period: the MDR and IVDR compliance burden that keeps pushing small competitors and manufacturers to sell or exit; the demographic and homecare demand that keeps direct-to-patient and consumables volumes compounding; and the rising system-sales penetration that lifts share of wallet in existing Nordic accounts without new tenders. Against these, the growth model itself is not gravitational: more than half of EBITA growth comes from acquisitions that must be sourced, priced, financed and integrated each year, and the cash that funds them must be generated each year against earn-outs and working capital; that is a treadmill, not a flywheel, in the Wisdom Library's terms, until cash conversion proves self-sustaining.

Reasoning on the financial outputs. If management did nothing new, the base business would grow sales 4 to 5% and adjusted EBITA 5 to 8% (management's own organic range), the gross margin would drift up with mix, and the Gothenburg savings would add about 30 basis points in 2027; that is a respectable mechanical outcome and is well below the 15% target. The heroic component is the deal engine, and its heroism is now constrained by the balance sheet. For the model: build the mechanical base (organic) with confidence and the heroic layer (acquired) with a haircut.

Asker Healthcare (ASKER-SE): First set of questions (continued)

Company - Economic strength

❌ D4711 questions
C-4955C-5060C-5150C-5242C-5355C-5430C-7550C-7638C-7740C-7858C-7945

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Asker's economic strength is real in the base business and compromised in its presentation. The base business is a capital-light distributor with top-decile working-capital returns (67 to 69% on net working capital), high returns on newly deployed capital (6 to 8x purchase multiples, a three-year incremental return of about 23%), rising gross and operating margins (adjusted EBITA 8.1% in 2023 to 10.0% in Q2 2026), modest and margin-dominated operating leverage, and a wide, defensive revenue perimeter. The compromises are three and they are linked: the adjusted earnings base is 10 to 15% above a cash-based measure because of capitalised development, provisioning choices and recurring integration cost; cash conversion has fallen from about 100% of adjusted EBITA to about 55 to 65% after earn-outs and intangibles; and financial leverage sits at the company's own ceiling and well above it on an all-in measure. The link is that acquisitions, which supply more than half of EBITA growth, depend on cash that the accounting presentation overstates. For the Consumer's model: adjusted EBITA growth of 18 to 22% in 2026 falling to 12 to 15% in 2027 to 2028; a cash-adjusted EBITA line 10 to 15% lower; EPS on the brokers' basis 5 to 10% below the current FactSet mean for 2027 to 2028 because of interest and amortisation leakage; free cash flow before acquisitions at 55 to 65% of adjusted EBITA; reported leverage falling to about 1.9x by end-2027 only in the case where conversion recovers. The thing that would raise this group's score most is the FY2026 annual report rebuilding provisions and disclosing capitalisation clearly, at a visible one-off cost; the thing that would lower it most is another quarter of statutory operating cash flow near zero.

C-49

EPS growth, with what revenue growth, and how durable

C55

Question asked What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

Judgement. On the brokers' adjusted basis (the only basis on which consensus exists), EPS was SEK 2.07 in 2024 and SEK 2.31 in 2025 (+11%) on revenue growth of 12%; consensus has SEK 2.79 in 2026 (+21%), 3.29 in 2027 (+18%) and 3.77 in 2028 (+15%) on revenue growth of 15%, 10% and 7.5%. Reported basic EPS was SEK 0.94 in 2024 and 1.34 in 2025, and H1 2026 reported EPS was SEK 1.08 against 0.62 in H1 2025 (+74%, flattered by lower shareholder-loan interest after the IPO set-off issue and by the 2025 share count). The durable rate, by my reckoning, is adjusted EPS growth of 12 to 16% a year over 2027 to 2028, below consensus, because revenue growth decelerates as acquisitions annualise, interest does not fall, and depreciation rises.

Reasoning on durability, line by line. Revenue: 15% in 2026 is secure (H1 was 15%; the 2026 deals annualise into H2); 10% in 2027 needs about SEK 1bn of new acquired revenue plus 4 to 5% organic, achievable but requires the deal pace to hold; 7.5% in 2028 already embeds a slowdown. Margin: consensus EBITDA margin 12.6 to 12.7% (brokers' basis, against 12.0% in 2025) is plausible on mix and Gothenburg. Below EBITA: the consensus EBIT-to-pre-tax gap (about SEK 300 to 315m a year through 2028) assumes net financial items flat while the company keeps acquiring, which needs leverage to fall; consensus D&A is held flat at about SEK 500m despite Gothenburg depreciation (SEK 10 to 18m a year from 2027) and rising amortisation of capitalised development (SEK 160 to 243m a year of additions). Share count is stable at 383.7m with 0.8% maximum incentive dilution. Tax about 23.5%. The three-year EPS path is therefore durable in direction and optimistic in slope by two to four points a year.

The most important observation for the Consumer is in the revision data: over the last twelve months consensus FY2027 EBITDA rose 11% and sales 5% while FY2027 EPS fell 3% and FY2028 EPS fell 14%. The bridge from EBITDA to EPS has leaked roughly fourteen points of growth in a year, through some combination of higher interest on acquisition debt, higher amortisation, a higher tax rate and definitional changes as brokers' bases shifted. That leak is the thing to model, and it is why "EPS growth" at Asker is a less reliable number than "EBITA growth".

C-50

Immediate scope to deploy capital at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years; implications for mid-term potential and the fair multiple

C60

Question asked How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

Judgement: the scope is large on the opportunity side and constrained on the funding side. Asker buys at 6 to 8x EBITA (a pre-tax earnings yield of 12.5 to 17% before synergies and integration cost), Carnegie puts the three-year return on incremental capital employed at about 23% to 2025, the pipeline holds about 800 priority candidates, and the target universe is 11,000 to 33,000 companies; that is immediate, repeatable, high-return deployment for well over five years. The constraint is that deployment at the company's 10 to 12% EBITA-contribution target needs about SEK 1.3 to 1.5bn a year, against free cash flow after earn-outs and dividend of perhaps SEK 600 to 800m at current conversion, with leverage at 2.37x against a 2.5x ceiling.

Reasoning on the mid-term and the multiple. If cash conversion recovers to 70 to 80% of adjusted EBITA (the 2022 to 2024 level), the company can deploy SEK 1bn-plus a year with leverage drifting down and earn a blended return on new capital in the mid-teens, which supports a compounder multiple (18 to 22x EV/EBITA, where it traded before September 2026). If conversion stays near 55% (the broker's assumption), deployment falls to SEK 600 to 800m a year, acquired growth to about 7%, and the fair multiple is the AddLife-adjusted 14 to 16x EV/EBITA the shares now carry. The difference between the two states is worth roughly 25 to 35% of the equity value, which is why the cash-conversion question dominates every other. The scope for high-return deployment is therefore a strong positive conditioned on a variable the company has not yet proven at the enlarged scale.

C-51

Returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed, on tangible capital

C50

Question asked How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

Judgement. Return on new invested capital is high: at a 7x purchase multiple, a 10 to 17% acquired EBITA margin and a 23% tax rate, post-tax return on new capital is about 11 to 13% before integration cost and synergy, and Carnegie's three-year ROICE of about 23% (pre-tax, on incremental capital) is consistent with that. Return on capital employed is low on the reported basis (8.1% in 2025, down from 10.0% in 2024; 8.2% in Q1 2026) because goodwill from more than 60 acquisitions sits in the base; on the company's adjusted basis excluding historic owner goodwill it is 10.1 to 10.2%; AddLife's Q3 2026 report cites Asker at 14.8% return on capital employed on a definition it does not state, against AddLife's roughly 11%. Return on tangible capital is very high: return on net working capital is 67 to 69% (target above 50%; AddLife 62 to 63%), net working capital is 8.4% of sales against AddLife's 12.3%, capital intensity is 0.6 to 1% of sales excluding Gothenburg, and the business converts almost all of EBITA into operating cash in a normal year.

Reasoning on which number to use. For valuing the compounding engine, the marginal return on acquisitions (low-to-mid teens post-tax) is the relevant one and it is attractive. For judging the base business, return on net working capital is the company's steering metric and is genuinely top-decile for distribution. For judging what shareholders have earned on all the capital put in, reported ROCE of 8% is the honest figure and it is below the 12 to 15% that mature European healthcare distributors sustain, because the group has bought growth at full price and has not yet earned the goodwill back. The trend in reported ROCE (down two points in a year) should reverse as acquired EBITA matures; the Consumer should model it rising toward 10% by 2028 only if integration costs fall.

C-52

Conversion of net income into free cash flow; behaviour of net working capital and similar items

❌ D42

Question asked How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

Judgement: conversion is deteriorating from an excellent base and is now the weakest line in the financial profile. On FactSet's definition (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex), free cash flow was SEK 914m in 2022, 968m in 2023, 1,069m in 2024 and 738m in 2025 against reported net income of 430m, 203m, 360m and 492m (1.5x in 2025, 3.0x in 2024), which looks strong only because reported net income is depressed by amortisation and because intangible investment, earn-outs and lease payments are not deducted. On the measure that matters for a serial acquirer, operating cash flow as a share of adjusted EBITA, the record is 101% on average over 2022 to 2024 (Carnegie), about 83% in 2025 (SEK 1,328m against 1,594m), about 100% in Q1 2026 (SEK 419m against 442m) and 20% in Q2 2026 on a statutory basis (SEK 92m against 471m) or 70% after adding back the SEK 238m of earn-outs the company routes through operating cash flow. Free cash flow after total capex and intangibles (SEK 590m in 2025) was about 46% of adjusted EBITA in 2025.

Net working capital behaviour. Net working capital rose from SEK 2,157m (2024) to 2,459m (2025) to 2,767m (June 2026), roughly in line with sales so that return on net working capital held at 67 to 69%; but the composition worsened: inventory days from 59 to 65 (2022) to 75 (2025) to 82 (Q2 2026), overdue receivables up 170% in 2025 while credit-loss provisions fell, and the Gothenburg transition adding SEK 50 to 100m of temporary inventory in 2026. Acquired companies typically have lower working-capital efficiency than the group (CEO), so each deal dilutes the metric before integration restores it. Other uses of cash: earn-outs (SEK 463m in Q2 2026; about SEK 150m expected in 2027; SEK 645m to 1,050m outstanding), capitalised development (SEK 160 to 243m a year), Gothenburg (SEK 150m in 2026), dividends (SEK 149m in 2026, about SEK 280m in 2027 on consensus).

What to model. I would set operating cash flow at 80 to 85% of adjusted EBITA for 2026 (Gothenburg inventory, earn-outs through operating cash flow), 85 to 90% for 2027 and 2028 (Gothenburg release, slower inventory build), then deduct capex at 1.5 to 2% of sales, intangible investment of SEK 160 to 200m, earn-outs of SEK 150 to 300m and dividends; that produces free cash flow before acquisitions of roughly 55 to 65% of adjusted EBITA, between the company's presentation and the broker's 55%. Consensus free cash flow of SEK 805m in 2026 rising to 1,262m in 2027 assumes a sharper recovery than this; I would treat the 2027 consensus figure as the upper bound.

C-53

Gross and operating margins and their trend

C55

Question asked How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

Judgement. On the company's definition, gross margin was 40.9 to 41.1% in 2025 and about 42% in Q2 2026 (up about 110 basis points year on year); on FactSet's standardised lines it was 36.1% in 2025 and 36.5% in Q2 2026 (the definitional gap reflects classification of logistics and service costs). The adjusted EBITA margin rose from about 8.1% in 2023 to 9.1% in 2024, 9.5% in 2025, 9.9% in H1 2026 and 10.0% in Q2 2026 (rolling 12 months 9.8%); quarterly it has ranged from 8.5% (Q3 2024) to 10.0% (Q4 2025 and Q2 2026). FactSet's adjusted EBIT margin (after amortisation) was 4.9% in 2023, 7.0% in 2024 and 7.6% in 2025, which is the number that connects to reported EPS. By business area in H1 2026: North 13.6%, West 9.6%, Central 9.2%. The trend is up at every level, driven by acquired-company mix (higher gross margin, more service and technical staff), product pruning in Central, private label and purchasing, and partly by the accounting choices flagged in C-54 (about 40 basis points from provision depletion over 2023 to 2025 on the broker's estimate).

Reasoning on sustainability. Management attributes most of the gross-margin gain to acquired-portfolio mix and warns that those businesses also need more equipment, service and sales staff, so "the eventual EBITA benefit is smaller than the gross-margin gain"; that is consistent with the gap between the 110 basis point gross-margin rise and the 50 basis point adjusted EBITA margin rise in Q2 2026. The peer context sets the ceiling: AddLife 11.5 to 12.1%, Uniphar Medtech much higher at gross level but the group at 4% EBITDA, Vimian 24.5% (a product owner), Lagercrantz 18.1% (a product owner). I expect the reported adjusted EBITA margin to cross 10% on a rolling basis around Q4 2026 to Q1 2027, dip in H2 2026 reporting quarters on Gothenburg costs, and reach 10.3 to 10.8% by 2028 with the warehouse savings and mix; DNB Carnegie's 9.7 to 9.8% flat assumption is too pessimistic on the reported definition and about right on a cash definition.

C-54

Cleanliness of the earnings base: adjusted versus reported, one-offs, capitalised costs, tax and other leakage

❌ D30

Question asked How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

Judgement: the earnings base is not clean, and this is the central weakness of the case. The gap between adjusted and reported earnings is wide: adjusted EBITA SEK 1,594m in 2025 against FactSet adjusted EBIT of 1,276m and reported pre-tax profit of 659m; brokers' adjusted EPS SEK 2.31 against reported basic EPS 1.34 and FactSet diluted EPS before unusual items 2.10. The items that widen it: (1) items affecting comparability of SEK 204m in 2025 (SEK 90m acquisition and integration costs, SEK 96m contingent-consideration revaluation, SEK 18m mainly IPO costs), up from 155m in 2024, with the broker estimating about SEK 15m a quarter of the integration cost is recurring; (2) capitalised development and acquired distribution rights of SEK 109 to 243m a year in 2024 to 2025 whose amortisation is excluded from adjusted EBITA (the broker estimates this lifted 2025 adjusted EBITDA after leases by about 8%), with software useful lives extended from 7.5 to 10 years in 2025; (3) the obsolescence reserve down from 14.9% of gross inventory in 2021 to 6.5% in 2025 (about 40 basis points of gross margin over 2023 to 2025) and credit-loss provisions down while overdue receivables rose 170% (about SEK 60m of under-provision on the broker's estimate); (4) provision releases of SEK 18 to 26m through the P&L in 2024 to 2025; (5) amortisation of acquired intangibles of roughly SEK 300 to 500m a year, which is legitimately excluded from adjusted EBITA but is why reported EPS is so far below adjusted. The tax rate has been volatile (22.6%, 33.4%, 32.8%, 22.6% from 2022 to 2025), reflecting non-deductible shareholder-loan interest before the IPO; consensus assumes about 23.5%, which is reasonable now that the loans are converted.

My own position on the dispute. DNB Carnegie's reconstruction (2025 adjusted EBITA of about SEK 1,226m rather than 1,594m, a 7.3% margin) treats every contested item as fully recurring and fully expensed. I accept the capitalised-development point in full (it is cash, it is recurring, and peer compounders expense more of it), accept the provisioning point as a one-time catch-up cost of SEK 60 to 100m that a prudent new Chair would take, accept half the integration cost as recurring, and reject the contingent-consideration revaluation and IPO costs as non-cash or non-recurring. That gives a "cash EBITA" for 2025 of roughly SEK 1,350 to 1,450m, about 10 to 15% below adjusted EBITA, and a cash margin of 8.0 to 8.6%, which is close to the broker's separately stated 8.1% cash EBITA margin. The Consumer should carry two EBITA lines (adjusted as reported, which the company and sell-side will keep using, and a cash-adjusted line 10 to 15% lower) and derive free cash flow from the second.

The forward implication is the more important one: if the company responds to the critique by expensing more and rebuilding provisions, 2026 or 2027 adjusted EBITA growth takes a one-off hit of 3 to 6 points and the quality improves; if it does not, the discount persists. Either way the model's EPS for 2027 to 2028 should sit 5 to 10% below the current FactSet mean (SEK 3.25 and 3.68), and FCF 15 to 20% below the FactSet estimate.

C-75

Fixed-cost operating leverage: how much a change in revenue moves operating profit, and the range of outcomes it creates

C50

Question asked How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

Judgement: operating leverage is moderate and is dominated by margin rather than volume effects. The cost structure is about 58 to 60% cost of goods (variable), 30 to 32% operating costs (semi-variable: warehouse, logistics and sales staff scale with volume over 12 to 24 months) and under 2% central costs. H1 2026 shows the gearing: 5% organic sales growth produced 7% organic adjusted EBITA growth, an incremental organic margin of about 14% against a 9.9% average; management's framework (5 to 8% organic EBITA growth on about 5% organic sales) implies the same 1.2 to 1.6x gearing. The sensitivities the Consumer should use: a one percentage point change in revenue growth moves EBITA by about SEK 20 to 25m (SEK 0.04 of EPS, about 1.3% of 2027 consensus EPS); a 50 basis point change in the EBITA margin moves EBITA by about SEK 105m (SEK 0.22 of EPS, about 7%); a 1% currency move about SEK 3.7m; a one percentage point interest-rate move about SEK 35m (SEK 0.07 of EPS).

Reasoning on the range of outcomes. Because the margin sensitivity is five times the volume sensitivity, the cone of EPS outcomes is set by margin (mix, Gothenburg timing, provisioning, acquired-company quality) and not by demand, and the demand cone is itself narrow (4 to 5% organic with a 3% floor and 6% ceiling). A plausible 18-month range for adjusted EBITA margin of 9.5% to 10.5% translates into a range of about plus or minus 10% on EPS around the central case; the volume range adds only plus or minus 3%. Downside operating leverage is limited because the cost base flexes with volume in a decentralised model (local managers cut when R/RK falls below 50%), and because volume does not fall in this market. The research request T-P1 asks for peer precedent on how distribution margins behaved through past volume cycles to tighten this.

C-76

Financial leverage and gearing: amplification of EPS and risk, and comfort

❌ D38

Question asked How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

Judgement: financial leverage is material, is at the company's own limit, and is comfortable only if cash conversion holds. Net debt was SEK 4,511m at June 2026 (company definition, excluding leases) against adjusted EBITDA of about SEK 1,900m, 2.37x reported, 2.43x with outstanding acquisition payments, 2.56x on FactSet's lease-inclusive measure (net debt SEK 5,699m against trailing EBITDA), and 3.1 to 3.4x on the broker's measure including SEK 645m to 1,050m of contingent consideration, SEK 127 to 134m of put and call options and treating capitalised development as expense; AddLife, the closest peer, includes earn-outs in its 2.5x. Net financial expense was SEK 350m in 2025 (SEK 244m net interest on FactSet's line) against adjusted EBITA of 1,594m, an interest cover of about 4.5 to 6.5x; undrawn facilities SEK 569m; covenant levels undisclosed. Amplification: at a 25% effective pre-tax interest-plus-amortisation burden on EBITA, a 10% change in EBITA moves pre-tax profit by about 13%; a one percentage point rate move is about 2.5% of EPS.

Reasoning on comfort. The leverage is comfortable in the operating sense (healthcare demand does not fall, interest cover is adequate, the facilities run beyond 2027 as far as the files show) and uncomfortable in the strategic sense: it leaves no room to accelerate acquisitions, it exposes the company to the Riksbank and ECB hiking path (a 100 basis point move on the floating share costs about SEK 35m), it makes the Q4 2026 cash collection a test the market will watch, and it means a shortfall in cash conversion converts directly into a slower deal pace rather than into a covenant breach. The honest comparison is that Asker runs its balance sheet like AddLife did in 2022 (3.5x peak) and has already been de-rated for it. For the model: leverage falling to about 2.1x reported by end-2026 and 1.8 to 1.9x by end-2027 in the base case, requiring free cash flow of SEK 900m-plus before acquisitions in 2027; a 2.5x-plus scenario if conversion stays at 2026 levels.

C-77

How much of each major cost part is cuttable or reducible

❌ D40

Question asked How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

Judgement: little, and the model is not a cost-cutting story. Cost of goods (58 to 60% of sales on FactSet's definition) is reducible only through purchasing scale and private label, both slow; logistics and warehousing (inside the roughly 30% operating-cost block) is the one area with a quantified programme (Gothenburg: SEK 50 to 60m a year from H2 2027, about 3% of EBITA; the Dutch warehouse already done) and a longer-dated automation opportunity (Morgan Stanley's 10% of logistics staff cost from artificial intelligence, perhaps 100 to 180 basis points over five years if retained); sales and service staff are largely non-cuttable because they are the moat and because acquired device businesses need more of them; central costs are already lean (about 45 central employees, 15% central steering) and cutting them would raise control risk, which the Audit Committee can ill afford; integration and acquisition costs (about SEK 60m a year recurring on the broker's view) fall only if the deal pace slows.

Reasoning on the one lever that exists. The decentralised model's mechanism for cost discipline is the return on net working capital threshold: subsidiaries below 50% get a group-mandated profitability plan, which is how Central's margin rose from 7.8% to 9.2% in a year by exiting low-margin product. That is a margin lever rather than a cost-cut lever, and it is the one the Consumer should model: a further 30 to 50 basis points of group margin from mix pruning over 2027 to 2028 is plausible. A large cost programme is neither available nor consistent with the strategy.

C-78

Beyond cost, the plausible scope to improve financial outputs over the mid term; peer precedents; few big or many small; congruence with strategy

C58

Question asked Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

Judgement: the scope is moderate, is achievable on peer precedent, comes from many small areas rather than a few big ones, and is congruent with the stated strategy. The levers: (1) mix, toward devices (about 70% of medtech activity), private label (10 to 15% of sales, rising), homecare and direct-to-patient; (2) pruning low-margin product and sub-scale categories, as in Central; (3) Gothenburg logistics savings (about 30 basis points) and Nordic warehouse automation; (4) working-capital normalisation after the Gothenburg transition (inventory days from 82 back toward 70 to 75, releasing SEK 300 to 400m); (5) acquired-company margins (new deals at 10 to 17% EBITA versus the group's 9.5 to 10%); (6) falling integration cost as the 2024 to 2025 deal cohort matures. Together these can lift the reported adjusted EBITA margin from 9.5% (2025) to 10.3 to 10.8% by 2028 and free cash flow conversion from the mid-50s to 65 to 70% of EBITA.

Peer precedents. AddLife took its EBITA margin from 10.5% (2023) to 12.1% (2025) and 12.5% (Q1 2026) through ten consecutive quarters of exactly these actions (closing Camanio, restructuring AddVision, pruning, divesting UK endoscopy, dismantling central functions) with no volume growth to speak of; that is the direct precedent and it shows the levers work and that the market pays for them (AddLife's multiple went from a 26% discount to proven compounders to a premium). Lagercrantz's proprietary-mix shift is the stronger precedent but it required a different business (product ownership) that Asker does not plan to become. Congruence: every lever is in the CEO's own list ("gradual realisation of benefits from our enhanced capacity and market presence, and maintaining a focus on cash flow and capital efficiency"), so the improvement case does not require a strategy change. The research request T-P1 asks for the dated precedents and what they delivered.

C-79

Narrowness of the business perimeter, how it is changing, and the meaning for predictability

❌ D45

Question asked How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

Judgement: the perimeter is wide and widening, and that reduces predictability at the margin line while leaving the revenue line predictable. Two reported segments (medical supplies, devices and equipment at SEK 15.2bn and services at SEK 1.6bn in 2025), three business areas (North 31% of sales at a 13.6% margin, West 51% at 9.6%, Central 18% at 9.2%), 19 countries (17 in 2024; four entered in 2025), more than 50 companies (about 45 in 2024), over 5,000 employees, 50,000 products, 2,000 suppliers, and sales channels that differ by country (public tenders in the Nordics and Baltics, insurers in the Netherlands, purchasing organisations in Germany, direct business-to-business elsewhere). Non-Swedish sales rose from 72% to 78% between 2023 and 2025 and non-Nordic from 61% to 69%.

Reasoning on what widening does to predictability. Revenue predictability is high and improving because defensive demand is diversified across more countries and channels and no contract exceeds 3% of sales. Margin and cash predictability are lower and falling because each new country adds a reimbursement system, a legacy IT system, a working-capital culture and a competition regulator; because the mix shift from North to West and Central dilutes the group margin by about 4 basis points per point of mix; and because the decentralised model (85% freedom) means central management sees local problems late (the company's own risk disclosure on legacy ERP and data quality; no internal audit function). The honest comparison is AddLife at 85 subsidiaries in 30 countries, which experts called "a pure reporting shell" with weak synergy extraction; Asker is slightly more centralised (45 central staff, Centres of Excellence) and is heading in the same direction. For the model: tight revenue cone, wider margin cone, widest cash cone, and a quarterly pattern in which the headline beats and the cash line surprises.

Asker Healthcare (ASKER-SE): First set of questions (continued)
