Company - Management

C5311 questions
C-5568C-5658C-5758C-57b52C-5850C-5955C-6042C-6160C-6245C-6345C-6452

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Management is a strong operating team with large personal ownership, a coherent and conventional strategy, a demanding growth ambition and a long record of delivering it, whose financial-reporting posture has become the main obstacle to the investment case. The chief executive's actions over fourteen years are those of a capable owner-operator; the actions missing are the ones that turn an adjusted-EBITA compounder into a cash compounder the market trusts: including earn-outs in leverage, expensing more development, rebuilding provisions, disclosing covenants and acquisition economics, establishing internal audit, and guiding on cash. The new Chair, the principal owner's representative on the Audit Committee, the public forensic challenge and the de-rating give management both the motive and the opportunity to make those changes in the FY2026 report, and the CEO's July 2026 language suggests the direction has already been chosen. For the Consumer this group means: trust the operating lines management guides (organic growth, acquisition pace, business-area margins) at roughly the stated level; discount the lines it does not guide (free cash flow, leverage after earn-outs, EPS) by 10 to 20% against the company's presentation; and treat an accounting clean-up as a likely 2027 event that lowers adjusted EBITA growth by a few points and raises the multiple by more.

C-55

The size of the company's ambition or vision, particularly against peers

C68

Question asked How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

Judgement: the ambition is large and explicit, and larger than every listed peer's in the cohort on growth. The framework (adjusted EBITA growth above 15% a year with no end date; 10 to 20 acquisitions a year; acquisitions contributing 10 to 12% of EBITA growth; organic EBITA growth of 5 to 8%; entering more European countries, with France, Spain and Italy named as white spots; a pipeline of about 800 priority candidates; the CEO's "never seen such a good pipeline" in 13 years) sits above AddLife's 15% target split half organic and half acquired, above Uniphar's EUR 200m EBITDA by 2028 (about 15% a year, 80% organic), in line with Lagercrantz's 15% EBT growth, and below only Vimian's EUR 300m by 2030 (about 27% a year), which no one believes. Asker has delivered on the ambition: adjusted EBITA compounded at 38% from 2019 to 2024, 17% in 2025 and 21% on a rolling basis to Q2 2026.

Reasoning on what the ambition signals. The force is genuine: 14 deals in 2025, four new countries, an IPO, a SEK 300m warehouse and a 10,000-person-equivalent integration programme in two years are the actions of a management that prioritises growth. The qualification is that the ambition has outrun the balance sheet and the control environment, and that the CEO's language in the last six months has shifted from "pick and choose the best ones" (July 2025) to "gradual realisation of benefits" and "focus on cash flow and capital efficiency" (July 2026). That is the right adjustment and it is a reduction in near-term ambition in practice if not in targets. Relative to peers, Asker remains the fastest grower by design and the most levered by consequence.

C-56

How great and smart the strategy is: moat-widening, growth priority, dynamism across environments

C58

Question asked How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

Judgement: the strategy is sound, conventional for the cohort, and smart in two specific respects; it is not bold and it is not yet proven at the enlarged scale. Sound: decentralised local companies (tender and regulatory knowledge, customer intimacy) plus central Centres of Excellence (purchasing, logistics, IT, own brands) plus programmatic bolt-on acquisitions at 6 to 8x is the Nordic serial-acquirer model that has created the cohort's best records. Smart in two respects: the system-sales and fourth-party-logistics model turns a distributor into a customer's supply-chain department, which widens the moat with each contract and is the reason the Nordic re-win rate is 88%; and the direct-to-patient model in the Netherlands, now being extended to Germany through GHC/MPF, is a channel OEMs cannot easily replicate and that compounds with chronic-disease demographics.

Reasoning on where it falls short. The strategy widens the moat where Asker is already strong and does not address the structural margin ceiling: there is no plan to raise the proprietary product share materially (the Lagercrantz lever), and the growth plan requires more capital than the business generates at current conversion. It is moderately dynamic across environments: healthcare demand is non-cyclical, the decentralised model adapts locally, and the acquisition engine can be throttled; but the same engine makes the strategy pro-cyclical to credit conditions, which is the environment now. The honest grade is a good strategy executed to the edge of its funding, and the smartest thing management can do in the next 18 months is the unglamorous one it has started to signal: digest, prove cash, and let the multiple recover before accelerating again.

C-57

How great the chief executive is, judged by actions

C58

Question asked How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

Judgement: Johan Falk is a strong operator with a rare profile (McKinsey, Getinge Infection Control CEO, B&B Tools business-area CEO, 14 years at OneMed and Asker, 9.06 million shares worth about SEK 640m at today's price) whose actions have built a pan-European platform from a Nordic distributor, delivered every stated financial target since listing, bought more than 60 companies at disciplined prices with a 23% three-year incremental return, and walked away from a deal when the process failed; and who has tolerated an accounting and disclosure posture that has now cost the company its premium multiple. The actions on the positive side are large and verifiable; the action on the negative side is the absence of one.

Reasoning on the balance. Enablers: the twin-engine model was his design; the R/RK steering metric is his and it works (69%); the IPO was executed at SEK 70 and the shareholder-loan set-off at the same price, which was fair to minorities; the pipeline, pricing discipline and integration model are his. Ossifiers: the leverage definition excludes earn-outs when the closest peer includes them; the company does not disclose covenants, EPS or free-cash-flow guidance or a price-volume bridge; the Montier C-score flagged all six criteria for 2025; the board under him has twice declined to establish an internal audit function; he deflects granular questions to rolling 12-month metrics. The Leadership Foundations report's harsh conclusion ("tolerance or enablement of aggressive accounting practices") overstates it, because none of the practices is outside the accounting standards and a new Chair with serial-acquirer experience now sits above him; but the pattern of maximising the adjusted headline is his responsibility and it is the thing that most limits the multiple. On the Animal CEO scale he scores high on force and agency, medium on capital-allocation discipline, and low on outlier transparency.

C-57b

Hiring well as a priority; the quality of the leadership team

C52

Question asked How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built (an enabler)?

Judgement: the team is stable, experienced and adequate; it is not demonstrably outlier. The Group Management Team is nine people: CEO; CFO Thomas Moss (Cambridge engineer, Diageo and Vattenfall treasury, at Asker since 2019, 989,000 shares, improved R/RK from 49% to 66% in late 2024); COO Peter Gustafsson (since 2013); three business-area heads (Peter Nilsson, North; Pieter-Jan Jongeling, West; Magnus Alsterlind, Central) with local P&L authority; Head of M&A Lovisa Hedin (since 2022, internal promotion, over 40 deals); Head of HR and ESG Kerstin Mjömark (ex-Boliden); and investor relations held by the CFO. Executive turnover is low (no material departures in 18 months above the two second-tier changes), succession planning is described as systematic, and the Asker Business School trains acquired managers. Total team compensation rose to SEK 50.8m in 2025 from 42.1m.

Reasoning on quality. The evidence that the CEO hires well is indirect: the regional heads delivered 10 to 15% organic growth across all three areas in 2026 with margin gains, and the M&A function sourced 14 deals in a year. The evidence against is the CFO's role in the leverage and capitalisation choices under dispute, his inability to explain SEK 24m of other operating income on the Q2 2026 call, his May 2026 share sale, and the combined CFO-IR role, which concentrates the market-facing function in the person whose judgements are being questioned. The bench below the nine is thin by design (decentralisation) and dependent on acquired founders staying past their earn-outs. Compared with AddLife (a two-person group management) the team is deeper; compared with Lagercrantz or Lifco it has no outlier track record outside the CEO. Hiring a strong independent Chair (Stöhr) and a non-executive investment director (Petersson) on the Audit Committee is the recent evidence that the governance side of hiring is being taken seriously.

C-58

How well resourced and invested the whole business is

C50

Question asked How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

Judgement: moderately well resourced for growth and under-resourced for control. Growth resourcing: the Gothenburg distribution centre (about SEK 300m, automation, capacity for the enlarged group), the completed Dutch warehouse, capitalised IT development of SEK 160 to 243m a year, Centres of Excellence in purchasing, logistics, IT and own brands, a 45-person centre, the Business School, and capex of 4% of sales in 2025 (1% normal run-rate excluding Gothenburg). Control resourcing: no internal audit function (the board again decided against one in 2025), sustainability-reporting controls still being developed, an internal-control function planned for 2026, four whistleblowing cases and one fraud incident in 2025 against one and none in 2024, legacy ERP systems retained in acquired companies, and a decentralised model that the company's own risk disclosure says can produce inconsistent data quality.

Reasoning on the "ant colony" test. The business was built for growth and is now being asked to be resilient; the Gothenburg investment and the IT capitalisation are the right long-term resources, and the Q2 2026 double-manning and double-inventory costs are the price of building them before they are needed, which is the right order. The gap is on the control side at exactly the moment the market is questioning the accounts: an internal audit function and a quantified integration cost line would cost perhaps SEK 20 to 30m a year and would be worth multiples of that in credibility. Buffer: SEK 569m of undrawn facilities and about SEK 800m of cash is adequate for operations and thin for a 10-to-20-deal programme.

C-59

How holistic and congruent the culture is across the total system

C55

Question asked How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

Judgement: congruent in design and strained in execution. The design is coherent: decentralised accountability (85% freedom, 15% control), one steering metric (return on net working capital above 50%, with autonomy above 70% and intervention below 50%), incentives that pay on adjusted EBITA growth (85% of LTIP) and sustainability (15%), a Business School that teaches the standard, an IPO structure that put the CEO, CFO and board into equity, and a strategy that rewards local managers for winning tenders and keeping capital light. The congruence shows in the outputs: 69% R/RK, broad-based organic growth across regions, a 76 engagement score above the 70 target, 92% code-of-conduct training.

The strains: total employee turnover 17.3% in 2025 (16.5% in 2024; voluntary 12.7%) against AddLife's 10% and a stable-distributor baseline of 8 to 12%; sick leave above the 4% target "due to organisational changes and several long-term absences"; the incentive weight on adjusted EBITA that rewards capitalising and adding back; and the inherent tension in a model that promises local autonomy while the centre consolidates warehouses, imposes a management standard within 12 months and pursues purchasing synergies. The Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair standard of a system in which goals, strategy, culture, execution and decisions reinforce each other is partly met: the operating culture and the strategy are aligned; the financial-reporting culture and the shareholder base's needs are not yet. The Consumer should expect the culture to deliver organic growth and working-capital discipline and should not expect it to deliver central cost synergies.

C-60

How clear, straightforward and helpful the communication is, including on the driver transmission mechanism

❌ D42

Question asked How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

Judgement: clear on the framework and the operating story, opaque on everything between adjusted EBITA and cash. Helpful: the targets are few and stated; the twin-engine logic is explained with its components (5 to 8% organic EBITA, 10 to 12% acquired, margin from mix and scale); acquisitions are announced with revenue, employees and rationale; the Q2 2026 report named the Gothenburg costs, their timing and the savings; the CEO says plainly what the group pays for deals; the CFO states inventory and earn-out effects. Unhelpful: no revenue, EPS or free-cash-flow guidance; no price-volume bridge; no covenant disclosure; "adjusted cash flow from operating activities" that adds back earn-outs paid; a leverage ratio that excludes contingent consideration; no breakdown of capitalised development; no quantified synergy target for any acquisition; no disclosure of subsidiary margins; a company-compiled consensus whose contributors are not named; and the CFO's hesitation on SEK 24m of other operating income in Q2 2026.

Reasoning on the transmission mechanism. The investor is told how sales become adjusted EBITA (mix, scale, organic growth, acquisitions) and is not told how adjusted EBITA becomes earnings per share and cash (amortisation of capitalised development, integration cost, earn-outs, interest, tax, working capital). The result is exactly the gap the sell-side revisions show: EBITDA estimates rising and EPS estimates falling with no company narrative to reconcile them. The Wisdom Library's "management that can't explain" pattern is partly present. The new Chair's tenure and the FY2026 annual report are the natural points for improvement, and the research request T-P4 asks for the inputs the company does not give.

C-61

Record of acting, not just talking: recent huge delivery and a mid-term record, whatever the environment

C60

Question asked How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

Judgement: the record of action is strong on growth and operations and weaker on the financial lines the market now prices. Recent delivery (last 18 months): IPO completed in March 2025 at SEK 70 raising SEK 1.5bn with SEK 1.2bn of debt repaid; 14 acquisitions signed in 2025 and 12 completed, four new countries; seven to eight more deals in 2026 with over half the annual target met by July; adjusted EBITA growth above 15% in every rolling period from Q1 2025 to Q2 2026 (17% to 22%); adjusted EBITA beats against broker estimates in four of six quarters, one in line, one 1% above company-compiled consensus; organic growth restored from -1% (Q4 2025) to 5% (H1 2026); the Dutch warehouse relocated; Gothenburg testing started on schedule in April 2026; Central's margin lifted 140 basis points by pruning; a first dividend paid. Mid-term delivery (2019 to 2025): more than 60 acquisitions integrated, sales from a Nordic base to SEK 16.8bn, adjusted EBITA from about SEK 300m to 1,594m, margin from about 8% to 9.5%, all through the pandemic, the 2022 to 2023 inflation and the 2023 to 2024 rate rise.

The weaker lines: the 2025 organic slowdown was explained as comparison, which was true, but management did not flag the SEK 350m defence orders as non-recurring when they were booked in 2024; cash conversion fell from about 100% to about 83% of adjusted EBITA in 2025 and to a statutory 20% in Q2 2026; reported leverage rose from 1.84x to 2.37x in a year; revenue per employee fell 20% in two years; and the Kirstine Hardam termination, though sensibly framed, is a miss against the deal plan. The pattern is "delivers what it targets, and targets what it can deliver". The environment test is passed: the company outgrew every listed peer through a difficult rates environment, which is more than AddLife or Vimian can say.

C-62

Outlier standards: driving higher, faster and better, with a record of out-executing competitors

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent does the company hold outlier standards, driving higher, faster and better, with a record of out-executing competitors?

Judgement: Asker has outlier standards on growth and on working-capital return, and ordinary standards on margin, cash conversion, disclosure and control. It out-executes competitors on deal velocity (19 acquisitions in 2025 and H1 2026 against AddLife's seven), on organic growth (5% against AddLife's 3 to 4%), on return on net working capital (69% against AddLife's 62 to 63% and a sector base rate of 40 to 50%; Lagercrantz and Addtech at 81% are the outliers above it), and on the Dutch direct-to-patient scale (570,000 patients). It is out-executed on margin (9.5 to 10% against AddLife 11.5 to 12.1%, Uniphar Medtech much higher at the division, Lagercrantz 18%, Lifco 23%), on cash conversion (AddLife 83% in 2025 and about 95% targeted; Lagercrantz about 72 to 74% lease-adjusted; Asker about 55 to 65% after intangibles and earn-outs), on balance-sheet conservatism (every peer includes earn-outs or holds leverage lower), and on reporting transparency (AddLife's first-time divisional disclosure, Uniphar's segmental EBITDA, Lagercrantz's divisional margins; Asker discloses business-area margins but not subsidiary or acquisition economics).

The one standard that would move the needle is cash: a Lagercrantz-style "we don't do budgets, we do cash" culture is not visible, and the company's own definition of cash flow adds back the largest cash cost. The honest grade is a fast and disciplined acquirer that has not yet adopted the outlier financial standards of the cohort's best.

C-63

Putting shareholders first, ahead of management; prudence of governance; the evidence

❌ D45

Question asked Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

Judgement: the alignment evidence is strong and the prudence evidence is weak, and the two conclusions sit side by side. Alignment: the CEO holds 9.06m shares (about 2.4% of the company), the CFO 989,000, the Chair 291,970 call options at SEK 94.90 (bought at Black-Scholes value, so real capital at risk), directors hold shares (Vinje 159,638; Linander 77,857); the LTIP 2026 requires executives to buy and hold investment shares to Q1 2029, with a 12.5% minimum EBITA compound rate to vest, and maximum dilution of 0.81% across plans; variable pay is capped at 75% of base for the CEO; pensions are defined-contribution; the IPO and the SEK 1,439m shareholder-loan set-off were both at SEK 70, so the controlling owner took no price advantage over new investors; a 30% dividend policy was honoured in the first year; Interogo (40.2%) is a patient owner with no record of extracting from minorities. The principal owner's representative sits on the Audit Committee, which is non-independent but aligned.

Prudence: no internal audit function after two board reviews; the leverage ratio defined to exclude SEK 645m to 1,050m of contingent consideration; covenant levels undisclosed; software lives extended; provisions reduced while overdue receivables rose; items affecting comparability at 11 to 13% of adjusted EBITA; a combined CFO-IR role; a six-person board with four independent of the owner; the CFO's SEK 17.5m share sale in May 2026 three months before the forensic downgrade; no scaffolding "put in place where it was not needed". The evidence therefore says management and the owner are heavily invested alongside shareholders (which lowers the risk of value extraction) and have not built the conservative financial-reporting apparatus that a listed serial acquirer needs (which raises the risk of a credibility shock). In the Wisdom Library's terms, insider ownership is not the same as alignment on reporting conservatism, and the incentive weight on adjusted EBITA is the mechanism that pulls the two apart.

C-64

An enlightened capital allocator and dynamic repurchaser, not just an operator; the record of allocating at a high return

C52

Question asked To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Judgement: an enlightened acquirer and an ordinary allocator in every other respect. The acquisition record is the strength: more than 60 deals at 6 to 8x EBITA, a three-year incremental return of about 23%, H1 2026 acquisitions at a 17% EBITA margin on consolidation, platform entries priced one to two turns higher and justified by cross-selling, and one terminated deal rather than one overpaid. The weaknesses: there is no buyback and no stated intention of one (the only repurchase was 670,000 Class C shares for the LTIP); the dividend is small and formulaic; the balance sheet was taken to its ceiling within fifteen months of listing; the Gothenburg investment is sensible but is the first large organic capital project and its return is not yet visible; capitalised development of SEK 160 to 243m a year is a capital allocation whose return the company does not report; and the return on all capital employed is 8.1% reported.

Reasoning on "dynamic repurchaser". The shares have fallen to the IPO price and to the 9th percentile of their own forward P/E history; a dynamic allocator with a clean balance sheet would be buying its own shares at 16x EV/EBITA rather than private companies at 7x only if the public multiple were below the private one, which it is not, so the absence of a buyback is defensible on arithmetic and the question is moot until leverage falls. What is missing is a stated capital-allocation hierarchy (deleverage to X, then acquire, then return) of the kind AddLife has made explicit and the market has rewarded; management's July 2026 language is a step toward it. For the model: no buybacks through 2028; dividends at 30 to 35% of adjusted net income; acquisition spend of SEK 0.8 to 1.2bn a year funded from cash flow with leverage drifting down.

Risks

❌ D424 questions
C-8050C-8142C-8235C-8340

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The risk profile is defined by one correlated cluster and one sentiment setup rather than by demand. The cluster is earnings quality, cash conversion and leverage: each is individually manageable, together they form the "false friend" path in which adjusted EBITA keeps growing at 15% while cash, leverage and the deal pace deteriorate until the model stalls; the Risks report's competing-hypotheses matrix found this profile the only one with no hard inconsistency, and I agree it is the most likely failure mode, with a probability over 18 months that I put at 25 to 35% in a mild form (a disclosed clean-up with a one-off hit) and 10 to 15% in a severe form (a forced one with a covenant consequence). The sentiment setup is poor and will stay poor until the FY2026 annual report answers the capitalisation and provisioning questions. The compensations are that demand carries no cyclical risk, the structural organic rate of 4 to 5% is intact, the de-rating has already taken the shares to the IPO price and the 9th percentile of their own multiple history, and the fair trough is 30 to 37% below the current price rather than the 50 to 60% that an undisrupted premium multiple would have implied. For the Consumer's model, the risks translate into: a base case with a 3 to 6 point one-off drag on 2027 adjusted EBITA growth for a voluntary clean-up; a downside case with acquired growth at 5%, organic at 3%, cash conversion at 55% and leverage flat at 2.4x; and an explicit note that the EPS cone is wide below the central case and narrow above it until the accounts are cleaned.

C-80

Has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: revenue growth versus the company's normal rate, and is the input change force slowing

C50

Question asked To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

Judgement: the organic engine decelerated in 2025 (3% against a 2022 to 2025 average of about 6% and a historic norm of about 7%), re-accelerated to 5% in the first half of 2026, and now runs about one point below its own normal rate; the acquired engine has not decelerated (12 to 13% of sales growth, 17 to 18% of adjusted EBITA growth in H1 2026); the input change force (European medtech market growth of 3 to 4%, the distributor share gain, the homecare shift) has slowed slightly at the margin (MedTech Europe's 1.8% in 2025; Morgan Stanley's cautious European medtech outlook; Neuca's 4% volume decline in Poland) but has not turned.

Reasoning on what the twelve-month pattern means. The 2025 deceleration was a comparison effect from SEK 350m of 2024 defence and preparedness orders in North, and the H1 2026 recovery was broad-based across regions, companies and countries (CFO), which argues that the engine itself did not slow; the adjusted-for-defence organic rate in 2025 was close to the 5% now reported, which the broker's own note concedes ("underlying organic expansion was very similar to AddLife"). The honest reading is that Asker's structural organic rate is 4 to 5%, not 7%, that the 7% historical average included pandemic and preparedness demand that will not recur, and that the company now grows organically at about 1 to 1.5 points above its market rather than 3 points above. That is a durable rate and it is below what the 2019 to 2024 record suggests. The input force (ageing, homecare, outsourcing) is intact; the only decelerating input is public budget capacity, which caps price rather than volume. Over the next twelve months, the comparison base is neutral in Q3 2026 and easy in Q4 2026, so organic growth should hold at 4 to 5%; the thing to watch is organic adjusted EBITA growth in North through the Gothenburg transition, which management has guided will run below trend.

C-81

Sudden, high-magnitude, low-probability fundamental risks: plausibility and size

❌ D42

Question asked What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

Judgement, ranked by expected impact. (1) An accounting event: an auditor-driven restatement, a covenant redefinition or a management-initiated provision rebuild and capitalisation reversal that cuts reported adjusted EBITA by 10 to 20% in one report; probability low-to-medium (10 to 20% over 18 months, higher for a voluntary clean-up than a forced one), magnitude 20 to 35% on the shares on top of the de-rating already taken; this is the one the files argue about and the one the FY2026 annual report (February to April 2027) will resolve one way or the other. (2) Loss of a major OEM line to direct sales in a device category across several countries (AddLife endoscopy precedent), or quota and single-channel restrictions on parallel supply; probability low (5 to 10% a year for a material line), magnitude 1 to 3% of sales and more of margin because devices carry higher gross margin. (3) A Dutch insurer re-contracting away from Asker in diabetes or care products; probability low (under 5% a year), magnitude several hundred million kronor of sales and a sharp hit to West's margin because the direct-to-patient infrastructure is fixed. (4) Loss of a large Nordic regional system-sales tender at renewal; probability low-to-medium given the 88% re-win rate implies about one in eight contracts is lost, magnitude up to 3% of sales for the largest; this is the most frequent of the sudden risks and the most survivable. (5) Key-person loss (the CEO, 14 years, central to the acquisition engine, no disclosed successor); probability low, magnitude a multiple event rather than an earnings event. (6) A cyber or data incident under NIS2 and GDPR as direct-to-patient and digital services grow; probability unknown, magnitude a fine of up to 4% of turnover plus reputational loss in public tenders. (7) A competition-authority block of a platform deal (precedent: Kirstine Hardam); probability medium, magnitude 1% of forecast sales per occurrence.

Reasoning on the joint risk. The fundamental risks are mostly independent of each other and mostly survivable; the exception is (1), which is correlated with leverage because a lower EBITA raises the all-in ratio toward 3.5x, and which could, in a bad case, coincide with a slower deal pace and a founder-departure wave after 2023 earn-outs expire. That correlated cluster is the "false friend" path the Risks report names, and I rate it as the one sudden risk worth a named scenario in the Consumer's model.

C-82

Perception- or sentiment-driven sudden risks, including events elsewhere in the sector

❌ D35

Question asked What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

Judgement: the sentiment risks are more numerous and more probable than the fundamental ones, and several are live. (1) A short-seller report on Asker: the Montier C-score result (the only Swedish compounder flagged on all six criteria), the capitalised-development, provisioning and leverage-definition points, and the Lifco precedent (a January 2026 short report took Lifco down 16% while peers rose) make Asker the most obvious target in the cohort; the 20% underperformance since September 2026 reduces the reward but not the material; probability medium over 12 months. (2) Another broker reversal: coverage is thin (three brokers on FactSet; six named by the company; one, DNB Carnegie, dominates the research pool), so a single downgrade moves the consensus and the price, as September 2026 showed. (3) Sector contagion: a cash-flow or leverage disappointment at AddLife, Vimian or Lagercrantz, or a failed Nordic roll-up (Storskogen is the standing example), would hit Asker as the most levered and least trusted member of the cohort; AddLife's Q4 2026 cash collection (year-end report 3 February 2027) is the nearest such event. (4) Rates: the Riksbank's expected November 2026 hike and a US 10-year near 5.1% keep the regime hostile to small-cap levered growth; the Fitness for Fighting digest's Double Underweight on Fragility applies directly. (5) Currency: the krona strengthening toward EUR/SEK 10.40 to 10.60 by 2027 would reinstate the reported-growth headwind and trigger small estimate cuts. (6) Ownership: Interogo's 40.2% stake is a potential overhang if the family ever placed shares, and the thin free float (about 50% after Interogo, SEB and Capital Group) and USD 6 to 8m daily traded value amplify any move; a lock-up expiry is not mentioned in the files but the IPO was in March 2025 and the 12-month lock-up has passed. (7) Healthcare as a crowded defensive: Jefferies' "healthcare has become the home for semis and tech dollars" means the sector's relative strength is flow-driven and reverses when technology stops falling; Asker's relative strength percentile of 20 means it has not even benefited from the flow.

Reasoning on which bite hardest. (1) and (2) are company-specific and are the reason the stock cannot be bought on valuation alone before the FY2026 report; (3) and (4) are the environment and are not controllable; (5) is small; (6) and (7) are structural. The honest summary is that the sentiment setup is poor: technical momentum F, the 200-day average falling in every one of the last twelve months, a 12-month return of -15.5% against the OMX Stockholm's underperformance of 36 points, and no catalyst dated before 4 November 2026.

C-83

How far the shares could fall to a fair trough multiple

❌ D40

Question asked How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

Judgement: a fair trough is SEK 45 to 50 (about 30 to 37% below SEK 70.8), and a stress trough is SEK 32 to 38 (about 45 to 55% below). The shares have already fallen from a 52-week high of about SEK 95 and a post-IPO EV/EBITA of 21 to 22x to about 16x (24-month forward P/E 19.5x, the 9th percentile of the five-year range whose median is 24.0x); so part of the de-rating is done, which is why the fair trough is closer than the historic range suggests.

Construction. Fair trough: a brand-neutral distributor with contested accounting and leverage at its ceiling deserves no premium to AddLife's cash-adjusted multiple; DNB Carnegie's September valuation of 15x 2027 EV to cash-adjusted EBITA and a 5.1% equity free-cash-flow yield gave SEK 60, and its October blend (peer value SEK 62, acquisition scenario SEK 71) gave 66.5. Applying 14 to 15x forward P/E to a 2027 EPS haircut 10% for interest and amortisation leakage (about SEK 3.0) gives SEK 42 to 45; applying 13x EV/EBITA (the Swedish small-cap average the Risks report cites) to 2027 cash-adjusted EBITA of about SEK 1,850m with net debt of SEK 5.7bn (lease-inclusive) gives an equity value of about SEK 48 a share. The band SEK 45 to 50 reconciles these. Stress trough: the Risks report's downside case (adjusted EPS of SEK 2.1 for 2026 if acquisitions halt, at a 15x P/E) gives SEK 32; a scenario in which a forced provisioning and capitalisation clean-up cuts 2027 adjusted EBITA 20% to about SEK 1,650m at 12x EV/EBITA gives about SEK 36 a share. The 52-week low of SEK 56.2 (set in the weeks after the September downgrade) is not a floor; it is where the first wave of sellers stopped.

Reasoning on asymmetry. Against the trough, the upside case (cash conversion restored, leverage below 2.0x, clean FY2026 accounts, the margin crossing 10%) returns the shares to 19 to 21x EV/EBITA on 2027 EBITA of about SEK 2.1bn, roughly SEK 95 to 105, which is where the two Buy-rated brokers (Nordea 106; the restricted broker 82 on a Hold) sit. The distribution is therefore roughly symmetric around the current price with a fat lower tail, which is the correct shape for a stock whose main uncertainty is a binary disclosure event rather than demand.

Guidance

C506 questions
C-6852C-7048C-7155C-7250C-8556C-7340

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Asker's guidance is a floor, not a forecast: a 15% adjusted EBITA growth target that management has beaten by two to seven points in every period, a 10% margin target that is reached on the reported definition and not on a cash one, and a 2.5x leverage ceiling that excludes the obligations that would breach it. The building blocks for EBITA are clearly stated and credible; everything below EBITA is unguided and is where the sell-side's doubts, the EPS dispersion and the de-rating all sit. Informal commentary runs above the formal framework on the operating engine and has begun, since July 2026, to run below it on pace and cash. For the Consumer: build adjusted EBITA from management's blocks (organic 5 to 7%, acquired 8 to 10% in 2027, Gothenburg 30 basis points from H2 2027) and build the rest from the broker's sensitivities with a conservative hand; expect consensus EBITDA for 2027 (10% growth) to prove too low and consensus EPS for 2027 to 2028 (18% and 15% growth) to prove too high, and expect no change to the formal targets before the margin crosses 10% on a rolling basis.

C-68

Company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this year and next, and why

C52

Question asked To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

Judgement: Asker gives no numerical annual guidance, so the comparison is between its standing framework (adjusted EBITA growth above 15% a year; margin above 10% medium term; organic EBITA growth 5 to 8%; acquisitions 10 to 12% of EBITA growth) and consensus. For FY2026 consensus is above the framework: FactSet median EBITDA growth of 20.9% (SEK 2,436m from 2,015m) and EPS growth of 20.8% (SEK 2.79 from 2.31), against a framework floor of 15%; the gap exists because H1 2026 already delivered 23% adjusted EBITA growth and DNB Carnegie expects 19% in Q3, so consensus is simply extrapolating delivery. For FY2027 consensus is below the framework: EBITDA growth of 10.2% (SEK 2,684m) and revenue growth of 10.1%, against a framework that implies 15% or more; EPS growth of 18.0% (SEK 3.29) is above the framework only because consensus assumes falling interest and flat amortisation. For FY2028 consensus EBITDA growth of 5.7% is far below the framework, reflecting DNB Carnegie's 7% acquired-growth assumption and the single-broker thinness of the out-years.

Why the gap. The sell-side has stopped believing the acquisition engine can run at 10 to 12% of EBITA growth from 2027 without leverage rising, and management has not addressed that doubt with anything more specific than "the pipeline is visible". My own position sits between: 2027 adjusted EBITA growth of 12 to 15% (organic 5 to 7% plus acquired 7 to 9%, less a possible clean-up drag), above consensus EBITDA growth of 10% and below the framework. The Consumer should note that the FY2027 consensus EBITDA of SEK 2,684m against DNB Carnegie's 2,650m and Nordea's 2,780m is a three-broker spread of 5%, which is narrow on EBITDA and misleadingly so given the 24% spread on 2026 EPS (SEK 2.47 to 3.57) that definitional differences create.

C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance

❌ D48

Question asked Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

Judgement: Asker has never raised its targets and shows no behavioural inclination to do so; it beats the framework quietly and lets the numbers speak. The 15% growth floor has been exceeded in every rolling period since listing (17% to 22%) without a raise; the margin target was approached (9.8% rolling) without a timeline; the acquisition contribution target was "more than halfway" by July 2026 without any statement about exceeding it; the leverage ceiling was tightened, not loosened. The CEO's qualitative commentary is where the upgrades happen: "never seen such a good pipeline" (July 2025), "excellent pipeline visibility", "already discussing targets for Q4 2026 and Q1 2027" (February 2026), 800 priority candidates (May 2026).

Reasoning on what this implies for the Consumer. There is no beat-and-raise cycle to ride, so the staircase of positive estimate revisions that drives compounder share prices (Lagercrantz, Lifco before 2026) has not operated at Asker and will not unless the company changes its communication habits. The flip side is that there is no guidance to cut, so a bad quarter produces a broker revision rather than a guidance-cut event. The one place a formal raise is plausible is the margin target once the rolling figure crosses 10%, and management would more likely restate it as "above 10% achieved" than set a new one. Treat the targets as a floor that management is comfortable beating by 2 to 7 points, and treat the qualitative pipeline commentary as the real signal.

C-71

How conservative current guidance is for the next one to three years against the last one to five, adjusted for the difference in input change forces

C55

Question asked How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

Judgement: the growth target (above 15% adjusted EBITA a year) is conservative against the record (38% compound 2019 to 2024, 17% in 2025, 21% rolling to Q2 2026) and roughly right against the forward input forces; the margin target (above 10% medium term) is undemanding on the reported definition (10.0% already in Q2 2026) and demanding on a cash definition (8.1% in 2025 on the broker's measure); the leverage ceiling (below 2.5x on the company's definition) is not conservative at all, since the all-in figure already exceeds it.

Reasoning on the adjustment for input forces. The 2019 to 2024 record was produced by a smaller base, private-company leverage, pandemic and preparedness demand, cheap debt from 2020 to 2022, a shareholder-loan structure, and a less competitive target market. None of those forces is present now: the base is three times larger, the debt is public and at its ceiling, rates are high, AddLife and Uniphar are back in the deal market, and the organic rate has normalised from 7% to 4 to 5%. Against that backdrop, 15% adjusted EBITA growth requires acquisitions at the full 10 to 12% contribution plus organic at the top of the 5 to 8% range, which is achievable in 2026 (because H1 deals annualise) and a stretch in 2027 to 2028 without leverage rising. So the target is conservative against the past and only mildly conservative against the next three years. The honest classification is "undemanding floor, realistic ceiling": management will beat 15% in 2026 by a wide margin and may only just meet it in 2027 to 2028 on a clean basis.

C-72

How specific the guidance is: built from stated building blocks or vague

C50

Question asked How specific is the guidance: built from stated building blocks, or vague about how results come about?

Judgement: specific at the level of building blocks for adjusted EBITA and silent below it. The components are stated and repeated: organic adjusted EBITA growth generally 5 to 8%; acquisitions contributing 10 to 12% to annual EBITA growth; margin progression from mix and scale; organic sales growth slightly above a 3 to 4% market; acquisitions at 6 to 8x EBITA, 10 to 20 a year; Gothenburg savings of SEK 50 to 60m a year from H2 2027 with SEK 150m of 2026 capex and low tens of millions of double-manning cost; about SEK 150m of earn-outs in 2027; a 30%-plus payout. The gaps: no revenue guidance; no EBIT, EPS, tax-rate, interest-cost or free-cash-flow guidance; no price-volume split; no capitalised-development guidance; no covenant disclosure; no disclosure of acquired-company EBITA at the group level beyond the half-year contribution; no synergy targets.

Reasoning. The building blocks are good enough to construct the EBITA line with confidence and leave the Consumer to construct everything below EBITA from FactSet, the broker and judgement, which is why the EPS consensus is so dispersed. The company's building blocks describe how results "come about" up to EBITA; below that line the result "just comes about". DNB Carnegie's sensitivity work (0.5 percentage points of margin equals about SEK 0.22 of EPS; one point of revenue growth about SEK 0.04) is the best available bridge and it is a broker's, not the company's.

C-85

Informal and qualitative commentary above or below formal guidance and consensus for this year and next, and why

C56

Question asked To what extent is informal/qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

Judgement: management's informal commentary is above its formal guidance and above FY2027 consensus, and about in line with FY2026 consensus. Above formal: 21% rolling adjusted EBITA growth against the 15% floor; "more than halfway" to the acquisition target by July; "excellent pipeline visibility"; 800 priority candidates; targets already under discussion for Q4 2026 and Q1 2027; "deal availability and quality should not be a problem over the next one to three years, capital under the 2.5x ceiling is the constraint". Above FY2027 consensus: a company already discussing Q1 2027 deals is describing an acquired contribution closer to 10% than to the 7% DNB Carnegie models from 2027, and management's organic range of 5 to 8% EBITA sits above the broker's 3 to 4% long-term organic sales assumption. In line with FY2026 consensus: DNB Carnegie's 19% Q3 adjusted EBITA growth forecast and 5% organic growth are consistent with management's tone, and the Gothenburg double-manning warning for H2 2026 and early 2027 has been given clearly and is in the broker's numbers.

Why the informal commentary runs above: management is signalling capacity and confidence in the operating engine while declining to address the financing and accounting doubts that drive the sell-side's lower numbers; the two sides are talking about different lines. Below formal, the one soft signal is the July 2026 shift to "gradual realisation of benefits" and "cash flow and capital efficiency", which is management's own acknowledgement that the pace must moderate. Net, informal commentary supports modelling acquired growth above the broker's 7% and below the framework's 10 to 12%.

C-73

What else is material or uncommon about the guidance or investor relations

❌ D40

Question asked What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

Judgement: five things. (1) The metric hierarchy is unusual: the company guides on adjusted EBITA growth, a non-GAAP measure that excludes acquisition and integration costs and contingent-consideration revaluations and that excludes amortisation of capitalised development as well as of acquired intangibles; on return on net working capital, a measure that flatters a business funding working capital through supplier terms; and on a leverage ratio that excludes earn-outs. Each is defensible; the combination presents the company at its most favourable on every line. (2) "Adjusted cash flow from operating activities" adds back contingent-consideration payments routed through operating cash flow (SEK 238m in Q2 2026), which is an uncommon presentation and one the Consumer should reverse. (3) Covenant levels are not disclosed (the broker flags this as a data gap), which for a company at its own leverage ceiling is material. (4) Coverage is thin and concentrated: three brokers on FactSet, six named by the company, with DNB Carnegie supplying nearly all the research in the files and having moved Buy to Sell to Hold within eight weeks under an analyst change; FactSet's "buy share 67%" and the three visible ratings (one Buy, two Hold) do not reconcile, which suggests the count includes brokers FactSet does not show. (5) The company compiles its own consensus for results comparisons without naming contributors.

The implication for the fourth set: the investor-relations setup makes the stock's narrative unusually dependent on one broker's interpretation and on the company's chosen metrics, which is why a single forensic note moved the shares 20% against a peer; the FY2026 annual report is the first document that can reset this, and a capital markets day (none announced) would be the second.

Asker Healthcare (ASKER-SE): First set of questions (continued)

Sell-side estimates

❌ D483 questions
C-6748C-6958C-7442

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The sell-side picture is thin, dispersed and internally inconsistent in ways that are more useful than a clean consensus would be. Three brokers set a FactSet median that has revenue and EBITDA growth decelerating sharply from 2026 to 2028, EPS growing faster than EBITDA on flat interest and flat depreciation, free cash flow doubling by 2027, and leverage falling to 1.5x while acquisitions continue at 7% a year; those last three cannot all be true. The record against estimates is good on the adjusted headline and weakening on cash; the cuts since September 2026 have come from one broker and have not reached consensus EBITDA at all. For the Consumer, the implication is to take consensus revenue and 2026 EBITDA as the anchor, raise 2027 EBITDA growth from 10% toward 12 to 15% on the acquisition arithmetic, lower 2027 to 2028 EPS by 5 to 10% for interest and depreciation leakage, cut 2027 free cash flow by 15 to 20% for capitalised development and earn-outs, and choose a single EPS definition (DNB Carnegie's adjusted, which adds back acquired-intangible amortisation, is the one the company's own adjusted EBITA is consistent with) rather than the FactSet blend that mixes bases.

C-67

The sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years against the last one to five, line by line, and the credibility of the gap

❌ D48

Question asked How do the sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five years, for the main financial statement line items in particular and the granular working up to them, and how credible is the gap given the change forces and foundations found above?

Judgement. The FactSet median consensus (three brokers for 2026 to 2028, one for 2029) and its comparison with history, line by line:

Revenue: history SEK 11,718m (2022), 13,453m (2023, +14.8%), 15,025m (2024, +11.7%), 16,787m (2025, +11.7%); 2019 to 2024 compound growth 27%; consensus 19,262m (2026, +14.7%), 21,214m (2027, +10.1%), 22,798m (2028, +7.5%). Credible: 2026 is locked by H1's 15% and the annualising 2026 deals; 2027 needs about SEK 1bn of new acquired sales plus 4 to 5% organic and a flat currency; 2028 already assumes the engine slows. I would put 2027 at 10 to 12% and 2028 at 8 to 10% if the deal pace holds at 8 to 10% acquired contribution; the gap to history is the acquisition pace and the lost currency tailwind, and it is credible.

EBITDA (brokers' basis): 2024 SEK 1,633m, 2025 2,015m (12.0% margin); consensus 2,436m (2026, +20.9%, 12.6%), 2,684m (2027, +10.2%, 12.7%), 2,838m (2028, +5.7%, 12.4%). On the company's adjusted EBITA definition: 1,090m (2023, about 8.1%), 1,362m (2024, 9.1%), 1,594m (2025, 9.5%); DNB Carnegie's October estimates imply 2026 about 1,900m at 9.8% and 2027 about 2,100m at 10.1%; Bloomberg consensus 2027 adjusted EBITA SEK 2.1bn at 10.3%. Credible for 2026; too low for 2027 (10% EBITDA growth is below the organic plus acquired arithmetic unless a clean-up drag is assumed); 2028 is a single-broker artefact and should not be used.

EBIT and D&A: consensus EBIT SEK 1,440m (2026), 1,647m (2027), 1,828m (2028) with D&A held flat at about SEK 500 to 510m; against FactSet's history of total D&A at SEK 816m in 2025 (which includes acquired-intangible amortisation and leases). The flat D&A assumption is the weakest line: Gothenburg depreciation (SEK 10 to 18m a year from 2027), rising amortisation of SEK 160 to 243m of annual capitalised development, and lease depreciation on a larger estate all point up. I would add SEK 30 to 60m a year to D&A through 2028.

Interest and tax: consensus pre-tax profit SEK 1,140m, 1,332m and 1,512m implies net financial items of about SEK 300m, 315m and 316m, roughly flat against SEK 350m in 2025 (SEK 244m net interest on FactSet's line); the tax rate implied is 23.5 to 24% against 22.6% in 2025 and 33% in 2023 to 2024. Flat interest requires leverage to fall while rates hold, which is the base case only if cash conversion recovers; the tax rate is reasonable now that shareholder loans are converted. Risk to interest is upward by SEK 20 to 50m.

Net income and EPS (brokers' basis): 2024 SEK 2.07, 2025 2.31 (+11%); consensus 2.79 (2026, +20.8%), 3.29 (2027, +18.0%), 3.77 (2028, +14.8%); the FactSet mean is 2.82, 3.25, 3.68; the three brokers span SEK 2.47 (Nordea) to 3.57 (DNB Carnegie) for 2026, a 45% range that can only be definitional (reported versus adjusted for amortisation). Share count 383.7m, stable. Credible in direction; the slope is 2 to 4 points a year too steep for 2027 to 2028 because of the D&A and interest points above and the possibility of a provisioning clean-up. I would use SEK 3.0 to 3.1 for 2027 and 3.4 to 3.5 for 2028 on the DNB Carnegie definition, and the Consumer must choose one definition and rebuild rather than use the FactSet blend.

Cash flow and balance sheet: FactSet free cash flow (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex) was SEK 914m, 968m, 1,069m and 738m from 2022 to 2025; consensus 805m (2026), 1,262m (2027), 1,388m (2028) on operating cash flow of 1,463m, 1,874m and 2,120m and capex of 403m, 285m and 358m. The 2027 step-up (operating cash flow from 60% to 70% of EBITDA) assumes Gothenburg inventory releases, earn-outs fall to about SEK 150m and working capital normalises; it is the upper bound of what is plausible and does not deduct capitalised development or earn-outs. Consensus has no net-debt line visible, but DNB Carnegie's 1.9x by 2027 and 1.5x by 2028 require essentially all of that free cash flow to go to debt reduction, which contradicts a 7 to 10% acquired-growth assumption; the two cannot both be right, and that internal inconsistency is the most useful thing the consensus tells the Consumer.

Dividends: SEK 0.39 (2025); consensus 0.725, 0.86, 0.97 (the 2029 figure of 5.75 is a data error). Capex: 1% of sales in 2022 to 2024, 4% in 2025 (Gothenburg), consensus 2% then 1 to 2%. Credible.

Working capital and other key metrics: not in consensus; the company's own return on net working capital of 67 to 69%, inventory days of 82, net working capital of 8.4% of sales (company definition) or 15.5% of trailing revenue (FactSet definition including payables differently) should be modelled explicitly, with inventory days falling to 75 by 2028.

Overall credibility of the gap. The forward numbers describe a company growing revenue at half its historical rate, EBITDA margins up 50 to 70 basis points, EPS growing faster than EBITDA because of falling interest, and free cash flow doubling in two years; the first two are credible, the third is optimistic, the fourth is the upper bound. The change forces and foundations above support revenue and margin and do not support the below-the-line improvement without a demonstrated recovery in cash conversion.

C-69

The record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over one, three and five years, including in comparable conditions

C58

Question asked What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

Judgement: against adjusted EBITA expectations the record is good; against organic growth and cash it is mixed; against guidance there is no record because there is no numerical guidance. Last year (Q3 2025 to Q2 2026): Q3 2025 adjusted EBITA SEK 383m, 6% above FactSet consensus and 7% above DNB Carnegie (organic 5%); Q4 2025 SEK 470m, 5% above both (but organic -1% against consensus +2%); Q1 2026 SEK 442m, 5% above FactSet and company-compiled consensus and 7% above DNB Carnegie (organic 5% against 3% expected); Q2 2026 SEK 471m, 1% above company-compiled consensus and in line with DNB Carnegie (organic 5% as expected; cash flow weak). Three years (as far as the files reach): Q1 2025 a 4% beat (organic 6%); Q2 2025 in line with FactSet and 2% below DNB Carnegie (organic 3%); FY2025 adjusted EBITA growth of 17% against the 15% target; 2024 and 2023 not covered by broker comparison in the files but the 2019 to 2024 compound rate of 38% was far above any target. Five years: the company was private until March 2025, so there is no estimate record; the delivery record is the growth figures above.

Comparable conditions. The relevant comparable is the 2023 to 2024 rate rise and inflation, through which Asker passed costs through, grew adjusted EBITA from SEK 1,090m to 1,362m (+25%) and sustained operating cash conversion of about 100%; the current rates environment is similar and the company's recent cash conversion is not, which is the one deterioration against comparable conditions. Pattern: a company that usually beats on the adjusted headline by 1 to 7%, misses occasionally on organic growth when comparisons distort it, and has begun to miss on cash. The largest identified beat is 7% (Q1 2026 against DNB Carnegie); the largest identified shortfall 2% (Q2 2025). For the Consumer: assume Q3 2026 lands at or slightly above the 19% adjusted EBITA growth the broker expects, with the cash and leverage lines the risk.

C-74

Have estimates been cut deeply and fully across all analysts; has guidance been rebased, kitchen-sinked or sandbagged

❌ D42

Question asked To what extent have sell-side estimates been cut deeply and fully across all analysts, and has company guidance recently been rebased lower, or more aggressively kitchen-sinked or sandbagged?

Judgement: no, no and no. The cuts have been partial, concentrated in one broker and below the EBITA line. Over the last twelve months FactSet's FY2027 consensus EBITDA rose 11% and sales 5% while FY2027 EPS fell 3%, FY2028 EPS fell 14%, the target price fell 8% over three months, and the buy-rating share fell 17 points over three and six months to 67%; the FactSet mean EPS range over the year was SEK 2.73 to 3.09 for 2026 and 3.65 to 4.54 for 2028. DNB Carnegie's September cut was 2 to 5% on 2027 to 2028 EPS, 15 to 17% on equity free cash flow, and placed its 2027 to 2028 adjusted EBITA 4 to 9% below Bloomberg consensus; its October note nudged EPS back up 1% on currency. Nordea (Buy, SEK 106, dated 7 October 2026) and the restricted broker (Hold, SEK 82) have not followed the forensic cut, and FactSet's consensus EBITDA has not moved. Guidance has not been rebased: the framework is unchanged, no profit warning has been issued, and management's commentary is confident; there is no kitchen-sinking (no provision rebuild, no impairment, no restructuring charge announced) and no sandbagging (the 15% floor has always been a floor).

Reasoning on what this means. The estimate cycle is incomplete in both directions: if the forensic critique is right, the other brokers and FactSet consensus still have 5 to 10% of EPS and 15 to 20% of free cash flow to cut, which is why the stock is not yet a "fully cut" recovery candidate; if the critique is overdone, the September downgrade will reverse and the Hold-heavy ratings (two of three visible) will migrate back to Buy. The sell-side revision score of 3 on the dashboard (C grade) is consistent with a stock in the middle of that process. The Wisdom Library's "composite signal masks component deterioration" applies directly: the headline revision picture (EBITDA up) hides the component (EPS and cash down), and the Consumer should model from the component.

Stock market narrative

C521 question
C-2252

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The narrative setup over the next 18 months is a proof cycle, not a discovery cycle: every positive development the market can look forward to (a 10% margin, Gothenburg savings, falling leverage, continued deals, a cleaner annual report) is already known, dated and doubted, and the share price at the IPO level and the 9th percentile of its multiple history says the market is waiting to be shown rather than told. The decisive step is the FY2026 annual report in early 2027, because it is the only event that can settle the earnings-quality dispute that has governed the stock since September 2026; the next most important are the two cash-flow prints (Q4 2026 and Q4 2027) that show whether the acquisition engine is self-funding. For the Consumer's model and for the fourth set, this means the positive narrative turns on three dated, observable, modellable items (statutory cash conversion, reported leverage including earn-outs, and the margin crossing 10%), and that the narrative's upside is larger than its novelty because the multiple has further to recover than the earnings have to grow.

C-22

The staircase of novel positive developments ahead: within six months, months 6 to 18, months 18 to 36; novelty; foundation

C52

Question asked What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

Judgement. The staircase is real, mostly dated, and modest in novelty; its steps are proofs rather than surprises, because the market already knows what the company plans and doubts whether it can fund it.

Within six months (to April 2027). (1) Q3 2026 results, 4 November 2026: expected adjusted EBITA growth of about 19% with 5% organic growth and the first Gothenburg double-manning costs in North; novelty low; well-founded; the market will read the statutory cash flow and the reported leverage before the EBITA line. (2) The acquisition announcements management says are in discussion for Q4 2026 and Q1 2027: novelty low in kind, medium in evidence (they show the engine still runs at the 2.5x ceiling); well-founded on the pipeline commentary. (3) Q4 2026 and full-year results, early February 2027: the year-end cash collection and the reported leverage ratio (my base case about 2.1x; the broker's 2.5x); the rolling 12-month adjusted EBITA margin plausibly crossing 10% for the first time; the proposed 2026 dividend (consensus SEK 0.73); novelty medium (the first stated target "met"); well-founded on H1 2026 delivery. (4) The Gothenburg distribution centre fully operational (guided for the end of 2026 or H1 2027): novelty low; well-founded; it ends the double-running cost and starts the savings clock. (5) The FY2026 annual report (February to April 2027): the first full disclosure since the forensic critique of capitalised development, provisioning, items affecting comparability and (possibly) covenant terms; novelty high if the company rebuilds provisions and re-presents leverage to include earn-outs, which I think is the more likely path under the new Chair; this is the single most important event on the staircase and it is as likely to produce a one-off earnings hit as a relief rally.

Months 6 to 18 (May 2027 to April 2028). (6) The AGM in May 2027: a possible LTIP revision toward cash or return metrics; the first full year of the new Chair; novelty low. (7) The first half of 2027: Gothenburg savings begin to show in North's margin from H2 2027 (SEK 50 to 60m a year, about 30 basis points of group margin); inventory days fall as the double stock unwinds; 2027 earn-outs of about SEK 150m are paid; novelty medium; well-founded. (8) Leverage falling below 2.0x on the company's definition during 2027 if cash conversion recovers, which would reopen the acquisition engine in the market's eyes and is the AddLife precedent's re-rating trigger; novelty medium; foundation conditional. (9) A possible capital markets day (none announced; the company has been listed two and a half years by then and has never held one); novelty high if it comes with a cash-flow framework and acquisition-economics disclosure; foundation speculative. (10) Possible return of Nordic preparedness and defence orders as budgets rise; novelty medium; foundation speculative and lumpy.

Months 18 to 36 (May 2028 to April 2029). (11) The 2028 results showing whether the adjusted EBITA margin reaches 10.5% or more with a cash margin near 9%, whether acquired growth has held near 8 to 10%, and whether reported leverage sits near 1.5 to 1.8x; novelty low; this is the period in which the compounder label is either re-earned or lost. (12) Entry into one of the named white spots (France, Spain, Italy) through a platform acquisition; novelty medium; foundation on management's stated intent only. (13) The MDR and IVDR transition deadlines (2027 to 2029) forcing further small-competitor exits and supplier outsourcing; novelty low; well-founded structurally.

How well-founded, overall. Steps 1 to 4, 7 and 11 are dated and rest on disclosed plans; step 5 is dated and binary; steps 8, 9, 10 and 12 are conditional. The staircase has no step that the market cannot already see, which is why the narrative is "prove it" rather than "discover it"; the one development that would be genuinely novel is a voluntary accounting clean-up with a leverage re-presentation, and it is novel precisely because it would cost reported earnings in the short run.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Asker Healthcare Group AB (ASKER-SE, Nasdaq Stockholm), a decentralised European distributor of medical supplies, devices and equipment (not pharmaceuticals) to hospitals, primary care, elderly care and homecare, operating more than 50 companies in 19 countries through three business areas: North (Sweden, Norway, Finland, the Baltics), West (Benelux, United Kingdom, Ireland, Denmark) and Central (Germany, Austria, Switzerland, France, Poland, Czech Republic, Slovakia); reported segments are Medical Supplies, Devices and Equipment, and Services; the group grows through organic sales and 10 to 20 bolt-on acquisitions a year.

Request: T-P1

Peers: AddLife AB (ALIF.B-SE), the closest listed European medical and laboratory distribution compounder, which expanded its EBITA margin from 10.5% in 2023 to 12.1% in 2025 through restructuring, pruning and warehouse and central-cost actions while deleveraging; Uniphar PLC (UPR-IE), an Irish pharmaceutical and medtech distributor building a new Greenogue distribution centre with disclosed dual-running costs in 2026; Lagercrantz Group AB (LAGR.B-SE), Addtech AB, Indutrade AB and Lifco AB, the Nordic serial-acquirer benchmarks for how decentralised groups capture or forgo central scale economies; Mediq (private, Netherlands, medical supplies distribution in 14 countries) and Oriola Corporation (Finland, pharmaceutical wholesale and retail) as close private and adjacent competitors for the cost structure of healthcare distribution; Cardinal Health, McKesson and Owens & Minor for the United States med-surg distribution precedent on logistics automation and margin behaviour through volume cycles. Inputs: What is the split of fixed, semi-variable and variable cost for a brand-neutral medical-supplies distributor, by cost line (cost of goods, warehousing and logistics, sales and technical service staff, central functions), and how does Asker's reported cost structure compare (Asker 2025 annual report: gross margin 41.1%, adjusted EBITA margin 9.5%; FactSet standardised gross margin 36.1%)? How much operating leverage did Asker show in H1 2026, when the company reported 5% organic sales growth and 7% organic adjusted EBITA growth (interim report, 21 July 2026), and how does that incremental margin compare with AddLife's and Uniphar's recent organic quarters? What did AddLife's 2023 to 2025 self-help programme (closure of Camanio, restructuring of AddVision, dismantling of central functions for more than SEK 20m of annual savings, divestment of UK endoscopy) deliver against what was promised, over what period, and which cost lines proved cuttable? What has Uniphar disclosed on Greenogue 2 dual-running costs (EUR 20m to 25m guided for 2026, Deutsche Bank, 8 September 2026) and the timing of the subsequent savings, as a precedent for Asker's Gothenburg distribution centre (total about SEK 300m of capital, management guidance of SEK 50m to 60m of annual savings from the second half of 2027, with double-manning costs in late 2026 and early 2027, per the Q2 2026 earnings call of 21 July 2026)? How have medical and laboratory distributors' operating margins behaved in past down-cycles (2008 to 2009, 2020) and up-cycles, with dated figures? What is the evidence that decentralised Nordic serial acquirers extract procurement synergies from acquired distributors, given an expert's statement (former Head of M&A at Asker, 4 April 2025) that some German targets already bought at lower unit costs than the group? Why did Asker's revenue excluding third-party-logistics agent revenue per full-time employee fall from about SEK 5.45m in 2023 to SEK 4.52m in 2025 (DNB Carnegie, 10 September 2026), and is that pattern seen at peers after rapid acquisition? What does the Morgan Stanley estimate (6 July 2026) that artificial intelligence in third-party logistics could cut staff costs by 10% imply for distributor margins, and which distributors have retained such gains rather than passing them to customers? How much of a scaled distributor's central cost (Asker about 45 central employees) can be reduced without weakening financial control, and what did AddLife's move to a two-person group management deliver? Focus: Whether a brand-neutral medical-supplies distributor of Asker's scale can lift its operating margin by mix, logistics consolidation and pruning rather than by volume, how much of the Gothenburg saving and the AddLife-style self-help precedent is realistically available to Asker over 2027 to 2029, and whether the fall in revenue per employee signals a structural cost problem or a temporary integration effect.

Request: T-P2

Peers: AddLife AB (ALIF.B-SE), which discloses that distributors absorb typical annual like-for-like price declines of 2 to 3% offset by mix, prices above competitors on service in several tenders, and has 21% of 2025 sales under currency indexation clauses; Uniphar PLC (UPR-IE), for Irish and United Kingdom wholesale and medtech pricing under reimbursement frameworks; Mediq (private, Netherlands), the direct competitor in Dutch insurer-contracted homecare supply; Oriola Corporation and Tamro (Finland), for duopoly pricing behaviour in Nordic healthcare wholesale; Coloplast, Convatec and Mölnlycke as upstream manufacturers whose pricing to distributors and direct-to-provider channels sets the distributor's margin; Cardinal Health and Owens & Minor for the United States precedent on med-surg distribution pricing through inflation and group-purchasing consolidation. Operating conditions: The 2021 to 2023 input-cost and freight inflation and its pass-through to public healthcare customers; a public healthcare budget squeeze with tenders weighted to price (the company states 55% of European procurement processes focus mainly on price); the Nordic 2+2-year public tender renewal cycle and the 88% re-win rate Asker reported for Sweden, Norway and Finland over 2019 to 2024 (Carnegie, 6 May 2025); centralisation of procurement across Nordic regions and borders (Sykehusinnkjøp in Norway, cross-border Nordic procurement pacts); the Dutch insurer-contracted channel where five insurers (Zilveren Kruis, CZ, VGZ, Menzis, DSW) set terms for diabetes and care products; German reimbursement reform and purchasing-organisation dominance; a strengthening or weakening Swedish krona against the euro (the company reports a 1% move changes group earnings by about SEK 3.7m, 2025 annual report); a period of rising interest rates in which customers extend payment terms. Inputs: What price increases has Asker achieved and held in each operating condition above, and what has it given back, with dated figures, given management's statements that cost inflation was passed through ("we are floating as a cork", Q4 2025 earnings call, 10 February 2026) and the CFO's statement in Q1 2026 that there was no supply-chain margin squeeze? How much of the 110 basis point year-on-year rise in Asker's gross margin to about 42% in Q2 2026 (Q2 2026 earnings call, 21 July 2026) was price, how much acquired-portfolio mix, how much private label and how much cost of goods, and what do customers and competitors say? What are the indexation and pass-through clauses, their lags and their caps in Nordic public system-sales contracts, Dutch insurer contracts, German homecare contracts and direct business-to-business contracts, and how do they compare with AddLife's currency clauses? Do Nordic regional tenders now weight service, sustainability and supply security above price, as Asker's CEO claimed in the Q2 2026 report, and what share of recent awards went to the lowest bidder? What switching costs do system-sales customers face (Asker reports system sales at 79% of Swedish and Norwegian sales in 2024, from 65% in 2019), and have any large Nordic regions switched distributor at renewal since 2022, at what price change? What pricing power does a distributor have in Dutch insurer-contracted homecare, and have the five insurers re-tendered or re-priced diabetes or incontinence supply since 2023? How do upstream manufacturers' list-price changes and rebate structures (retrospective discounts, quotas against parallel trade) constrain a distributor's realised price? What does the sell-side and the expert network say about European medical-consumables price trends to 2030 (a B. Braun executive, 8 October 2025, expected prices flat or under pressure for five years)? How did AddLife and Uniphar price through the 2022 to 2023 inflation and what margin did each retain? Focus: Whether Asker's pricing is purely defensive (cost pass-through that holds margin) or whether a scaled system-sales distributor can extract real price on tender renewal and in insurer channels, and how much of the recent gross-margin expansion is price that can persist against mix that can reverse.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Asker's investment case is a set of linked loops (cash conversion funds acquisitions, acquisitions supply more than half of EBITA growth, leverage against a 2.5x ceiling limits acquisitions, and acquired-company mix lifts gross margin while raising device exposure to OEM direct sales), and the direction of those loops over the next 18 months decides whether the company compounds or stalls. Peers: AddLife AB (ALIF.B-SE), the direct precedent for a Nordic medical distribution acquirer whose loop reversed (net debt to EBITDA peaked at 3.5x at year-end 2023, acquisitions were paused for two years, then resumed in 2025 to 2026 with six deals in twelve months as leverage fell to 2.2 to 2.5x); Vimian Group AB (VIMIAN-SE), a decentralised healthcare acquirer whose Q2 2026 operating cash flow fell to EUR 3.8m on a working-capital build; Lagercrantz Group AB (LAGR.B-SE), Lifco AB and Addtech AB for the self-funded compounding loop working as intended; Storskogen Group AB as the Nordic precedent for a debt-funded roll-up whose loop broke; Uniphar PLC (UPR-IE) for a distributor whose acquisition capacity is set by a 2.5x net-debt covenant and a return-on-capital hurdle. Inputs: Loop 1, cash conversion to acquisition capacity: Asker's operating cash flow averaged about 101% of adjusted EBITA over 2022 to 2024 (Carnegie, 6 May 2025) and the company reported statutory operating cash flow of SEK 92m in Q2 2026 after SEK 238m of contingent-consideration payments through operating cash flow (interim report, 21 July 2026); how have peers' cash conversion and deal pace moved together, quarter by quarter, and what stopped or restarted the loop? Loop 2, leverage to deal pace: Asker reported net debt to adjusted EBITDA of 2.37x at June 2026 against a target below 2.5x, with contingent consideration of SEK 645m excluded from net debt (interim report, 21 July 2026), while AddLife includes earn-outs in its reported leverage; at what leverage did peers slow or stop acquisitions, how long did the pause last, and what happened to organic growth and the share price during it? Loop 3, acquired mix to margin to OEM exposure: Asker's management attributed most of the Q2 2026 gross-margin gain to acquired businesses' higher margins and said advanced products, devices and equipment were about 70% of medtech activity by September 2026 (DNB Carnegie, 1 October 2026); AddLife lost its United Kingdom endoscopy business to the OEM in 2025; how often do OEMs reclaim distribution once a distributor's line passes a scale threshold (an expert formerly at Asker, 4 April 2025, cited SEK 20m to 30m of local sales), and what share of distributors' device revenue has been lost that way over five years? Loop 4, system-sales penetration to retention to share: Asker reports system sales rising from 65% to 79% of Swedish and Norwegian sales between 2019 and 2024 and an 88% re-win rate; does rising penetration raise re-win rates and share of wallet further, or does it saturate? Loop 5, regulation to consolidation to pipeline: MDR and IVDR compliance costs are reported to be driving small distributors and manufacturers to sell or exit (Henry Schein expert, 27 January 2026; Asker 2025 annual report); is the supply of targets rising or falling, at what prices (AddLife disclosed Unicam and Lehnen at under 7x EV/EBITDA, October 2026), and what happens to the pipeline if the European Commission's MDR simplification passes? Loop 6, earn-outs and founder retention: Asker paid SEK 463m of contingent consideration in Q2 2026 and discloses SEK 645m to 1,050m outstanding; what is the record at Asker and peers of founder departures after three-year earn-outs expire, and of organic growth in those subsidiaries afterwards? Loop 7, working capital and acquisitions: the CEO said newly acquired companies typically have lower working-capital efficiency and dilute the group's return on net working capital (Q1 2026 earnings call, 6 May 2026), and inventory days rose from 59 to 65 in 2022 to 82 in Q2 2026 (DNB Carnegie, 10 September 2026); how long does integration take to restore working-capital efficiency at peers? Focus: Which of these loops is currently reinforcing and which is balancing at Asker, with dated evidence from the company and from AddLife's 2023 to 2026 cycle in particular, and what level of cash conversion and leverage would, on peer precedent, keep the acquisition engine running at the company's stated 10 to 12% EBITA contribution without leverage rising.

Request: T-P4
