Why requested: The company guides only on adjusted EBITA growth, margin and leverage, and the inputs that turn adjusted EBITA into earnings per share and free cash flow (capitalised development and its amortisation, acquisition and integration costs, contingent-consideration revaluations and payments, depreciation of the new warehouse, net financial items under floating rates, the tax rate after the shareholder-loan conversion and working-capital movements) are undisclosed, contested by the sell-side and dispersed across brokers, so the earnings-per-share bridge cannot be built from the company's own disclosure. Peers: AddLife AB (ALIF.B-SE), for a like-for-like disclosure of capitalised development, amortisation, earn-outs within leverage and items affecting comparability at a Nordic medical distribution acquirer; Lagercrantz Group AB (LAGR.B-SE), Lifco AB, Addtech AB and Indutrade AB, for the accounting conventions of the Nordic serial-acquirer cohort (which expense development, how they define EBITA, how they treat contingent consideration and put and call options, and typical interest and tax rates); Vimian Group AB (VIMIAN-SE), which capitalised EUR 6.0m of research and development in 2025 and reports items affecting comparability; Uniphar PLC (UPR-IE), which discloses EUR 76.45m of software under construction and separates exceptional dual-running costs. Inputs: Capitalised development and distribution rights: Asker capitalised SEK 109m to 243m a year in 2024 to 2025 (2025 annual report; DNB Carnegie, 10 September 2026) and extended software useful lives from 7.5 to 10 years in 2025; what are the amounts, the useful lives, the amortisation profile and the cash cost, how do peers treat equivalent spend, and what does the sell-side assume (DNB Carnegie assumes SEK 160m to 170m a year of intangible investment through 2028)? Items affecting comparability: SEK 204m in 2025 (SEK 90m acquisition and integration, SEK 96m contingent-consideration revaluation, SEK 18m other) and SEK 141m of acquisition and integration costs over Q1 2024 to Q2 2026; what portion recurs at a company acquiring 10 to 20 businesses a year, on peer evidence, and how do AddLife and Lifco classify the same costs? Provisions: the obsolescence reserve fell from 14.9% of gross inventory in 2021 to 6.5% in 2025 and credit-loss provisions fell while receivables overdue by more than two months rose 170% in 2025 (DNB Carnegie, 10 September 2026); what are peer reserve ratios, what are actual historical write-off rates for distributors to public healthcare, and what would a return to peer ratios cost? Contingent consideration: SEK 645m to 1,050m outstanding, SEK 463m paid in Q2 2026, about SEK 150m guided for 2027 (Q2 2026 earnings call); what are the performance conditions, the payment schedule by year and the historical ratio of paid to initially estimated earn-outs at Asker and peers? Depreciation: the Gothenburg distribution centre (about SEK 300m of capital; DNB Carnegie estimates SEK 10m to 18m of annual depreciation from 2027) and lease depreciation on an enlarged estate; what should total depreciation and amortisation be in 2027 and 2028, and how does FactSet consensus (about SEK 500m a year flat) compare? Net financial items: SEK 350m in 2025 (2025 annual report), a disclosed sensitivity of about SEK 35m per percentage point of interest rates, hedging of 50 to 70% of first-year transaction flows without hedge accounting, and undisclosed covenant levels; what is the debt mix by currency and rate type, what are the facility maturities, and what do brokers assume for 2027 and 2028 interest? Tax: the reported rate moved from 22.6% in 2022 to 33.4% in 2023, 32.8% in 2024 and 22.6% in 2025 (FactSet); what is the normalised rate after the SEK 1,439m shareholder-loan set-off at the March 2025 IPO, and what do brokers assume? Share count: 383.7m shares, incentive-plan dilution capped at about 0.81% across the 2025 and 2026 plans (AGM notice, 30 March 2026); is any acquisition-funded issuance plausible? Working capital: net working capital of SEK 2,767m at June 2026 and return on net working capital of 69%; what is the seasonal pattern of cash collection (AddLife collects about 70% of its annual cash in the fourth quarter, DNB Carnegie, 29 September 2026) and what inventory release is realistic after the Gothenburg move? Definitions: the three brokers visible on FactSet show 2026 adjusted EPS ranging from SEK 2.47 (Nordea, 7 October 2026) to SEK 3.57 (DNB Carnegie, 30 September 2026); which definitions does each use, and which is consistent with the company's adjusted EBITA? Focus: A reconciled bridge, on the company's own disclosure and peer conventions, from adjusted EBITA to reported and adjusted earnings per share and to free cash flow after capitalised development, earn-outs and working capital, for 2025 as reported and for the items that will change in 2026 to 2028, so that the second set's Analyst can decide how much of the gap between adjusted and cash earnings is recurring and which earnings-per-share definition to forecast.

Layer 2

Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

9 questions in 1 group · 13,389 words · written 23 October 2026, 10:48 to 11:08 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Asker Healthcare (ASKER-SE): Second set of questions, translating the foundations into an operating and financial model

Written 2026-10-23 for the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets. Money is in SEK unless stated. "Adjusted EBITA" and "adjusted EBITDA" are the company's own measures (before items affecting comparability and before amortisation of acquired intangibles); "brokers' basis" and "FactSet" figures are from the financials block of 2026-10-22. Last reported period: Q2 2026 (interim report of 21 July 2026); Q3 2026 results are due 4 November 2026.

How to read this set, and where I differ from the first set

The first set's foundations hold: a defensive 4 to 5% organic engine, an acquisition engine that supplies more than half of EBITA growth and is now funded to the edge of the balance sheet, a reported margin that overstates cash earnings by 10 to 15%, and a sell-side picture that is thin and mixes earnings definitions. The four AlphaSense reports written for this set sharpen five things, and on three of them I now think differently from the first set.

  1. The bank covenant is disclosed. The first set said covenant levels were undisclosed. The EPS-bridge report found the March 2025 prospectus states a leverage covenant of net debt to EBITDA of 3.50x. The binding constraint on the acquisition engine is therefore the company's own 2.5x target and its credibility, not the lenders. That lowers the probability of a forced stop and raises the importance of a chosen slowdown (P-02, P-05).
  2. The earnings-per-share definition. The first set's "SEK 3.0 to 3.1 for 2027 on the DNB Carnegie definition" was, on the arithmetic, a cash-adjusted figure (capitalised development and recurring integration cost expensed), not DNB Carnegie's definition, which adds back acquired-intangible amortisation and items affecting comparability without a tax charge. On the basis the Financial Analyst's model computes (adjusted EBITA less net financial items less tax), 2027 EPS is about SEK 3.5; on DNB Carnegie's basis about SEK 3.85; on a cash basis about SEK 3.05; reported about SEK 2.55. The Consumer must carry all four and never compare one with a consensus figure on another (P-08, P-10, and the bridge in the forecast inputs).
  3. Leverage at the end of 2026. The first set's base case was about 2.1x reported. With SEK 1.35bn of acquisition and earn-out cash in 2026, SEK 150m of Gothenburg capital, double inventory and the krona weakening since September (which raises euro debt in kronor), I now expect 2.2 to 2.3x, below the 2.5x ceiling but with less headroom than the first set implied (P-02).
  4. Working capital does not release in the fourth quarter at Asker the way it does at AddLife. FactSet's net working capital rose in every quarter from Q1 2025 (SEK 2,285m) to Q2 2026 (SEK 2,788m), including Q4 2025 (+SEK 48m). The first set's "Q4 2026 cash collection as the first test" is a weaker event than it sounded; the test is the full-year statutory cash flow and the inventory-day count (P-05, P-08).
  5. The tender win rate. The pricing report found the prospectus discloses a total tender win rate of 69% in Sweden, Norway and Finland in 2024, so 31% of tenders bid were lost. The 88% re-win rate applies to renewals; new tenders are won about a third of the time. This is consistent with the first set's picture of a defended installed base, but it caps the organic growth that can come from tender share gain in the Nordics (P-04).

Everything else in the first set stands and is built on below. The base case, in one line: revenue growth 15% in 2026 falling to 10% in 2027 and 9% by 2028 to 2030; adjusted EBITA margin 9.8% in 2026, 10.0% in 2027, 10.4% in 2028 and 10.7% by 2030; adjusted EBITA growth 19%, 12.5%, 14%, 11%, 10%; net financial items flat at about SEK 330m; tax 24%; no buybacks; acquisitions of SEK 1.0 to 1.3bn a year including earn-outs; reported leverage 2.25x at the end of 2026, 2.1x at the end of 2027, 1.85x at the end of 2028; free cash flow before acquisitions of about SEK 0.5bn in 2026, SEK 1.1bn in 2027 and SEK 1.35bn in 2028.

Financial forecasts

C509 questions
P-0152P-0242P-0350P-0446P-0546P-0655P-0842P-0950P-1052

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The forecasts describe a good business compounding at 12 to 14% adjusted EBITA growth over 2027 to 2028 and 10 to 11% after, below its 15% framework and below its own history, with a margin that reaches 10% in 2027 and 10.4% in 2028 on the reported definition and about 1.3 to 1.5 points lower on a cash definition, net financial items that give no help until 2028, EPS that grows about two points slower than EBITA in 2027 and two points faster from 2028, and free cash flow that triples from a 2026 trough to about 56% of EBITA by 2028 while leverage drifts from 2.25x to 1.85x with net debt flat. The operating leverage is modest and margin-driven, the cost opportunity is one logistics project worth 30 basis points, pricing is pass-through and nothing more, and the loops that made the company are throttled by the balance sheet for the next 18 months. The decisive input is free cash flow conversion, and the decisive 18-month events are the FY2026 cash flow and inventory print (February 2027), the FY2026 accounts' treatment of provisions and capitalisation (February to April 2027), and the Q3 2027 report in which Gothenburg first helps. For the Consumer's third set: the revenue and EBITA lines are predictable (cones of plus or minus 2 points and plus or minus 5%), the EPS line is predictable only once its definition is fixed (plus or minus 8%), and the cash line is not (plus or minus 30%), and the skew is negative on EPS and cash and neutral on EBITA. For the fourth set: consensus revenue is right, consensus EBITDA is 2 to 3% too high for 2026 and about right for 2027, consensus D&A and interest are too low, consensus EPS is not on one basis and must be read as growth rates, consensus free cash flow for 2027 is about 20% too high, and the company's framework (15% growth, 10% margin, 2.5x) will be met in 2026, missed narrowly on growth in 2027, and met on margin from 2027. The net effect of these forecasts on the investment case is neutral: the earnings path is sound and slower than the market's framework, and the quality of the earnings improves exactly as their growth rate falls.

P-01

Fixed-cost operating leverage: how it shapes the main line items over 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years

C52

Question asked After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

Judgement: Asker's operating leverage is moderate, is dominated by margin effects rather than volume, and over the next 18 months will look weaker in the reported numbers than it is underneath, before turning visibly positive from the second half of 2027. The model should carry an incremental organic EBITA margin of 12 to 14% on 4.5 to 5% organic sales growth (organic EBITA growth of 6 to 8%), an adjusted EBITA margin that rises about 30 basis points a year on average (9.5% in 2025 to 9.8% in 2026, 10.0% in 2027, 10.4% in 2028, 10.7% by 2030), and an EBITDA margin on the brokers' basis of 12.3%, 12.5%, 12.9%, 13.1% and 13.2% for 2026 to 2030 against 12.0% in 2025. Against the last four to six years, the pace of margin improvement is similar (about 8% in 2023 to 9.5% in 2025) but its composition changes: less from provisioning and acquired mix, more from logistics consolidation, with a dip in the middle.

Reasoning, from the cost structure. On the company's presentation cost of goods is about 59% of sales (gross margin 41.1% in 2025, about 42% in Q2 2026), selling expenses about 25% and administration about 9% (prospectus split of 2024 operating expenses: 64.7% cost of goods, 24.9% selling, 9.4% administration). The 2024 switch to net recognition of third-party-logistics contracts raised the reported gross margin without changing gross profit, which is why FactSet's standardised gross margin (36.1% in 2025) is five points lower; the Consumer should use the company's 41 to 42% for mix analysis and FactSet's for nothing. The genuinely fixed layer is small: central functions (about 45 people) are under 2% of sales, and warehousing and logistics are semi-fixed over a 12 to 24 month horizon. The large layer, sales, technical service and local administration in more than 50 decentralised companies, flexes with volume because local managers are steered on return on net working capital and add staff as they add device and service business. That is why the measured operating leverage is low: H1 2026 produced 7% organic adjusted EBITA growth on 5% organic sales, an incremental organic margin of about 14% against a 9.9% average, a gearing of 1.3 to 1.4x; management's own framework (5 to 8% organic EBITA growth on about 5% organic sales) implies the same. The operating-leverage report confirms the pattern at peers: AddLife's 11% adjusted EBITA growth on 4% organic sales in Q3 2026 came from self-help (Camanio closure, AddVision restructuring, pruning), not from volume gearing, and its Q3 2026 margin actually fell 110 basis points sequentially (11.5% from 12.6%) when Spanish strikes removed volume, which is the downside gearing of the same structure.

Why the leverage is not higher, and whether that is structural. Three mechanisms absorb gross-profit growth before it reaches EBITA. First, like-for-like public tender prices fall 2 to 3% a year (AddLife's disclosure, confirmed by the pricing report), so about half of volume growth is given back in price and must be replaced by mix. Second, the device and homecare mix that lifts gross margin needs more service, installation and sales staff; the CFO's own warning that "the eventual EBITA benefit is smaller than the gross-margin gain" showed in Q2 2026 (gross margin +110 basis points, adjusted EBITA margin +50). Third, revenue per full-time employee fell 20% from SEK 5.45m (2023) to SEK 4.52m (2025); the operating-leverage report shows AddLife at SEK 4.56m and Lifco at SEK 4.16m, so Asker's level is now normal for the cohort and its fall is the arithmetic of buying homecare and device businesses with heavier staffing, plus the Gothenburg pre-hiring, rather than a structural cost problem. I treat the fall as an integration effect that stops in 2027 and reverses slowly, not as a trend to extrapolate.

What the next 18 months look like, line by line. Revenue: 15% growth in 2026 (5% organic, 10 to 11% acquired, about -0.5% currency) and 10% in 2027; the revenue line has almost no cyclical content and the cone is narrow (plus or minus two points). Gross margin: 41.6% in 2026, 41.8% in 2027, 42.0% in 2028 on the company's definition, all from mix. Operating costs as a share of sales: flat to up 10 basis points in H2 2026 and H1 2027 because the Gothenburg distribution centre runs two sites with double manning (management: low tens of millions of kronor per quarter in late 2026 and early 2027; the Uniphar precedent is EUR 20 to 25m of dual-running cost for a larger programme), then down 25 to 30 basis points from H2 2027 as the SEK 50 to 60m of annual savings arrive (Carnegie's own estimate is SEK 50 to 70m, a 17 to 23% return on the SEK 300m of capital). Adjusted EBITA margin by quarter: 9.6% in Q3 2026 (Q3 2025 was 9.3%), 9.8% in Q4 2026 (Q4 2025 10.0%), 9.7% in Q1 2027, 9.9% in Q2 2027, 10.0% in Q3 2027, 10.3% in Q4 2027. The shape is the important forecast: two quarters in which the year-on-year margin gain narrows to 20 to 30 basis points and organic EBITA growth could print at only 5 to 7%, then a step. That is the window in which a "the operating leverage is not there" narrative peaks, and the Consumer should expect it rather than be surprised by it.

The sensitivities the Consumer should carry. One percentage point of organic sales growth moves adjusted EBITA by about SEK 20 to 25m (SEK 0.04 of EPS on the model's basis, about 1.2% of 2027 EPS). Fifty basis points of adjusted EBITA margin moves EBITA by about SEK 105m in 2027 (SEK 0.21 of EPS, about 6%). The margin sensitivity is five times the volume sensitivity, so the EBITA cone is a margin cone: a plausible 18-month range for the 2027 adjusted EBITA margin of 9.5% to 10.5% is a range of about plus or minus 5% on EBITA and plus or minus 6% on EPS; the organic growth range (3% to 6%) adds only plus or minus 2%. Downside operating leverage is limited: healthcare volumes do not fall in a recession (McKesson grew gross profit 9% a year through 2008 to 2010; Cardinal's medical segment expanded margin in fiscal 2010; Indutrade's 2020 margin was a record), and the one precedent of a volume shock (Oriola's "lost quarter" in Q2 2020) reversed within one quarter. The relevant downside is not volume but a mix reversal (an OEM reclaiming a device line) or a provisioning catch-up, both of which are margin events.

Against the last four to six years. From 2019 to 2024 revenue compounded at 27% and adjusted EBITA at 38%, so EBITA grew 1.4 times as fast as sales; the margin rose from about 8% to 9.1% (2024) and 9.5% (2025). About 40 basis points of that came from the obsolescence reserve falling from 14.9% of gross inventory (2021) to 6.5% (2025) and a further amount from credit-loss provisions not keeping pace with overdue receivables (the EPS-bridge report puts each at about SEK 60m, 40 basis points, over 2023 to 2025). Those tailwinds end, and in my base case partly reverse (about SEK 15m of year-end prudence in Q4 2026 and SEK 25m of higher provisioning run-rate in 2027, which costs about 0.2 points of 2027 margin and 2 to 3 points of 2027 EBITA growth). The next four years therefore deliver a similar average margin gain with worse accounting optics in 2026 to 2027 and better ones from 2028, and a revenue growth rate of 9 to 10% rather than 12 to 27%, so EBITA growth of 12 to 14% a year rather than 17 to 38%.

Peers and what differs. AddLife's margin (12.1% in 2025) is 2.5 points above Asker's and the gap is structural: a higher proprietary and diagnostics share, a leaner centre (two people in group management against Asker's 45 central staff and Centres of Excellence), and three years of self-help already done. DNB Carnegie's suggestion, repeated in the operating-leverage report, that Asker could lift margin by cutting central headcount toward AddLife's model is one I reject for the model: central cost is under 2% of sales, a 20% cut would be worth perhaps SEK 50m, and it would weaken financial control at the moment the market is questioning the controls. Uniphar's 6.9% organic gross-profit growth converting to 11% adjusted earnings growth in H1 2026 shows what a scaling platform delivers once dual-running ends; it is the right template for Asker's 2028, not its 2027.

P-02

Financial leverage: how it shapes earnings per share and the balance sheet over 18 months to four years

❌ D42

Question asked After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: financial leverage is high on every measure, is at the company's self-imposed limit and well inside the lenders' limit, and will shape the next four years in two ways that pull in opposite directions. It holds EPS growth about two points below EBITA growth in 2026 to 2027 (net financial items flat at about SEK 330 to 340m while EBITA grows, with no help from rates) and then lets EPS grow about two points faster than EBITA from 2028 as net debt stays flat in absolute terms against a growing EBITDA. More importantly, it governs the acquisition pace: each 0.1x of headroom below the 2.5x target is about SEK 200m of acquisition capacity, about SEK 28m of EBITA and about 1.5 points of EBITA growth, so the leverage line is a growth input, not just a financing cost. Base case: reported net debt to adjusted EBITDA of 2.25x at the end of 2026, 2.1x at the end of 2027, 1.85x at the end of 2028, 1.65x at the end of 2029 and 1.5x at the end of 2030, with absolute net debt flat at SEK 4.7 to 4.9bn throughout and the whole of the deleveraging coming from EBITDA growth.

The facts the model needs, from the EPS-bridge report and the first set. Net debt on the company's definition (excluding leases) was SEK 4,511m at June 2026, 2.37x adjusted EBITDA after lease costs; 2.43x including outstanding acquisition payments; 2.56x on FactSet's lease-inclusive measure (SEK 5,699m); 3.1 to 3.4x on DNB Carnegie's all-in measure (adding SEK 645m to 1,050m of contingent consideration, SEK 127 to 134m of put and call liabilities, and treating capitalised development as expense). The facility, refinanced at the IPO, comprises term loans of SEK 1,025m, EUR 175m and CHF 25m (about SEK 3.2bn) and a SEK 1,600m revolving credit facility; the prospectus discloses a leverage covenant of 3.50x. Interest-rate swaps of SEK 460m and EUR 90m (about SEK 1.5bn) hedge roughly 30% of gross debt of SEK 4,781m; the disclosed sensitivity is SEK 35m of net financial items per percentage point of rates (down from SEK 54.7m in 2024). Net financial items were SEK 350m in 2025 (SEK 407m in 2024), including lease interest of roughly SEK 50m, unwinding of discount on earn-outs and unhedged currency effects, which is why the line is larger than FactSet's net interest of SEK 244m. DNB Carnegie's October model has net financial expenses of SEK 299m (2026), 336m (2027) and 372m (2028) on a 5% average rate with debt drawn for deals; FactSet consensus pre-tax profit implies about SEK 300 to 316m flat. Covenant headroom on the bank's definition is therefore more than one turn; the first set's worry about an undisclosed covenant is resolved, and the constraint is reputational and self-imposed.

How leverage transmits to EPS. On the model's basis (adjusted EBITA less net financial items, taxed at 24%), the interest line consumed 22% of adjusted EBITA in 2025 (SEK 350m of 1,594m), consumes about 17% in 2026 (330 of 1,896) and 16% in 2027 (340 of 2,134), then 13.5% in 2028, 11.5% in 2029 and 10% in 2030. Holding the tax rate at 24%, EPS on that basis grows 23% in 2026, 14% in 2027, 17% in 2028, 13% in 2029 and 11% in 2030 against EBITA growth of 19%, 12.5%, 14%, 11% and 9.5%; the 2026 figure is flattered by the IPO having removed shareholder-loan interest from Q2 2025 onward and by the tax rate falling from 2024's 32.8%. Amplification of operating risk is moderate: a 10% EBITA shortfall is a 12% pre-tax shortfall; a one-point rate rise is SEK 35m, about SEK 0.07 of EPS or 2% (the Riksbank's expected November 2026 hike of 25 basis points is SEK 6m, immaterial, but the floating-rate structure means no relief is coming either). The more important EPS effect is indirect: the interest line stays flat only because net debt stays flat, and net debt stays flat only because I hold acquisition spending to what free cash flow after dividends plus EBITDA growth can carry (SEK 1.0 to 1.3bn a year including earn-outs). If management spent to the 2.5x ceiling instead, net financial items would run SEK 40 to 60m higher by 2028 and EBITA perhaps SEK 100m higher; EPS would be marginally higher and the equity riskier.

Why I now expect 2.2 to 2.3x at the end of 2026 rather than 2.1x. From the June 2026 position of SEK 4,511m, H2 2026 adds about SEK 370m of new deals (management is already discussing targets for Q4 2026 and Q1 2027), about SEK 160m of Gothenburg and other capital spending above the 1% run-rate, double inventory of SEK 50 to 100m at the warehouse, and a translation increase in euro-denominated debt as the krona has weakened since September; against that, H2 free cash flow before acquisitions of about SEK 300m (seasonally stronger EBITDA, but Asker's working capital does not release in the fourth quarter, as the first-set correction above explains). Net debt of about SEK 4.7bn against 2026 adjusted EBITDA after lease costs of about SEK 2.08bn gives 2.25x; the company's own ratio, which includes acquired companies' trailing EBITDA on a pro-forma basis, could print 2.2x. Either way the year ends with 0.2 to 0.3 turns of headroom, not 0.4, and the 2027 acquisition budget is set by cash conversion, not by the ceiling.

The balance sheet over four years. Goodwill and acquired intangibles rise by about SEK 0.9 to 1.1bn a year; equity rises from about SEK 7.3bn (consensus end-2026) toward SEK 9.5bn by 2029 as reported net income (SEK 840m in 2026 rising to SEK 1.4bn in 2029) exceeds dividends (SEK 270m rising to SEK 390m); net debt holds at SEK 4.7 to 4.9bn; lease liabilities rise with the estate (lease depreciation SEK 280m in 2025 toward SEK 320m by 2028 on DNB Carnegie's figures); contingent consideration cycles between SEK 0.6bn and 1.0bn as new deals add and old ones pay out (SEK 463m paid in Q2 2026, about SEK 150m guided for 2027, SEK 200 to 250m a year thereafter on my assumptions). Return on capital employed on the reported basis stays near 8 to 9% because goodwill accumulates faster than EBITA matures; the Consumer should not expect it to reach 12% within the horizon. The equity ratio rises from about 40% toward 45%, which is the slow visible proof the market will want.

Peers and the precedents. AddLife paused acquisitions for two years at 3.5x (year-end 2023) and resumed at 2.2x; Storskogen stopped at 4.35x and needed three years to reach 2.3x; Uniphar is bound by a 2.5x net-debt covenant with half-yearly tests and its leverage jumped from 1.6x to 2.4x in one half-year (H1 2026) on a working-capital unwind, which shows how fast the ratio can move at a distributor; Vimian holds 2.1x against a 3.0x ceiling; Lagercrantz runs at 1.2x. On the cohort's evidence the engine runs freely below about 2.0x, runs with the market's doubt between 2.0x and 2.5x, and stops above 2.5x. Asker will spend all of 2026 and most of 2027 in the middle band. The skew for the next twelve months is slightly negative (the ratio is more likely to disappoint by 0.1 to 0.2 turns on inventory and currency than to beat), and the cone is narrow (2.1x to 2.45x at the end of 2026); the tail that matters is not a covenant event but a disclosed decision to slow the deal pace, which would cut 2027 to 2028 EBITA growth by 3 to 5 points.

P-03

Cost elimination and efficiency: how much reaches the main line items over 18 months to four years, and when

C50

Question asked After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

Judgement: a modest amount, dated, and concentrated in one programme. I expect about SEK 80 to 110m of gross annual cost benefit to reach adjusted EBITA by 2028 (35 to 45 basis points of margin), of which SEK 55 to 60m is the Gothenburg distribution centre (from H2 2027), SEK 20 to 30m is product and category pruning in Central and West, and SEK 10 to 20m is the normalisation of integration cost as the 2024 to 2025 deal cohort matures; against it sit about SEK 30 to 40m of double-running cost in H2 2026 and H1 2027, SEK 10 to 18m of new depreciation from 2027, and SEK 40m of provisioning normalisation (un-releasing provisions that flattered 2023 to 2025). The net cost-side contribution is therefore roughly nil in 2026, slightly negative in H1 2027, about plus 15 basis points in H2 2027, and plus 20 to 30 basis points in 2028 and beyond. A large cost programme is neither available nor consistent with the strategy, and the model should not contain one.

Reasoning on each lever, with timing. (1) Gothenburg. Three buildings consolidate into one 41,000 square metre automated centre (automation level from 35% to 70%), about SEK 300 to 350m of capital of which about SEK 150m falls in 2026, testing since April 2026, fully operational around the turn of the year or in H1 2027, savings of SEK 50 to 60m a year guided from H2 2027 (Carnegie: SEK 50 to 70m). In-year effect: 2026 about -SEK 20 to 25m (double manning in Q3 and Q4), 2027 about +SEK 5 to 10m net (-SEK 15m in H1, +SEK 25 to 30m in H2, less SEK 10 to 15m of depreciation which sits below EBITDA but above EBITA), 2028 about +SEK 40 to 45m net of depreciation, 2029 onward +SEK 45 to 50m. The precedents say the timing risk is real but bounded: Uniphar deferred Greenogue's go-live to February 2027 to avoid the seasonal peak at a cost of EUR 20m of extra capital; Oriola's warehouse-management programme slipped and raised costs (DNB Carnegie cut its target in October 2026). I carry a one-quarter slip as the central case within the ranges above and would treat a two-quarter slip as a plausible downside worth SEK 20 to 30m of 2027 EBITA. (2) Pruning. Central's adjusted EBITA margin rose from 7.8% to 9.2% in a year by exiting low-margin products, and West and Central both show "positive margin trends from product mix improvements" (Q2 2026 report). Another 30 to 50 basis points on Central (18% of sales) and 20 to 30 on West (51%) over 2027 to 2028 is about SEK 25 to 40m, of which I carry SEK 20 to 30m; this is a margin lever that costs sales growth (Central's organic sales growth lagged its acquired growth while pruning), so it is already inside my 4.5 to 5% organic assumption. (3) Integration cost. Items affecting comparability for acquisition and integration ran SEK 90m in 2025 and SEK 141m over Q1 2024 to Q2 2026, but total items affecting comparability fell to SEK 9m in H1 2026; DNB Carnegie's view that SEK 15m a quarter recurs is reasonable, so the "saving" here is only that the adjusted line already excludes it and the reported line sees less of it as the deal cohort ages. I model SEK 60 to 70m a year of items affecting comparability through 2030, flat, which is SEK 20 to 30m below the 2024 to 2025 run-rate. (4) Procurement. Not a lever. The decentralised Nordic acquirers (Addtech, Indutrade, Lagercrantz, Lifco) deliberately forgo central purchasing to protect local motivation; the expert formerly at Asker reported German targets already bought cheaper than the group because German purchasing organisations control up to 90% of local demand; the one counter-example (OEM International, which centralises warehousing in Sweden) is a different model. I carry no purchasing synergy. (5) Central cost. About 45 central employees and the Centres of Excellence; under 2% of sales. DNB Carnegie's suggestion that Asker could run its 50 subsidiaries with fewer central employees on AddLife's two-person model would be worth perhaps SEK 30 to 50m, but AddLife's own 2023 annual report warned that decentralised structures "place high demands on financial reporting" and its former M&A head said the lack of a central M&A function made growth harder; Asker, with no internal audit function and a public earnings-quality challenge, needs more control resource, not less. I carry a small increase in central cost (SEK 20 to 30m for internal audit and reporting controls by 2027) rather than a cut. (6) Warehouse automation and artificial intelligence beyond Gothenburg. Morgan Stanley's estimate that artificial intelligence could cut third-party-logistics staff cost by 10% (about 180 basis points of logistics margin) applies to a logistics pure play; at Asker warehouse and logistics wages are perhaps SEK 600 to 800m, so 10% is SEK 60 to 80m (30 to 40 basis points) over five years, and the same note warns such gains are "frequently competed away" in tender pricing while only 11% of customers prioritise such tools when choosing a distributor. Nothing before 2029 in the base case; SEK 20 to 30m retained by 2030 as an upside.

What this means for the model's lines, by year. 2026: cost actions net about -SEK 20m on EBITA (Gothenburg double running, offset by pruning); the adjusted EBITA margin gain of 30 basis points over 2025 is all mix and acquired-company margins. 2027: net about +SEK 5 to 15m; the margin gain of 20 basis points is mix (+30) and Gothenburg H2 (+13) less double running (-10) and provisioning normalisation (-12). 2028: net about +SEK 70 to 90m; the margin gain of 40 basis points is Gothenburg full year (+15), pruning (+10) and mix (+20), with no further provisioning drag. 2029 to 2030: +10 to 15 basis points a year, mostly mix. Below EBITA, the new depreciation (SEK 10 to 18m) and rising lease depreciation (toward SEK 320m) are already inside my depreciation and amortisation line (SEK 485m in 2026 rising to SEK 690m by 2030, which is 2.5% of sales throughout and SEK 30 to 60m a year above FactSet's flat SEK 500m).

The peer precedent and what it says about the ceiling. AddLife's self-help took its margin from 10.5% (2023) to 12.1% (2025) through ten consecutive quarters, but it required SEK 134m of write-downs and one-off costs in Q4 2023, a closed business (Camanio, SEK 60m of annual losses eliminated), a restructured one (AddVision, SEK 20m of savings), a divestment (UK endoscopy, SEK 158m of income) and a dismantled centre; none of those exists at Asker, which has no loss-making digital unit to close and no central function to dismantle. The honest reading is that Asker's cost opportunity is one logistics project plus ordinary pruning, worth 35 to 45 basis points gross, and that the AddLife 160 basis point precedent is not available without a different kind of restructuring. The timing is the useful thing for the third and fourth sets: Q3 2026 to Q2 2027 is the cost trough, Q3 2027 is the first quarter in which the cost line helps, and 2028 is the first clean year.

Asker Healthcare (ASKER-SE): Second set of questions (continued)

P-04

Pricing power: how price contributes to revenue and margin over 18 months to four years

❌ D46

Question asked After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: price contributes nominal growth of roughly 1 to 2% a year to revenue (inflation pass-through on indexed public contracts and annual direct-to-patient renewals) and nothing to real margin; the gross margin's path over the next four years is a mix path, and the model should hold the price-driven component of gross margin at zero. Concretely: company-definition gross margin of 41.6% in 2026, 41.8% in 2027, 42.0% in 2028 and 42.2% by 2030, from 41.1% in 2025, all of it from acquired-company mix (devices, homecare, private label) and pruning, with like-for-like price falling 2 to 3% a year on public tender lines and rising with inflation elsewhere. Pricing power is defensive and demonstrated (margin held through 2021 to 2023 inflation), offensive and undemonstrated, and the pricing report's search for evidence of real price on renewal found none at Asker, at AddLife or at Uniphar.

Reasoning, condition by condition, from the pricing report. Inflation pass-through: management's "floating as a cork" (Q4 2025 call) and the CFO's "no supply-chain margin squeeze" (Q1 2026) are supported by the record (gross margin held at about 41% through 2022 to 2025) and by AddLife's July 2023 statement that supplier increases were "largely offset by price increases to customers"; the mechanism is indexation in Nordic public contracts with a lag of about three months on raw materials (an Amcor executive) and six to nine months in medtech more broadly (Jefferies), and the indices "rarely cover related cost increases in freight, energy and localised labour" (Rabobank). So a renewed inflation burst would compress gross margin by 30 to 50 basis points for two to three quarters before recovery, and a renewed deflation (resin prices have been falling) would give a small temporary gain. Price-led tenders: the B. Braun executive's figure that over 55% of European procurement processes focus mainly on price stands, and the prospectus discloses a total tender win rate of 69% in 2024 (so 31% lost), which means new volume is won at competitive prices; the 88% re-win rate protects installed volume, not installed price. Procurement centralisation: Sykehusinnkjøp buys about EUR 5bn a year for Norway's four regional authorities and is piloting the PAS 2090 environmental standard, which favours large compliant suppliers in scoring but raises buyer leverage on price; DNB Carnegie's reading that ESG credentials "strengthen brand loyalty in public procurement" is a retention effect, not a price effect. Dutch insurers: five insurers set terms, contracts are typically one year with extension options, and the report found no public evidence of re-tendering or re-pricing of diabetes or incontinence supply since 2023; it rated this the lowest-confidence channel in the whole analysis. Germany: the reform caps hospital reimbursement growth at wage growth less one point for 2027 to 2029 and cuts laboratory fees; purchasing organisations control up to 90% of hospital demand; DNB Carnegie notes reimbursement cuts are shared along the chain (distributor margin, efficiencies, manufacturer concessions), so the distributor absorbs a third to a half. Upstream: Coloplast's distributor agreements "involve significant volume discounts and rebates", retrospective rebates are designed to stop distributors quoting low (an expert on parallel trade), and Coloplast and Convatec have bought direct-to-patient distributors (Comfort Medical, 180 Medical) to secure flow; Mölnlycke manages price actively to hold its own gross margin high, which creates a price umbrella for distributors of premium lines. Long-run trend: a B. Braun executive expects European consumable prices flat or under pressure for five years; Fischer Medical notes buying power shifting from clinical to economic buyers.

What the 110 basis points in Q2 2026 were. Management attributed the gain to portfolio mix first (larger equipment carries higher gross margin), then cost-of-goods work and supplier relationships, then "pricing"; private label is about 10% of revenue and "not being driven particularly hard" (Q2 2026 call); DNB Carnegie's reading is that acquisitions contributed about 50 basis points to the adjusted EBITA margin and that obsolescence-reserve releases added about 40 basis points to gross margin over 2023 to 2025 cumulatively. My decomposition of the year-on-year 110 basis points: 60 to 70 acquired mix, 20 to 30 organic mix and pruning, 10 to 15 provisioning, 0 to 10 price. The provisioning part reverses; the acquired-mix part persists while the deals keep coming and fades as they annualise; the organic-mix part persists. That is why the gross margin rises only 50 basis points in 2026 and 20 a year after, not 110 a year.

Contribution to the model's lines. Revenue: nominal price of about +1.5% a year inside my 4.5 to 5% organic growth (volume and mix about +3 to 3.5%); in Germany and Belgium, price of about -1% a year on reimbursed lines from 2027. Gross margin: as above, 41.6% to 42.2%, with the contribution from price nil. Adjusted EBITA margin: the pass-through protects it (no inflation squeeze in the base case); the pruning contributes 10 to 15 basis points a year at the EBITA line; the device mix contributes at gross level but is partly consumed by service staff. Currency: a 1% move in the krona changes earnings by about SEK 3.7m; transaction flows are hedged 50 to 70% in year one without hedge accounting, so the effect comes through net financial items and gross margin with a lag; the krona's weakening since September 2026 is a small reported tailwind for H2 2026 (perhaps +SEK 10 to 15m of EBITA) that I expect to reverse in 2027 if the krona strengthens toward EUR/SEK 10.40 to 10.60 as the house view holds; I carry currency at about -0.5% of reported sales in 2026, -1.5% in 2027 and -0.5% in 2028. Payment terms: the Commonwealth Bank precedent of Southern European hospitals stretching to over a year is the risk that matters as Asker enters France, Spain and Italy; Asker's overdue receivables already rose 170% in 2025 while provisions fell, so the model should expect receivable days to drift up one to two days a year with geographic mix and should not expect the Dutch or Nordic payer base to tighten.

Where the cone is wider than it looks. The pricing report could not decompose organic pricing from mix because the company gives no price-volume bridge; the Dutch insurer channel is a black box; and German contract terms are undisclosed. The skew over twelve months is mildly negative at the gross-margin line (provision reversal, German cap, krona reversal) and neutral at the EBITA line (mix offsets). A real price lever would require two things the evidence does not show: tender scoring that weights service above price in a majority of awards, and renewal pricing above indexation in system-sales contracts. The T-D1 request asks for both. Until they are shown, the gross margin is a mix forecast and nothing more, and the model's gross-margin cone should be plus or minus 40 basis points around my path.

Peers. AddLife's gross margin (38.1% in 2025) rose in Q2 2026 on "price management and advanced products"; Uniphar's medtech division holds a 41.2% gross margin while its wholesale division holds 9.8%, which is the clearest demonstration that gross margin in this sector is product mix, not pricing behaviour; Cardinal Health recovered its medical segment through contract amendments to "mitigate one hundred percent of inflation" exiting fiscal 2024 after two years of squeeze, and Owens & Minor could pass costs "on a limited basis" only; the 2016 generic-deflation shock at the US drug wholesalers (Cardinal's May 2016 warning) is the precedent for what consolidated buyers do to distributor pricing when they get maximum leverage. Asker's structure is closer to Cardinal's medical segment than to Owens & Minor's because of indexation and system sales, and nothing like the 2016 wholesaler shock is visible in European public procurement; but the German 2027 to 2029 cap is the nearest thing to it and lands inside the model horizon.

P-05

Reinforcing and balancing feedback loops: which are at work, and how they shape the main line items

❌ D46

Question asked After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: at Asker today the balancing loops dominate and will do so for 12 to 18 months; the reinforcing loops are intact but throttled, and they re-engage from the second half of 2027 only if free cash flow before acquisitions reaches about 55 to 60% of adjusted EBITA (SEK 1.1 to 1.2bn in 2027). The loop that governs everything is the leverage-to-deal-pace loop: at 2.37x against a 2.5x ceiling, the acquired contribution to EBITA growth falls from about 11% in 2026 (deals already done) to about 7% in 2027 and 6 to 7% in 2028 to 2030 in my base case, below management's 10 to 12% target and above DNB Carnegie's 7% only in 2028 and beyond. The peer precedent is unambiguous on the threshold: the engine runs freely below 2.0x and stops above 2.5x. For the model: acquired sales growth of 10 to 11% in 2026, 7% in 2027, 6 to 7% thereafter; organic 5% in 2026 and 4.5 to 5% after; adjusted EBITA margin up 25 to 30 basis points a year; free cash flow conversion improving from about 25% of EBITA in 2026 (on the model's convention) to 50% in 2027 and 55 to 57% from 2028.

Loop by loop, with the dated evidence and the modelled effect.

Loop 1, cash conversion to acquisition capacity (weakening reinforcing loop). Operating cash flow was about 101% of adjusted EBITA over 2022 to 2024 (Carnegie), about 83% in 2025, about 100% in Q1 2026 and 20% statutory in Q2 2026 (SEK 92m) or 70% after adding back the SEK 238m of earn-outs routed through operating activities; the company's "adjusted" operating cash flow was SEK 330m in Q2 2026 against 211m a year earlier. The first set was right to make cash conversion the governing input. What the feedback-loop report adds is the peer rhythm: Vimian's operating cash flow fell to EUR 3.8m in Q2 2026 on a receivables build (net working capital 26% of revenue) and management promised normalisation in Q3; AddLife's Q1 2026 operating cash flow fell 56% to SEK 104m, then Q4 is the collection quarter (SEK 888m in Q4 2025 on a SEK 426m working-capital release); Addtech funds 15 to 20% earnings growth from its own cash at 0.7x net debt to equity. Asker, as the first-set correction above shows, has no fourth-quarter release of its own: net working capital rose in every quarter from Q1 2025 to Q2 2026, by SEK 503m in total, roughly in line with sales (so return on net working capital held at 67 to 69%) but with inventory days rising from 75 to 82. The loop will reinforce again only when inventory days fall; I model 82 at Q2 2026, about 80 at the end of 2026 (double stock), 76 at the end of 2027 and 72 at the end of 2028, which releases about SEK 150 to 200m of cash in 2027 and again in 2028 relative to a flat-days case. Working capital as a share of revenue on FactSet's definition: 14.8% at the end of 2025, 15.3% at the end of 2026, 14.8% in 2027, 14.3% in 2028, 14.0% thereafter.

Loop 2, leverage to deal pace (the binding balancing loop). 2.37x reported at June 2026; the company's own alternative measure including outstanding considerations was 2.43x; DNB Carnegie's all-in 3.1 to 3.4x; the bank covenant 3.5x. AddLife's leverage peaked at 3.5x at the end of 2023, acquisitions paused for about two years, organic growth became "the sole driver of earnings performance" and the share price de-rated to a 26% discount, then four acquisitions followed from late 2025 at 2.2x and six in twelve months; Storskogen peaked at 4.35x (November 2022), halted deals for two years and restarted selectively at 2.3x (Q4 2025); Uniphar's covenant is 2.5x with half-yearly tests. The modelled consequence is the acquisition budget: SEK 1.35bn in 2026 (SEK 0.9bn of deals plus SEK 0.46bn of earn-outs, mostly already spent), SEK 1.1bn in 2027 (SEK 0.95bn of deals plus SEK 0.15bn of earn-outs), SEK 1.2bn in 2028, SEK 1.25bn in 2029, SEK 1.3bn in 2030. At 7x EBITA that buys SEK 130 to 150m of EBITA a year, 6 to 7% of the prior year's EBITA, with in-year timing taking the reported contribution to about 7% in 2027 (helped by H2 2026 deals annualising) and 6 to 7% thereafter. Management's 10 to 12% would need SEK 1.5 to 1.9bn a year, which my cash flows do not fund without leverage rising; the loop therefore caps growth at about 12 to 14% adjusted EBITA growth rather than 15% plus, which is the single largest difference between my model and the company's framework.

Loop 3, acquired mix to margin to OEM exposure (reinforcing on margin, balancing on risk). Acquisitions are cherry-picked above a 10% EBITA margin (Q1 2026 call), contributed a 17% margin in H1 2026 on consolidation, and DNB Carnegie expects mix to add about 30 basis points a year; about 70% of medtech activity is now advanced products, devices and equipment. The balancing side is dated and quantified: AddLife's loss of its UK endoscopy line to the OEM cost SEK 120m of organic sales in the first nine months of 2026 (about 1.5% of group sales) and SEK 140m a year, although it produced SEK 158m of divestment income; the expert formerly at Asker put the trigger at SEK 20 to 30m of local sales in a line. I carry no loss in the base case and a named downside of 1 to 2% of sales and 20 to 40 basis points of margin from a device-line loss in any one year, with a probability of perhaps 10 to 15% a year of a material one.

Loop 4, system sales to retention (reinforcing, saturating). Penetration 65% to 79% of Swedish and Norwegian sales (2019 to 2024; the pricing report cites 86% in the 2024 annual report on a wider definition), 88% re-win, 69% total win. The loop still runs in the Nordics, where it adds share of wallet without new tenders, but at 80 to 86% penetration it is close to its ceiling; its growth contribution is now in West and Central (fourth-party logistics in Benelux; the Dutch platform's extension to Germany through GHC/MPF), where the evidence of penetration is not disclosed. Modelled effect: Nordic organic growth of 4 to 5% from 2027 (10% in Q2 2026 was comparison-driven), West 5 to 6%, Central 4 to 5% with pruning.

Loop 5, regulation to consolidation to pipeline (reinforcing, with a dated risk). MDR documentation and post-market surveillance burden small players (Morgan Stanley, October 2026); AddLife paid under 7x for Lehnen and Unicam (October 2026), below its historical average, so target prices have not risen; the European Commission's MDR simplification proposal (Bernstein, April 2026) could let small distributors stay independent; and a Henry Schein executive reports some manufacturers exiting Europe altogether, which would shrink the product range rather than the target pool. Modelled effect: deal multiples held at 6 to 8x through 2030; deal supply not a constraint; the thinning of professionalised targets above EUR 50m of revenue shifts the mix toward more, smaller deals (under EUR 15m of sales) from 2027, which raises integration cost per krona and is one reason I hold items affecting comparability at SEK 60 to 70m a year rather than letting them fall further.

Loop 6, earn-outs and founder retention (balancing, undisclosed). Contingent consideration SEK 645m at June 2026 (SEK 1,050m at March 2026); SEK 463m paid in Q2 2026, mostly for 2022 to 2023 deals; about SEK 150m guided for 2027. The Lifco acquisitions expert's observation that a departing multi-skilled founder is often replaced by two or three specialised managers, raising opex and diluting the subsidiary's margin, is the mechanism; Asker discloses nothing on founder retention. The 2023 deal cohort (several large ones) reaches the end of three-year earn-outs in 2026 to 2027. Modelled effect: a 10 to 15 basis point annual drag on acquired-company margins from 2027 inside my mix assumption, and a named risk rather than a base-case item.

Loop 7, working capital dilution from acquisitions (balancing, temporary). The CEO's statement that acquired companies typically have lower working-capital efficiency; inventory days 59 to 65 (2022) to 82 (Q2 2026); AddLife restored return on working capital to 61 to 63% (target 45%) through structured inventory and payment-term programmes; DNB Carnegie's view that restoration "requires significant integration time". Modelled as the inventory-day path above; note for the Consumer that the model's working-capital line will treat the working capital of acquired companies as an operating outflow (about SEK 150 to 200m a year at 15% of acquired sales) when in reality it sits inside the acquisition price; my cross-check free cash flow in the forecast inputs follows the model's convention and I give the economic figure beside it.

The threshold question, answered. On the peer precedent (AddLife needs about 95% cash conversion and six to eight deals a year to hold 2.5x through its 2027 refinancing; Addtech self-funds 15 to 20% growth at 0.7x net debt to equity), keeping Asker's engine at the 10 to 12% EBITA contribution without leverage rising needs free cash flow before acquisitions and dividends of about 75 to 80% of adjusted EBITA (SEK 1.6 to 1.7bn in 2027), or operating cash flow after interest and tax of roughly 95% of EBITA. My base case has about 50 to 55% in 2027 (SEK 1.1 to 1.2bn), which funds about 7%. The gap is the Gothenburg capital tail, SEK 190m of capitalised development, earn-outs, and inventory; two of those four fade in 2028, which is why the loop can re-engage then. The indicators that decide it, in order of timing: inventory days at the Q4 2026 report (below 80 is the first good sign), statutory operating cash flow for FY2026 above SEK 1.3bn before earn-outs, the 2027 acquisition announcements running at SEK 200 to 250m a quarter, and reported leverage printing at or below 2.2x at the end of 2027.

P-06

Track record of delivery: how much weight the forecasts should carry, and which way to lean them

C55

Question asked After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

Judgement: the forecasts for the next 18 months deserve high weight on revenue, medium weight on adjusted EBITA and low weight on everything below it and on cash; and they should be leaned up on EBITA against consensus growth for 2027, down on EPS against any consensus figure on a comparable basis, and down on free cash flow and leverage against both consensus and the company's presentation. Over four years the weight falls on all lines because the acquired component (half or more of growth) is a decision made each year rather than a trend, and because the accounting basis is likely to change in the direction of prudence. The company's record is "delivers what it targets, and targets what it can deliver" at the EBITA line, and "the cash line surprises" below it.

The record, as it bears on each line. Revenue: no guidance, but the two engines are observable; organic growth has printed 6, 3, 5, -1, 5, 5% over the last six quarters, with the two outliers explained by the 2024 defence orders, and acquired growth has run 12 to 13% for a year with the deal list public. Weight: high; cone plus or minus two points on the year. Adjusted EBITA: beats of 4 to 7% against broker estimates in four of the last six quarters, one in line, one 2% short (Q2 2025); 17% growth in 2025 and 21% rolling to Q2 2026 against a 15% floor; Gothenburg costs flagged a year ahead. Weight: medium-high on the next two quarters (DNB Carnegie's 19% for Q3 2026 is consistent with my SEK 456m), medium on 2027 because of the provisioning question. Direction of lean: up on 2027 growth (my 12.5% against consensus EBITDA growth of about 10%), but not on the 2027 level, because my 2026 base is 2 to 3% below consensus EBITDA (Gothenburg, year-end provisioning); the two roughly cancel, so my 2027 adjusted EBITDA of SEK 2.67bn sits 1% below the FactSet median of 2,684m and my adjusted EBITA of about SEK 2.13bn sits in line with Bloomberg's 2.1bn. Below EBITA: the company guides nothing, the EPS consensus spans 45% across three brokers on definitional differences, DNB Carnegie's own model has net financial items rising from SEK 299m to 372m by 2028 while FactSet's consensus implies flat; depreciation is held flat by consensus when Gothenburg and lease growth say otherwise. Weight: low; lean down against the FactSet mean on a like-for-like definition by 5 to 10% for 2027 to 2028 (interest and depreciation), while recognising that on the model's own basis my EPS prints above the FactSet median because the median mixes cash-basis brokers in (see P-08). Cash: operating cash conversion 101% (2022 to 2024), 83% (2025), 20% statutory in Q2 2026; inventory days rising for four years; FactSet consensus free cash flow of SEK 1,262m for 2027 is the upper bound. Weight: low; lean down by 15 to 20% against consensus for 2027 and treat the 2026 figure as a Gothenburg-and-earn-out trough.

Why the EBITA record transfers only partly to the forecasts. Three of the drivers behind the record do not recur at the same strength: the obsolescence and credit provisions that added 40 basis points each over 2023 to 2025 have reached a floor and under a new Chair will more likely be rebuilt than released; items affecting comparability of 11 to 13% of EBITA in 2024 to 2025 (which the sell-side has now priced as partly recurring) fell to under 1% in H1 2026 and cannot fall further; and the 2024 to 2025 acquisition cohort, which supplied 12 to 13 points of sales growth, annualises through H1 2027 while the 2026 to 2027 cohort is capital-constrained. The fourth driver, organic growth at 4.5 to 5%, does recur, and it is the one the record proves best. So the forecast's weight should be apportioned as: the organic engine as trend, the acquired engine as a capital-allocation decision each year with a budget set by cash conversion, the margin as mix plus Gothenburg less normalisation, and the below-EBITA lines from first principles.

Comparable conditions and the peer read. Asker grew through the 2023 to 2024 rate rise with 100% cash conversion; it is now in a similar rate regime with about half that conversion, which is the one deterioration against comparable conditions and the reason the lean on cash is down. Lagercrantz's streak of 15 years of EPS growth ended on a 3 to 5% EBITA miss in Q1 2026/27 and the multiple followed; AddLife's record of beats over 2024 to 2026 was met with a downgrade to Hold on 22 October 2026 "after a strong run" because the beats were priced. Asker's position is the opposite: beats are not priced (the shares sit at the IPO price and the 9th percentile of their own multiple history), so an in-line adjusted EBITA print with a clean cash line would be rewarded and a beat with a weak cash line would not. For the third set's purposes: the predictability of the EBITA line over 18 months is good (my cone for 2027 adjusted EBITA is SEK 2,020 to 2,250m, plus or minus 5%), the predictability of EPS is moderate (plus or minus 8 to 10%, definition-dependent), and the predictability of free cash flow is poor (plus or minus 30%).

The weight over four years. By 2029 to 2030 the forecasts rest on an acquisition budget that compounds at SEK 1.25 to 1.3bn a year, a margin at 10.6 to 10.7%, and leverage near 1.5x; each is reasonable and none is demonstrated at this scale. I would give the 2029 to 2030 lines half the weight of the 2027 lines, treat the revenue growth of 8.5 to 9% as the most reliable element (because even a slower deal pace plus 4.5% organic produces 8%), and the EPS of SEK 4.70 to 5.20 as a central path with a cone of plus or minus 15%. The lean over four years is the same as over 18 months: EBITA roughly at the framework's floor (I have 10 to 11% in 2029 to 2030 against the 15% target, because the acquired contribution runs at 6 to 7%), EPS and cash below the company's presentation and the consensus extrapolation.

Asker Healthcare (ASKER-SE): Second set of questions (continued)

P-08

Below-operating-profit items and share count: how they shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years

❌ D42

Question asked After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement: the items between adjusted EBITA and earnings per share leak more at Asker than at any peer in the cohort, the leak is at its widest now and narrows slowly, and it must be modelled on four explicitly named bases because the market mixes them. On the basis the Financial Analyst's model computes (adjusted EBITA less net financial items, taxed), EPS is SEK 3.10 in 2026, 3.54 in 2027, 4.14 in 2028, 4.70 in 2029 and 5.22 in 2030 (SEK 2.51 in 2025 on the same basis). On the reported basis it is SEK 2.19, 2.55, 3.12, 3.63 and 4.12 (SEK 1.28 in 2025 on FactSet's reported net income over 383m shares). On DNB Carnegie's add-back basis about SEK 3.40, 3.85, 4.45, 5.05 and 5.60. On a cash basis (model basis less after-tax capitalised development and recurring integration cost) about SEK 2.60, 3.05, 3.65, 4.20 and 4.70. Free cash flow before acquisitions, earn-outs and dividends, on the model's convention (full working-capital change deducted), is about SEK 480m in 2026, 1,070m in 2027, 1,350m in 2028, 1,540m in 2029 and 1,670m in 2030, against a true figure of about SEK 450 to 500m in 2025 and SEK 400 to 800m in 2022 to 2024 once intangibles, leases and earn-outs are deducted from FactSet's SEK 914 to 1,069m.

The items, one by one, with what changes. (1) Amortisation of acquired intangibles: about SEK 341m in 2025 (DNB Carnegie's add-back), rising to about SEK 400m in 2026, 430m in 2027 and 490m by 2030 as each year's deals add customer relationships and brands; legitimately excluded from adjusted EBITA and from the model's EPS, and the reason reported EPS is 30% below the model's. (2) Items affecting comparability: SEK 204m in 2025 (SEK 90m acquisition and integration, SEK 96m contingent-consideration revaluation, SEK 18m mainly IPO), SEK 9m in H1 2026; I model SEK 60 to 70m a year, of which about half integration cost (DNB Carnegie's SEK 15m a quarter is the right order) and half earn-out revaluation, which is non-cash and can go either way (strong acquired performance raises the liability and charges the income statement). The EPS-bridge report's peer benchmarking is the useful addition: Lifco books all acquisition costs (SEK 45m in 2025) in administrative expenses and defines EBITA before acquisition-intangible amortisation only; AddLife books transaction costs in selling expenses and adjusts only for the remeasurement of contingent consideration; very few compounders adjust integration cost at all. If Asker moved to the AddLife convention, adjusted EBITA would fall by about SEK 30 to 35m a year (0.15 points of margin) with no cash effect; I do not model the change but the fourth set should know it is the most likely form a "cleaner" presentation would take. (3) Depreciation and amortisation other than acquired intangibles: SEK 421m in 2025 (the gap between adjusted EBITDA of 2,015m and adjusted EBITA of 1,594m), comprising lease depreciation of about SEK 280m, tangible depreciation and the amortisation of capitalised software (whose useful life was extended from 7.5 to 10 years in 2025, lowering the charge by SEK 80 to 85m according to DNB Carnegie). I model SEK 485m in 2026, 535m in 2027, 585m in 2028, 637m in 2029 and 691m in 2030, about 2.5% of sales throughout, which includes Gothenburg's SEK 10 to 18m from 2027 and lease depreciation rising toward SEK 320m; FactSet consensus holds total D&A at about SEK 500m flat and is SEK 30 to 60m a year too low on this line. (4) Net financial items: SEK 350m in 2025; I model SEK 330m in 2026, 340m in 2027, 330m in 2028, 315m in 2029 and 300m in 2030 (P-02). (5) Tax: 22.6% in 2025, 33% in 2023 to 2024 on non-deductible shareholder-loan interest, now removed by the IPO set-off; Carnegie's normalised 25%, DNB Carnegie's 22 to 24% peer range, a mix drift toward Germany and France; I model 24%. (6) Minorities: SEK 41m on the balance sheet, immaterial; put and call options over minority stakes of SEK 127 to 134m sit outside reported net debt and are settled in cash within the acquisition line of my model. (7) Share count: 383.7m diluted; incentive dilution capped at about 0.81% across the 2025 and 2026 plans; I add 0.4m shares in 2026, 0.8m in 2027 and 2028, 0.6m in 2029 and 0.5m in 2030; no acquisition-funded issuance (management funds deals from cash and debt; Interogo's 40.2% and the IPO at SEK 70 argue against a discounted issue, and the shares at 16x EV/EBITA are not an attractive currency against 7x targets). (8) Buybacks: none through 2030; the arithmetic (private companies at 7x against the public share at 16x) and the leverage both argue against. (9) Dividend: above 30% of net profit; SEK 0.39 for 2025 (30% of reported EPS); I model SEK 0.70 for 2026, 0.85 for 2027, 1.00 for 2028, 1.15 for 2029 and 1.30 for 2030 (32 to 33% of reported EPS), paid in the following year; consensus has SEK 0.725 and 0.86. (10) Capitalised development and distribution rights: SEK 94m of development in 2025 (85m in 2024) plus SEK 173m of trademarks, patents and distribution rights over 2024 to 2025 (about SEK 100m of it a one-off purchase of Baltic orthopaedic distribution rights); DNB Carnegie assumes SEK 160 to 170m a year through 2028; I model SEK 180 to 220m a year inside capital spending, which is the cash cost of the "adjusted" presentation and the reason the cash-basis EPS is SEK 0.45 to 0.50 below the model's. (11) Earn-outs: SEK 463m paid in Q2 2026, about SEK 150m guided for 2027, SEK 200 to 250m a year thereafter as the 2025 to 2027 deal cohorts mature; inside my acquisition line, and to be reversed out of the company's "adjusted" operating cash flow whenever that figure is quoted. (12) Provisions: the obsolescence reserve at 6.5% of gross inventory (14.9% in 2021) and credit-loss provisions that fell while receivables overdue by more than two months rose 170% to over 3% of revenue (1.5% in 2022 to 2024); peers hold much higher allowances (Lifco 5% of receivables) but actual write-offs at distributors to public healthcare are negligible (AddLife SEK 0 in 2025; Lifco SEK 16m on SEK 3.6bn). I model SEK 15m of year-end prudence in Q4 2026 and SEK 25m of higher run-rate in 2027, not the full SEK 120m catch-up DNB Carnegie computes, because the public-payer credit record justifies lower allowances than Lifco's industrial book and because an auditor-driven catch-up is less likely than a management-chosen gradual one.

The bridge for 2026, so the Consumer can see the leak. Adjusted EBITA SEK 1,896m; less items affecting comparability SEK 60m; less amortisation of acquired intangibles SEK 400m; reported EBIT about SEK 1,436m; less net financial items SEK 330m; pre-tax SEK 1,106m; tax at 24% SEK 265m; reported net income about SEK 840m, 44% of adjusted EBITA (31% in 2025). On the model's basis: adjusted EBITA 1,896 less net financial items 330 = 1,566, taxed at 24% = 1,190, over 384.1m shares = SEK 3.10. The gap between SEK 3.10 and the reported SEK 2.19 is SEK 0.91, of which SEK 0.79 is acquired-intangible amortisation after tax and SEK 0.12 items affecting comparability after tax; the gap between SEK 3.10 and a cash SEK 2.60 is capitalised development and recurring integration cost after tax. By 2030 the reported figure reaches 54% of adjusted EBITA (SEK 1.6bn of 2.96bn) as the interest share falls and the amortisation share stabilises.

Free cash flow, and against the last four to six years. My definition: adjusted EBITDA, less cash interest (net financial items), less cash tax (at 24% on reported pre-tax profit), less the change in working capital, less capital spending including intangibles, less lease principal; before acquisitions, earn-outs and dividends. 2026: 2,381 less 330 less 270 less 478 less 520 less 300 = about SEK 480m (about 25% of adjusted EBITA; about SEK 660m or 35% if the SEK 180m of working capital that arrives with acquired companies is treated as part of the acquisition price, which is the economic truth). 2027: 2,669 less 340 less 310 less 199 less 435 less 315 = about SEK 1,070m (50% of EBITA; about 55% economic). 2028: SEK 1,350m (56%). 2029: SEK 1,540m (57%). 2030: SEK 1,670m (57%). Consensus free cash flow on FactSet's definition (before intangibles, leases and earn-outs) is SEK 805m, 1,262m and 1,388m for 2026 to 2028; on a like-for-like definition mine is below it by about 20% in 2027 and 10% in 2028, as the first set anticipated. Against 2022 to 2024, when FactSet free cash flow was SEK 914 to 1,069m with capex at 1% of sales and before earn-outs became large, the true figure was SEK 600 to 800m; so the 2027 to 2028 path is a genuine improvement, driven by the end of Gothenburg capital (+SEK 300m against 2025), earn-outs normalising (+SEK 300m against 2026) and inventory days falling (+SEK 150 to 200m a year), and not by anything heroic. The sources of risk to it are, in order: inventory (each five days is about SEK 220m), earn-out timing, and a provisioning catch-up that would be non-cash but would alarm the market.

How the Consumer should use the four EPS bases against consensus. The FactSet median (SEK 2.79 for 2026, 3.29 for 2027, 3.77 for 2028) and mean (2.82, 3.25, 3.68) blend brokers that expense capitalised development (Nordea: 2.47, 3.13, 3.75) with brokers that add back everything untaxed (DNB Carnegie: 3.57, 3.81, 4.01) and others between (the restricted broker: 3.05, 3.70, 4.29); the FactSet actual for 2025 on the brokers' basis is SEK 2.31, below the model's-basis actual of SEK 2.51. So the model's output will print above the FactSet median by about 10% in every year, and that is a definitional artefact, not a view. The like-for-like comparisons are: growth rates (mine 23%, 14%, 17% for 2026 to 2028 against consensus 21%, 18%, 15%), DNB Carnegie's figures against my add-back basis (mine 3.40, 3.85, 4.45 against 3.57, 3.81, 4.01: below in 2026 on Gothenburg and provisioning, in line in 2027, above in 2028 because DNB Carnegie slows revenue growth to 4%), and Nordea's against my cash basis (mine 2.60, 3.05, 3.65 against 2.47, 3.13, 3.75: close throughout). The first set's "5 to 10% below the FactSet mean" holds on the cash basis for 2027 (3.05 against 3.25) and not on the model's basis; I correct the first set on that point and ask the Consumer to quote the basis every time.

P-09

Net likely impact on the main financial line items over 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years

C50

Question asked After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement: the next four years are a slower, cleaner and more cash-generative version of the last four, with a trough in the middle. Revenue growth halves (12 to 15% a year over 2022 to 2025, with 27% compound over 2019 to 2024, against 15% in 2026 falling to 8.5 to 10% over 2027 to 2030); adjusted EBITA growth falls from 17 to 38% to 12 to 14% in 2027 to 2028 and 10 to 11% in 2029 to 2030; the adjusted EBITA margin rises at a similar pace (about 30 basis points a year, 9.5% to 10.7% over 2025 to 2030, against 8.1% to 9.5% over 2023 to 2025) but with a flat spot in H2 2026 to H1 2027; EPS on the model's basis compounds at about 16% a year over 2025 to 2030 (SEK 2.51 to 5.22), faster than EBITA because the interest share falls; reported net income rises from 31% of adjusted EBITA to 54%; free cash flow before acquisitions improves from 25 to 35% of EBITA in 2026 to 55 to 57% from 2028, against roughly 50 to 70% in 2022 to 2024 and 30% in 2025; reported leverage falls from 2.25x at the end of 2026 to 1.5x by 2030 with absolute net debt flat.

The table the Consumer should carry (SEK m unless stated; 2025 actual on the company's and brokers' basis; 2026 to 2030 my base case).
