What drives the difference from the past, in order of size. First, the acquired contribution falls from 12 to 13% of sales to 5 to 7% because the funding is cash flow after a 2.5x ceiling rather than a private balance sheet and a shareholder loan; this alone takes revenue growth from the mid-teens to 9 to 10% and EBITA growth from high-teens to 12 to 14%. Second, the currency tailwind of 2020 to 2022 and headwind of 2024 to 2025 (-3% a year) gives way to a small headwind in 2027 and nothing thereafter. Third, the margin levers change: provisioning releases (about 80 basis points cumulatively over 2023 to 2025 at the EBITA line on DNB Carnegie's figures) stop and partly reverse, while logistics consolidation (30 basis points) and pruning (10 to 15) arrive; the net annual margin gain is unchanged at about 30 basis points but is of higher quality from 2028. Fourth, the below-EBITA lines stop deteriorating: the tax rate falls from 33% to 24% (already in 2025), net financial items stop rising (they rose from SEK 179m to 407m over 2022 to 2024), and depreciation rises only with the estate. Fifth, cash: capital spending falls back from 3.5% of sales in 2025 to 2.5% in 2026 and 2% thereafter, earn-outs normalise, and inventory days fall; free cash flow before acquisitions roughly triples from 2026 to 2028. The one line that does not improve is return on capital employed on the reported basis (8 to 9% throughout), because goodwill compounds faster than EBITA.

The interplay the third set should test. The five drivers are not independent: the acquisition budget (driver one) is set by the cash flow (driver five), which is set by the margin and the working capital (driver three and the inventory path), which are in turn affected by the acquisitions (acquired companies dilute working capital and raise integration cost). The model therefore has one dominant input, free cash flow conversion, and the Consumer should run it at three levels: 45% of EBITA (deal budget falls to SEK 0.8bn, acquired growth to 5%, leverage flat at 2.3 to 2.4x, 2028 EBITA about SEK 2.3bn), 55% (my base), and 70% (deal budget SEK 1.5bn, acquired growth 9 to 10%, leverage 2.0x in 2027 and 1.6x in 2028, 2028 EBITA about SEK 2.55bn). The EBITA cone across those three cases is about plus or minus 6% in 2028, and the EPS cone about plus or minus 8%; the leverage cone is 1.6x to 2.4x. That is the width of the case, and it is set by one variable.

Where consensus and I differ, line by line, for the fourth set. Revenue: in line for 2026 and 2027, above by 2.5% for 2028 (consensus 22,798 is pulled down by one broker's 4% growth). EBITDA: 2 to 3% below for 2026 (12.3% against 12.6% margin, on Gothenburg and year-end prudence), 1% below for 2027 in level but above in growth, 6% above for 2028. D&A: SEK 30 to 60m a year above consensus. Net financial items: 5 to 10% above consensus, in line with DNB Carnegie's rising path for 2027 and below it for 2028. Tax: 24% against about 23.5%. EPS: not comparable with the median; comparable on growth (2026 above, 2027 below, 2028 above). Free cash flow: 20% below for 2027 on a like-for-like basis. Dividend: in line. Share count: in line.

P-10

The base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over 18 months to four years

C52

Question asked Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: my base case is a company that spends the next 18 months proving it can deleverage while still acquiring, and the following two and a half years compounding at a lower rate than its history and its framework but at a higher quality than its last two years. The operational drivers and the financial lines follow.

Operational drivers. Organic sales growth: 5% in H2 2026 (Q3 against a normal comparison, Q4 against an easy one), 4.5 to 5% in 2027 and 4.5% a year to 2030, made of 3 to 3.5% volume and mix and 1 to 2% nominal price, with the Nordics at 4 to 5%, West at 5 to 6% (the Dutch direct-to-patient platform compounding from 570,000 patients and its German extension) and Central at 4 to 5% net of pruning; nothing for preparedness orders; a downside of 3% if public budgets squeeze tenders further and an upside of 6% if a preparedness cycle returns. Acquired growth: 10.5% of sales in 2026 (already secured by the seven 2026 deals of about SEK 1.3bn of annual sales and the 2025 cohort annualising), 7% in 2027, 6% in 2028, 5% in 2029, 4.5% in 2030, from a deal budget of SEK 0.95bn of new acquisitions in 2027 rising to SEK 1.05bn by 2030 plus earn-outs, at 6 to 8x EBITA, 8 to 12 deals a year of SEK 50 to 350m of sales each, increasingly in Benelux, Germany, Poland and one new white-spot platform (France, Spain or Italy) in 2028 to 2029; the acquired EBITA contribution is 11% in 2026, 7% in 2027 and 6 to 7% thereafter against the 10 to 12% target. Currency: -0.5% in 2026, -1.5% in 2027 (krona strengthening toward EUR/SEK 10.40 to 10.60), -0.5% in 2028, nil after. Gross margin: 41.6%, 41.8%, 42.0%, 42.1%, 42.2% (company definition), all mix. Gothenburg: double running of SEK 20 to 25m in H2 2026 and SEK 15m in H1 2027; savings of SEK 25 to 30m in H2 2027 and SEK 55 to 60m a year from 2028; depreciation of SEK 15m a year from 2027. Provisioning: SEK 15m of prudence in Q4 2026, SEK 25m of higher run-rate in 2027 and thereafter. Items affecting comparability: SEK 60 to 70m a year (excluded from adjusted EBITA, inside reported EBIT). Inventory days: about 80 at the end of 2026, 76 at the end of 2027, 72 at the end of 2028, flat after; return on net working capital 65 to 70%. Employees: about 5,300 at the end of 2026 rising with deals; revenue per employee flat in 2026 to 2027 and up 2 to 3% a year from 2028. Capital spending: 1% of sales tangible plus SEK 180 to 220m of capitalised development and rights, plus the Gothenburg tail (SEK 150m in 2026, SEK 30m in 2027). Earn-outs paid: SEK 463m in 2026, 150m in 2027, 200m in 2028, 250m in 2029 and 2030. Dividend: 32 to 33% of reported net income. No buybacks, no equity issuance beyond incentive plans. Interest: 4.5 to 5% average cost on net debt plus lease interest and earn-out discount unwinding; no rate relief in the horizon. Tax 24%.

Financial lines by year (SEK m; EPS in SEK; the full table is in P-09 and the forecast inputs). 2026: revenue 19,355 (+15.3%), adjusted EBITDA 2,381 (12.3%), adjusted EBITA 1,896 (9.8%, +19%), net financial items -330, EPS 3.10 on the model's basis and 2.19 reported, dividend 0.70, free cash flow before acquisitions about 480 (model convention) or 660 (economic), acquisitions and earn-outs -1,350, year-end net debt about 4,700, leverage 2.25x. 2027: revenue 21,354 (+10.3%), EBITDA 2,669 (12.5%), EBITA 2,134 (10.0%, +12.5%), net financial -340, EPS 3.54 and 2.55, dividend 0.85, free cash flow about 1,070, acquisitions -1,100, net debt about 4,890, leverage 2.1x. 2028: revenue 23,383 (+9.5%), EBITDA 3,016 (12.9%), EBITA 2,431 (10.4%, +14%), net financial -330, EPS 4.14 and 3.12, dividend 1.00, free cash flow about 1,350, acquisitions -1,200, net debt about 4,940, leverage 1.85x. 2029: revenue 25,487 (+9.0%), EBITDA 3,339 (13.1%), EBITA 2,702 (10.6%, +11%), net financial -315, EPS 4.70 and 3.63, dividend 1.15, free cash flow about 1,540, acquisitions -1,250, leverage 1.65x. 2030: revenue 27,654 (+8.5%), EBITDA 3,650 (13.2%), EBITA 2,959 (10.7%, +9.5%), net financial -300, EPS 5.22 and 4.12, dividend 1.30, free cash flow about 1,670, acquisitions -1,300, leverage 1.5x.

The next 18 months by quarter (revenue growth; adjusted EBITA margin; EBITDA margin on the brokers' basis). Q3 2026: +15%; 9.6%; 12.1%. Q4 2026: +16%; 9.8%; 12.3%. Q1 2027: +12%; 9.7%; 12.2%. Q2 2027: +10%; 9.9%; 12.4%. Q3 2027: +10%; 10.0%; 12.5%. Q4 2027: +9%; 10.3%; 12.7%. The rolling twelve-month adjusted EBITA margin crosses 10.0% at Q2 or Q3 2027 rather than at Q4 2026 to Q1 2027 as the first set suggested, because of the Gothenburg and provisioning drag; that is a small but important change for the fourth set's reading of the "target met" narrative event.

The cone and the skew. Revenue over 18 months: narrow (plus or minus 2 points), symmetric. Adjusted EBITA 2027: SEK 2,020 to 2,250m around 2,134 (plus or minus 5%), with a slightly negative skew because the two identified drags (Gothenburg slip, provisioning) are more likely to be larger than smaller and the one identified upside (a faster deal pace) is capital-constrained. EPS 2027 on the model's basis: SEK 3.25 to 3.80 around 3.54 (plus or minus 8%), with a negative skew because interest and depreciation can only disappoint from here and a voluntary accounting clean-up would take a one-off bite. Free cash flow 2027: SEK 750 to 1,350m around 1,070 (plus or minus 30%), negative skew on inventory and earn-out timing. Leverage at the end of 2027: 1.9x to 2.4x around 2.1x. Over four years the cone widens mainly on the acquisition budget; the 2030 EPS range of SEK 4.4 to 6.0 is roughly symmetric because by then the deleveraging path either has or has not reopened the engine.

The three things that would move me off the base case, with direction. Up: statutory operating cash flow for FY2026 above SEK 1.3bn before earn-outs with inventory days below 78 at the year end, which would raise the 2027 deal budget by SEK 300 to 400m and 2028 EBITA by 3 to 4%; and a Nordic preparedness order cycle, worth 1 to 2 points of organic growth in the year it lands. Down: a second quarter of statutory operating cash flow near zero outside the earn-out effect, which would cut the deal budget to SEK 0.8bn and leverage headroom to nil; an auditor-driven provisioning and capitalisation catch-up in the FY2026 accounts (I put 10 to 20% on it), which would cut 2027 adjusted EBITA by SEK 100 to 150m; and a device-line loss to an OEM (10 to 15% a year), worth 1 to 2% of sales and 20 to 40 basis points of margin.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

The company reports two product segments and three business areas. Product segments (FY2025, FactSet): Medical Supplies, Devices and Equipment SEK 15,223m (91% of sales); Services SEK 1,564m (9%). No margin is reported by product segment. Business areas (H1 2026 sales share and adjusted EBITA margin): North (Sweden, Norway, Finland, the Baltics) about 31% of sales at 13.6%; West (Benelux, United Kingdom, Ireland, Denmark) about 51% at 9.6%; Central (Germany, Austria, Switzerland, France, Poland, Czech Republic, Slovakia) about 18% at 9.2%. Group adjusted EBITA margin 9.9% in H1 2026 and 9.5% in FY2025. North's share was 39% in 2023; every point of mix shift from North to West or Central costs about 4 basis points of group margin.

My business-area path. North: sales growth about 12% in 2026 (10% organic in Q2 2026 on the lapsed defence comparison, acquisitions Med Kjemi and Ambercell), 7 to 8% in 2027, 6 to 7% after; margin 13.2% in H2 2026 and 13.0% in H1 2027 (Gothenburg double running), 13.8% in H2 2027 and 14.3% in 2028 (SEK 50 to 60m of savings land here); share of group falling to about 29% by 2028. West: sales growth about 17% in 2026 (Van Heek, RMS, Murray Surgical, homecare), 11% in 2027, 10% after; margin 9.7% in 2026, 9.9% in 2027, 10.1% in 2028; share rising to about 53%. Central: sales growth about 15% in 2026 (GHC/MPF, Labo Clinic), 10% in 2027, 9% after; margin 9.4% in 2026, 9.7% in 2027, 10.0% in 2028 (product pruning); share about 18 to 19%. Group adjusted EBITA margin 9.8%, 10.0%, 10.4% for 2026 to 2028; the mix drag from North's falling share (about 10 basis points a year) is inside those figures.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

Revenue growth. Organic sales growth 5% in H2 2026 (Q3 2026 against a normal comparison of 5% in Q3 2025; Q4 2026 against -1% in Q4 2025), 4.5 to 5% in 2027, 4.5% a year to 2030: about 3 to 3.5% volume and mix plus 1 to 2% nominal price pass-through; no preparedness orders. Acquired growth 10.5% of sales in 2026 (the 2025 cohort annualising plus the seven 2026 acquisitions of about SEK 1.3bn of annual sales: Van Heek about 350, GHC/MPF about 300, Ambercell about 250, RMS 170, Labo Clinic about 100, Murray Surgical 73, Med Kjemi about 50), 7% in 2027, 6% in 2028, 5% in 2029, 4.5% in 2030, from a new-deal budget of about SEK 0.95bn in 2027 rising to SEK 1.05bn in 2030 at 6 to 8x EBITA, which is an acquired EBITA contribution of about 7% in 2027 and 6 to 7% thereafter against the company's 10 to 12% target. Currency -0.5% in 2026, -1.5% in 2027 (the house view of the krona strengthening toward EUR/SEK 10.40 to 10.60), -0.5% in 2028, nil after. Quarterly revenue (SEK m): Q3 2026 about 4,750; Q4 2026 about 5,420; Q1 2027 about 5,060; Q2 2027 about 5,160; Q3 2027 about 5,220; Q4 2027 about 5,910.

EBITDA margin. The EBITDA margin is on the brokers' adjusted basis (FY2025 actual 12.0%, SEK 2,015m), which is the company's adjusted EBITA (before items affecting comparability and before amortisation of acquired intangibles) plus depreciation and amortisation other than acquired intangibles (SEK 421m in 2025: lease depreciation about SEK 280m, tangible depreciation and capitalised-software amortisation the rest). Adjusted EBITA margin path: 9.5% (2025), 9.8% (2026), 10.0% (2027), 10.4% (2028), 10.6% (2029), 10.7% (2030). Drivers: acquired-company and organic mix plus pruning +25 to 30 basis points a year; Gothenburg -SEK 20 to 25m in H2 2026 and -SEK 15m in H1 2027, +SEK 25 to 30m in H2 2027, +SEK 55 to 60m a year from 2028; provisioning normalisation -SEK 15m in Q4 2026 and -SEK 25m a year from 2027; no purchasing synergies; no central cost cut; no price above inflation. Quarterly adjusted EBITA margin: Q3 2026 9.6% (about SEK 456m, +19%), Q4 2026 9.8% (about SEK 530m, +13%), Q1 2027 9.7%, Q2 2027 9.9%, Q3 2027 10.0%, Q4 2027 10.3%; the Part 3 EBITDA margins add about 2.4 to 2.5 points of other D&A to these. The rolling twelve-month adjusted EBITA margin crosses 10% at Q2 or Q3 2027.

Depreciation and amortisation. About 2.5% of sales throughout: lease depreciation rising from about SEK 280m (2025) toward SEK 320m (2028) and beyond with the estate; tangible depreciation including SEK 10 to 18m a year for the Gothenburg distribution centre from 2027; amortisation of capitalised development and distribution rights on a 10-year software life. Excludes amortisation of acquired intangibles (about SEK 341m in 2025, rising to about SEK 400m in 2026 and SEK 490m by 2030), which the model should treat as the bridge from adjusted to reported earnings.

Net financial items. The company's reported line (SEK 350m in 2025; SEK 407m in 2024), which includes lease interest of roughly SEK 50m, the unwinding of discount on contingent consideration and unhedged currency effects, on top of interest on net debt of about SEK 4.5 to 4.9bn at an average 4.5 to 5% with about 30% hedged by swaps. Flat because net debt stays flat in absolute terms while acquisitions continue; no rate relief assumed; a one-point rate move is SEK 35m. Because the lease interest sits in this line, the lease payments in Part 2 are the principal portion only.

Tax. 24% (22.6% in 2025 after the IPO removed non-deductible shareholder-loan interest; 33% in 2023 to 2024; Carnegie's normalised 25%; peers 22 to 24%; mix drifting toward higher-tax Germany and France).

Dividend. Above 30% of net profit; SEK 0.39 for FY2025 (30% of reported EPS); my figures are 32 to 33% of reported EPS of SEK 2.19, 2.55, 3.12, 3.63 and 4.12 for 2026 to 2030, declared for each fiscal year and paid in the following year (cash paid: about SEK 150m in 2026, 270m in 2027, 330m in 2028, 385m in 2029, 445m in 2030).

Share count. 383.7m diluted; incentive-plan dilution capped at about 0.81% across the 2025 and 2026 plans; no acquisition-funded issuance; no buybacks (private targets at 7x against the public share at about 16x EV/EBITA, and leverage at the ceiling).

Capital spending. Tangible capital spending at about 1% of sales (the 2022 to 2024 run-rate) plus the Gothenburg tail (about SEK 150m in 2026, SEK 30m in 2027) plus capitalised development and distribution rights of SEK 180 to 220m a year (SEK 94m of development in 2025 plus trademarks, patents and rights; DNB Carnegie assumes SEK 160 to 170m). 2025 actual SEK 590m on FactSet's combined line.

Working capital. FactSet's definition (receivables plus inventories less payables) as a share of trailing revenue: 14.8% at the end of 2025 (SEK 2,483m), 15.5% at June 2026 (SEK 2,788m); Asker's net working capital has risen in every quarter since Q1 2025 and does not release in the fourth quarter the way AddLife's does. Path: 15.3% at the end of 2026 (Gothenburg double inventory, acquired companies' weaker working capital; inventory days about 80 against 82 at June 2026), 14.8% in 2027 (inventory days 76), 14.3% in 2028 (72), 14.0% after. Note for the model: about SEK 150 to 200m a year of the working-capital increase arrives with acquired companies and economically sits inside the acquisition price; the model's working-capital line will deduct it as an operating outflow, so the model's free cash flow will be that much below the economic figure in acquisitive years.

Lease payments. Principal portion of IFRS 16 lease payments, approximately equal to lease depreciation: SEK 300m in 2026 rising to SEK 375m in 2030 with the estate; lease interest (about SEK 50m) is inside net financial items.

Acquisitions less disposals. Cash paid for acquisitions net of cash acquired plus contingent consideration paid plus put and call option settlements; no disposals. 2026: about SEK 890m of acquisitions (SEK 522m in H1 on FactSet's line; about SEK 370m in H2) plus SEK 463m of earn-outs paid in Q2 2026 (SEK 238m of which the company routes through operating cash flow and adds back in its "adjusted" operating cash flow; I treat all of it as acquisition cash). 2027: about SEK 950m of new deals plus about SEK 150m of earn-outs (management's guidance). 2028: SEK 1,000m plus SEK 200m. 2029: SEK 1,000m plus SEK 250m. 2030: SEK 1,050m plus SEK 250m.

Resulting leverage. Net debt on the company's definition (excluding leases) about SEK 4,700m at the end of 2026 (2.25x adjusted EBITDA after lease costs; the company's pro-forma ratio may print 2.2x), about SEK 4,890m at the end of 2027 (2.1x), about SEK 4,940m at the end of 2028 (1.85x), about SEK 4,930m at the end of 2029 (1.65x), about SEK 4,870m at the end of 2030 (1.5x). The bank covenant is 3.5x (prospectus, 17 March 2025); the company's target is below 2.5x.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

The company gives no numerical annual guidance. Its standing financial targets: adjusted EBITA growth above 15% a year (components: organic adjusted EBITA growth generally 5 to 8%, acquisitions contributing 10 to 12% of EBITA growth); adjusted EBITA margin above 10% over the medium term; net debt to adjusted EBITDA below 2.5x; return on net working capital above 50%; dividend above 30% of net profit. Operational guidance: Gothenburg savings of SEK 50 to 60m a year from H2 2027, with double-manning costs of low tens of millions of kronor in late 2026 and early 2027 and about SEK 150m of 2026 capital; about SEK 150m of contingent consideration payable in 2027; acquisitions funded from cash flow and debt; organic sales growth slightly above a 3 to 4% market.

My inputs against the targets. Adjusted EBITA growth: 19% in 2026 (above the target), 12.5% in 2027, 14% in 2028, 11% in 2029, 9.5% in 2030 (below the 15% target from 2027, because the acquired contribution is 7% and then 6 to 7% against the 10 to 12% target, the organic EBITA contribution is 6 to 7% within the 5 to 8% range, and 2027 carries about 2 to 3 points of Gothenburg and provisioning drag). Margin: 9.8% in 2026 and 10.0% in 2027 on the full year, so the "above 10%" target is reached in 2027 on the company's definition; on a cash definition (expensing capitalised development and recurring integration cost) the margin is about 8.6 to 8.8% in 2027 and does not reach 10% in the horizon. Leverage: inside the 2.5x ceiling throughout, with 0.25 turns of headroom at the end of 2026 and about 0.4 at the end of 2027. Return on net working capital: 65 to 70% against the 50% target. Dividend: 32 to 33% against the 30% floor. Gothenburg, earn-outs and the acquisition funding model are taken as guided. The difference between my model and the company's framework is almost entirely the acquisition budget, which I set from free cash flow after dividends plus the room created by EBITDA growth rather than from the pipeline.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

Company to brokers' EBITDA. The company reports adjusted EBITA (FY2025 SEK 1,594m, 9.5%), before items affecting comparability (SEK 204m in 2025: SEK 90m acquisition and integration, SEK 96m contingent-consideration revaluation, SEK 18m mainly IPO) and before amortisation of acquired intangibles (about SEK 341m). The brokers' EBITDA on FactSet (FY2025 actual SEK 2,015m, 12.0%) is adjusted EBITA plus other depreciation and amortisation (SEK 421m), so it is an IFRS 16 figure before lease costs and before items affecting comparability; FactSet's own operating EBITDA (SEK 2,092m) is on a different standardised basis and should not be used. The company's leverage ratio uses adjusted EBITDA after lease costs (about SEK 1,735m for 2025; about SEK 1,900m trailing at June 2026) and net debt excluding leases. My EBITDA margin inputs are on the brokers' basis.

EBITDA to EPS, and the four EPS bases. (1) The model's basis, which is what Parts 1 to 3 produce: adjusted EBITA less net financial items, taxed at the stated rate, over the share count; FY2025 on this basis is SEK 2.51 ((1,594 less 350) times 0.774, over 383m). (2) The FactSet brokers'-basis actual for 2025 is SEK 2.31 and the consensus is SEK 2.79, 3.29 and 3.77 (median) or 2.82, 3.25 and 3.68 (mean) for 2026 to 2028; it blends Nordea (2.47, 3.13, 3.75, a cash-earnings definition that expenses capitalised development and recurring integration cost), DNB Carnegie (3.57, 3.81, 4.01, reported net income plus acquired-intangible amortisation and items affecting comparability, without tax effect) and a restricted broker (3.05, 3.70, 4.29), and apparently further contributors FactSet does not show. The model's output will therefore print about 10% above the FactSet median in every year as a matter of definition, and the only like-for-like comparisons are growth rates (mine 23%, 14%, 17% against consensus 21%, 18%, 15% for 2026 to 2028). (3) DNB Carnegie's basis: my figures are about SEK 3.40, 3.85, 4.45 for 2026 to 2028 (reported net income of about SEK 840m, 980m, 1,200m plus acquired-intangible amortisation of about SEK 400m, 430m, 450m plus items affecting comparability of about SEK 60 to 70m, over the share count). (4) Reported basis: SEK 2.19, 2.55, 3.12, 3.63, 4.12 for 2026 to 2030 (FY2025 SEK 1.28 on FactSet's reported net income of SEK 492m over 383m shares; FactSet's basic EPS of 1.34 reflects a lower weighted share count before the March 2025 IPO). (5) Cash basis: the model's basis less after-tax capitalised development and recurring integration cost (about SEK 250m pre-tax), about SEK 2.60, 3.05, 3.65, 4.20, 4.70. The Consumer should quote the basis with every EPS figure.

Reported bridge for 2026 (SEK m): adjusted EBITA 1,896; items affecting comparability -60; amortisation of acquired intangibles -400; reported EBIT about 1,436; net financial items -330; pre-tax about 1,106; tax at 24% about -265; reported net income about 840.

Free cash flow. My definition: adjusted EBITDA less net financial items, less cash tax (24% of reported pre-tax profit), less the change in working capital, less capital spending including intangibles, less lease principal; before acquisitions, earn-outs and dividends. FactSet's consensus free cash flow (SEK 805m, 1,262m, 1,388m for 2026 to 2028) is operating cash flow less fixed-asset capital spending and deducts neither intangibles, nor leases, nor earn-outs; the company's "adjusted cash flow from operating activities" adds back contingent-consideration payments routed through operating activities (SEK 238m in Q2 2026) and should be reversed when quoted.

Your cross-check: your own earnings per share and free cash flow for each of the five fiscal years

Earnings per share 3.10 3.54 4.14 4.70 5.22

Earnings per share is on the model's basis (adjusted EBITA less net financial items, taxed at 24%, over the diluted share count of 384.1m, 384.9m, 385.7m, 386.3m and 386.8m); reported EPS on the same assumptions is SEK 2.19, 2.55, 3.12, 3.63 and 4.12. Free cash flow is before acquisitions, earn-outs and dividends, on the model's convention of deducting the full change in working capital (adjusted EBITDA 2,381, 2,669, 3,016, 3,339, 3,650; less net financial items; less cash tax of about 270, 310, 380, 443, 504; less working-capital change of about 478, 199, 184, 224, 304; less capital spending; less lease principal). Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Asker Healthcare Group AB (ASKER-SE, Nasdaq Stockholm), a decentralised European distributor of medical supplies, devices and equipment (not pharmaceuticals) to hospitals, primary care, elderly care and homecare, with more than 50 companies in 19 countries in three business areas, North (Sweden, Norway, Finland, the Baltics), West (Benelux, United Kingdom, Ireland, Denmark) and Central (Germany, Austria, Switzerland, France, Poland, Czech Republic, Slovakia), reported product segments Medical Supplies, Devices and Equipment, and Services, growing through organic sales and 10 to 20 bolt-on acquisitions a year

Request: T-D1

Peers: AddLife AB (ALIF.B-SE), the closest listed Nordic medical and laboratory distribution acquirer, which paused acquisitions at 3.5x net debt to EBITDA at the end of 2023, took its adjusted EBITA margin from 10.5% in 2023 to 12.1% in 2025 through self-help and resumed acquisitions at 2.2x; Uniphar PLC (UPR-IE), an Irish pharmaceutical and medtech distributor with a 2.5x net-debt covenant, a new Greenogue distribution centre with disclosed dual-running costs and a go-live deferred to February 2027; Lagercrantz Group AB (LAGR.B-SE), Lifco AB, Addtech AB and Indutrade AB, the Nordic serial-acquirer benchmarks for self-funded compounding, accounting conventions and the treatment of acquisition costs; Vimian Group AB (VIMIAN-SE), a decentralised healthcare acquirer whose Q2 2026 operating cash flow fell to EUR 3.8m on a working-capital build; Storskogen Group AB, the Nordic precedent for a debt-funded roll-up that halted acquisitions at 4.35x; Mediq (private, Netherlands), the direct competitor in Dutch insurer-contracted homecare supply; Oriola Corporation, whose warehouse-management programme slipped and raised costs in 2026; Cardinal Health, McKesson and Owens & Minor for the United States med-surg precedent on distribution-centre automation and on margins through volume and inflation cycles Operating conditions: a serial acquirer running at its own leverage ceiling (reported net debt to adjusted EBITDA of 2.37x at June 2026 against a target below 2.5x, with a 3.5x bank covenant disclosed in the March 2025 prospectus) while contingent consideration of SEK 645m to 1,050m sits outside the reported ratio; a logistics consolidation with double-running costs in late 2026 and early 2027 before savings (management guides SEK 50m to 60m a year from H2 2027 for the Gothenburg distribution centre); a public earnings-quality challenge (DNB Carnegie, 10 September 2026, on capitalised development, provisioning and leverage definitions) under a new Chair elected in May 2026; a rising-rate regime with the Riksbank expected to hike in November 2026 and floating-rate debt with a disclosed sensitivity of SEK 35m per percentage point; rising inventory days (59 to 65 in 2022, 75 in 2025, 82 in Q2 2026); price-led public procurement (55% of European processes mainly on price) with German hospital reimbursement capped for 2027 to 2029; the annualisation of a large 2025 to 2026 acquisition cohort (14 deals signed in 2025, seven to eight completed or announced by September 2026) Inputs: The change expected is a shift from maximum expansion to digestion: continued bolt-on acquisitions at a pace funded from operating cash flow while reported leverage falls, a margin lifted by mix, product pruning and logistics consolidation rather than by volume or price, and improved cash conversion as Gothenburg completes and earn-outs normalise. Test: (1) The record: adjusted EBITA growth of 38% compound over 2019 to 2024, 17% in 2025 and 23% in H1 2026 with 5% organic sales growth; operating cash flow of about 101% of adjusted EBITA over 2022 to 2024 (Carnegie, 6 May 2025) falling to about 83% in 2025 and SEK 92m statutory in Q2 2026 after SEK 238m of earn-outs routed through operating activities; an 88% tender re-win rate and a 69% total tender win rate in Sweden, Norway and Finland in 2024 (prospectus, 17 March 2025); Central's adjusted EBITA margin raised from 7.8% to 9.2% in a year through product pruning. (2) The strengths claimed: system sales at 79% of Swedish and Norwegian sales, a Dutch direct-to-patient platform of 570,000 patients, acquisition pricing at 6 to 8x EBITA with H1 2026 acquisitions consolidated at a 17% EBITA margin, return on net working capital of 69%, and a decentralised model with Centres of Excellence in purchasing, logistics, IT and own brands. (3) The strengths that would have to be acquired: deleveraging while acquiring (has any Nordic distributor or compounder reduced leverage from about 2.4x to below 2.0x while keeping acquisitions at 7% or more of EBITA a year, and over how long?); restoring working-capital efficiency after rapid acquisition (how long did AddLife, Lifco and Indutrade take to bring inventory days back after acquisition waves?); delivering a distribution-centre consolidation on time (how often did peers' automated warehouse projects slip, by how long, and what did the slips cost?); a move to more conservative accounting without a one-off earnings shock (which Nordic compounders rebuilt provisions or changed capitalisation policy after a public critique, how did they phase it, and what happened to the share price and estimates?); and founder retention after three-year earn-outs expire (what is the record at AddLife, Lifco, Lagercrantz and Vimian of subsidiary organic growth after the founder leaves?). (4) Precedents of companies attempting the same transition from a similar starting point (leverage near the ceiling, organic growth of 4 to 5%, margin near 10%, acquisitions supplying more than half of growth): AddLife 2023 to 2026, Storskogen 2022 to 2026, Uniphar 2024 to 2026, Vimian 2023 to 2026, and any United States med-surg distributor after a large acquisition cohort; how many succeeded, how long it took, and what distinguished success from failure. Focus: Whether a decentralised medical-supplies distributor at its leverage ceiling can lower reported leverage toward the cohort's comfortable range while continuing to acquire at a meaningful pace and completing a logistics consolidation, on the strengths Asker has demonstrably shown (organic growth, tender retention, deal pricing, working-capital returns), or whether the transition requires capabilities (cash discipline at scale, conservative reporting, integration of working capital) that the company has not yet shown and peers took two or more years to build.

Request: T-D2

Peers: AddLife AB (ALIF.B-SE), the direct precedent for the dispersion of outcomes at a Nordic medical distribution acquirer through a deleveraging cycle (2023 to 2026) and for the margin effect of losing a device line to its OEM (UK endoscopy, SEK 120m of organic sales lost in the first nine months of 2026); Uniphar PLC (UPR-IE), for a distribution-centre transition and for leverage moving from 1.6x to 2.4x in one half-year on a working-capital unwind (H1 2026); Vimian Group AB (VIMIAN-SE), for cash-conversion dispersion at a decentralised healthcare acquirer; Lagercrantz Group AB (LAGR.B-SE), Lifco AB, Addtech AB and Indutrade AB, for how narrow the outcome range is at self-funded compounders and what widened it (Lagercrantz's first EBITA miss in Q1 2026/27; Lifco's January 2026 short report); Storskogen Group AB, for the widest Nordic roll-up outcome; Oriola Corporation and Mediq for distribution cost-structure and working-capital dispersion; Cardinal Health and Owens & Minor for United States med-surg margin dispersion through inflation and destocking (2021 to 2023) Operating conditions: a defensive, non-cyclical end market (European medtech growing 3 to 4% a year) in which revenue dispersion is low but margin and cash dispersion are high; a leverage ceiling (target below 2.5x; 2.37x at June 2026) that makes the acquisition budget a function of cash conversion; a dual-running logistics project (Gothenburg, late 2026 to H1 2027) with savings guided from H2 2027; a disputed earnings base (DNB Carnegie's 10 September 2026 reconstruction of 2025 adjusted EBITA at about SEK 1.2bn against the reported SEK 1,594m) with the FY2026 annual report due in early 2027 under a new Chair; a thin sell-side (three brokers visible on FactSet, 2026 adjusted EPS from SEK 2.47 to 3.57 on differing definitions); a device mix of about 70% of medtech activity exposed to OEM direct-sales decisions; German reimbursement caps for 2027 to 2029 and Dutch insurer contracts renewed annually; a krona that weakened from September 2026 after strengthening through H1 2026 Inputs: The differences between the next 18 months to four years and the last four to six years to test: revenue growth slowing from 12 to 15% a year (2022 to 2025) as the acquired contribution falls from 12 to 13% of sales (H1 2026) toward whatever a cash-funded deal budget allows; the adjusted EBITA margin continuing to rise from 9.5% (2025) but with its source shifting from acquired mix and provisioning (the obsolescence reserve fell from 14.9% of gross inventory in 2021 to 6.5% in 2025) to logistics consolidation and pruning; net financial items staying high (SEK 350m in 2025) rather than falling; depreciation rising with Gothenburg (DNB Carnegie: SEK 10m to 18m a year from 2027) and lease growth against a flat FactSet consensus of about SEK 500m; free cash flow recovering from the 2025 to 2026 trough (capital spending of SEK 590m in 2025; earn-outs of SEK 463m paid in Q2 2026; about SEK 150m guided for 2027) as inventory days fall from 82. Test: (1) How concentrated are the drivers: how much of the forecast change rests on cash conversion alone (which sets the deal budget, which sets more than half of EBITA growth), and how often have forecasts resting on one driver been met in the cohort? (2) The historical dispersion of outcomes at Nordic distribution acquirers and United States med-surg distributors over 18-month windows: the range of revenue growth, EBITA margin, EPS and free cash flow against consensus at the start of the window, and the events that widened it (leverage pauses, OEM line losses, warehouse slips, accounting changes, short reports, currency). (3) The specific skew events: how often did a distribution-centre consolidation slip by one or two quarters and what did it cost; how often did a public earnings-quality critique lead to a provision rebuild or policy change within 12 months and what was the earnings effect; how often did an OEM reclaim a device line above the SEK 20m to 30m local-sales threshold an expert formerly at Asker cited (4 April 2025); how often did a Nordic preparedness or defence order cycle add to medical-supplies demand (Asker booked about SEK 350m in 2024)? (4) The width of the EPS range created by definitional differences alone at Nordic compounders (reported, adjusted for acquired-intangible amortisation, cash-adjusted) and how the market resolved them. Focus: How much of the difference between Asker's next four years and its last four rests on the single variable of cash conversion, how wide the 18-month range of EBITA, EPS and free cash flow outcomes has been for comparable acquirers at a similar point in the leverage cycle, and whether the identified skew events (Gothenburg timing, an accounting clean-up, an OEM line loss, a preparedness order cycle) have historically been one-sided or symmetric.

Request: T-I1

Peers: AddLife AB (ALIF.B-SE), the closest comparable, which reported Q3 2026 on 21 October 2026 (adjusted EBITA margin 11.5%, EPS SEK 1.05 against consensus SEK 0.86, DNB Carnegie downgrade to Hold on 22 October 2026 after a strong run) and reports Q4 2026 on 3 February 2027; Uniphar PLC (UPR-IE), which reported H1 2026 on 8 September 2026 (leverage 2.4x; Greenogue go-live deferred to February 2027) and guides on dual-running costs; Vimian Group AB (VIMIAN-SE), which reported Q3 2026 on 21 October 2026 with working capital at 25% of sales; Lagercrantz Group AB (LAGR.B-SE), Lifco AB, Addtech AB and Indutrade AB for the cohort's guidance and revision behaviour; Oriola Corporation (warehouse-programme slip, DNB Carnegie target cut, October 2026); Cardinal Health and McKesson for the sector's recent guidance pattern Operating conditions: no numerical annual guidance, only standing targets (adjusted EBITA growth above 15% a year; adjusted EBITA margin above 10% over the medium term;
